Part 1
Fundamentals
What you need to write models and run them: the language, analog and digital behavior, what a simulation does with them, projects, the library, errors and the command line.

Chapter 1

Welcome

Goal

By the end of this chapter you have created a project, run an RC filter from the command line, read its results, and made a model tell you what it is doing. You can read a small MSL file from top to bottom.

MSL, the Piperine mixed-signal language, is a language for describing circuits that a simulator runs: analog, digital, and both at once. It has two layers: a small, strict language of values and functions for what a model computes, and a handful of constructs for the hardware itself. The difference from a programming language is the second layer: a model describes hardware that exists all the time, not a program that runs once.

This book is for people who want to write models and read other people's models. It teaches the language through complete examples and stands on its own. Each chapter of the language builds one or two small circuits, explains every new construct the first time it appears, shows how to run the example with the piperine command, lists the mistakes you are most likely to make with the error code you will see, and ends with something to try.

The book has two parts. Part 1, Fundamentals, is what you need to write models and run them: the rules of the language, analog and digital behavior and how they meet, what a simulation does with your model, projects and packages, the library, errors, and the command line. Part 2, In depth, opens the machine: the object model tools read, the device interface, the solver with every option of a run, the compiler, and how the crates fit together. Most designs are written without Part 2; it is there for the day you want to know why, or want to build on Piperine itself.

A first look: one resistor

Here is a resistor. It is the smallest useful model, and it already shows the two halves every model has.

/// Ideal resistor.
module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    /// Resistance, in ohms.
    r: f64 where r > 0.0;
}

analog for Resistor {
    i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r;
}

Read it line by line:

  1. module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) declares a hardware module with two ports. The type of a port says what flows through it. Electrical is a circuit node: it has a voltage, and currents into it sum to zero.
  2. r: f64 where r > 0.0; is a param. It has a name and a type, and here no default, so whoever places a Resistor must give r. The where clause is a check: a zero or negative resistance is refused before anything is simulated.
  3. analog for Resistor { … } is the behavior. The module says what the thing is (ports, params, structure); a separate block, in the same file, says what it does over time.
  4. i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; is Ohm's law. v(p, n) reads the voltage from p to n. i(p, n) +> x adds x to the current that flows from p to n. The arrow +> is called a contribution.

The /// lines are documentation. They stay attached to the module and the param, and tools show them.

Run it: your first project

A resistor alone does nothing, and reading is no substitute for running. One command creates a project that runs: an RC low-pass filter, 1 kΩ and 1 nF, with a bench that measures its step response.

piperine new rc
cd rc
created rc
rc/
├── Piperine.toml          the manifest: the package and its runs
├── .gitignore
├── src/
│   └── lib.msl            the library: the parts
└── benches/
    └── step.msl           a bench: the circuit, its source and a monitor

The library holds the parts. The resistor you just read is the first of three. This is src/lib.msl:

/// Ideal resistor.
pub module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    r: f64 where r > 0.0;
}

analog for Resistor {
    i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r;
}

/// Ideal capacitor.
pub module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    c: f64 where c > 0.0;
}

analog for Capacitor {
    i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n));
}

/// A constant voltage from `p` to `n`.
pub module Vsource(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    dc: f64 = 1.0;
}

analog for Vsource {
    v(p, n) +> dc;
}

The bench is the circuit you actually simulate: a module with no ports that places the parts, wires them together and watches them. A bench is written in MSL, like everything else. This is benches/step.msl:

use rc::{Capacitor, Resistor, Vsource};

/// Writes a record when `a` rises through `level`.
module Monitor(a: Electrical) {
    level: f64 = 0.5;
}

digital for Monitor {
    on cross(v(a) - level) {
        emit!("trip", v = v(a));
        require("crossed_upward", v(a) >= level);
    }
}

/// A 1 V source into R and C. `vout` starts at 0 V, so a transient is the step response.
module Step() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal vout: Electrical = 0.0;
    let v1 = Vsource(vin, gnd) { dc: 1.0 };
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c: 1n };
    let mon = Monitor(vout);
}

The manifest, Piperine.toml, names the package and describes its runs. A run is a bench plus an analysis:

[package]
name = "rc"
version = "0.1.0"

[run.op]
bench = "benches/step.msl"
analysis = "op"

[run.step]
bench = "benches/step.msl"
analysis = "transient"
stop = 5e-6
print_step = 1e-6

Now run the first one. op is the operating point: the circuit at rest, with every capacitor open.

piperine run op
run op: op, top Step, backend rust

signal     value
Step.vin   1.000000e0
Step.vout  1.000000e0

The first line says what ran: the run's name, the analysis, the top module and the backend that executed it. Then comes the result: one row per signal of the bench. At rest no current flows, so the output sits at the source's 1 V.

The second run is a transient of 5 µs, printed every microsecond:

piperine run step
run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

That is a capacitor charging through a resistor: 63 % after one time constant r · c = 1 µs, 86 % after two. The line that starts with @ is the bench talking: its monitor saw the output pass 0.5 V at 693.1 ns. The theory puts it at 1 µs · ln 2 = 693.147 ns; the solver locates the crossing to within its time tolerance, here 0.04 ns early. The numbers in the table are the solver's too, so they agree with 1 − e−t/τ to about four digits, not to the last one. You will learn to write such lines in a moment.

The first time a design runs, the command compiles it, and says so on a separate line that is not part of the result. Later runs of an unchanged design start at once. Chapter 15 is the full reference of the command; from here on, every chapter tells you how to run its example.

Reading the project

Go back over the three files. Six things are new.

The capacitor. ddt(x) is the time derivative of x. The capacitor's current is the derivative of its charge, c * v(p, n). You write the physics; the simulator differentiates and integrates.

The source. v(p, n) +> dc; contributes to a voltage instead of a current, which makes the branch a voltage source.

SI suffixes. 1k is 103 and 1n is 10−9. A number with an SI suffix is always an f64. A plain 1 is an integer and never turns into an f64 by itself, which is why the defaults read 1.0. (The manifest is TOML, not MSL, so there you write 5e-6.)

The bench is a netlist. Step has no ports and no behavior of its own. signal vin: Electrical; declares a node. let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k }; places a resistor: the call connects the ports in order, and the braces bind params by name. gnd is the reference node, available everywhere. Every signal and instance gets a path from the top module down, which is what the output shows: Step.vout, Step.mon.

The initial value. signal vout: Electrical = 0.0; makes a transient start with the output at 0 V, a discharged capacitor. An operating point holds nothing, which is why op printed 1 V for the same node.

Two crates. The library under src/ is the package's crate. A bench is a small crate of its own that depends on it, so it imports the parts by the package's name: use rc::{…}, and it sees only what the library marks pub. Stimulus and monitors stay out of the library that other people's designs will use.

Seeing what your model does: emit!

The monitor is seven lines, and it is the most important idea of this chapter:

module Monitor(a: Electrical) { level: f64 = 0.5; }
digital for Monitor {
    on cross(v(a) - level) {
        emit!("trip", v = v(a));
        require("crossed_upward", v(a) >= level);
    }
}

emit! is MSL's print. It is the one output statement of the language, and you will use it in every chapter of this book as your window into a model. Read the block from the outside in:

  • digital for Monitor { … } is the second kind of behavior block. It holds reactions: on event { body } runs the body each time the event fires.
  • cross(e) is an event that fires when the expression e goes from negative to positive. The solver finds the time of the crossing for you; it is not rounded to a time step.
  • "trip" is the name of a stream: an ordered list of records. It must be a string literal.
  • v = v(a) is a field: a name, =, any value. An emit! takes as many as you like. There is no format string. Each field keeps its type.
  • require(name, pass) is a check. It is a library function that itself calls emit!, on the stream checks. A check that passes is not printed unless you ask for it, which is why the run above shows only the trip record.

Each record is printed as one line, the moment it is written:

run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

The line has four parts: @ and the time, the path of the instance that wrote the record, the stream name, and the fields. You wrote only the last two; where and when come for free. Two monitors on two nodes never mix their records, because each has its own path.

A design full of checks would bury its own prints under passing checks: a monitor that checks every clock edge writes one record per edge. So the terminal shows a checks record only when it fails. To see the passing ones too, add --all-checks:

piperine run step --all-checks
run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon checks kind="require" name="crossed_upward" pass=true
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

Both records belong to the same instant, and they come in the order the solver hands them over, which here puts the check first. The filter is for your eyes only: a records file (chapter 15) keeps every record, and piperine test counts every check, passing or not.

When a design prints more than you want to read, keep one stream:

piperine run step --stream trip

Three rules make emit! a debugging tool you can trust:

  1. It never changes the simulation. No construct of the language can read a stream. Adding or removing an emit! gives the same waveforms.
  2. A reaction's record is real. A solver often tries a time step, rejects it and tries a shorter one. A record written by a reaction during a step that is later rejected does not exist: what you see in the stream happened.
  3. It goes where behavior goes. emit! is allowed in a reaction and in an analog block, and in a function called from one of them. It is not allowed in a module body.

Chapter 5 gives every rule of emit!, and chapter 8 shows what it does inside analog, where the second rule is different on purpose.

A second bench: a source that steps

The first bench got its step for free, from an initial value. A real stimulus changes during the run. Add a second bench to the project, with a source that switches on at 1 µs:

// benches/delayed.msl
use rc::{Capacitor, Resistor};

/// A voltage step on `p`, referred to ground: 0 V until `t0`, then `v1`.
module StepSource(p: Electrical) {
    v1: f64 = 1.0;
    t0: f64 = 1u where t0 >= 0.0;
    signal fired: Bit = false;
}
digital for StepSource { on at(t0) { fired <- true; } }
analog for StepSource { v(p) +> if fired { v1 } else { 0.0 }; }

module Delayed() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal vout: Electrical;
    let src = StepSource(vin) { v1: 1.0, t0: 1u };
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c: 1n };
}
digital for Delayed {
    on cross(v(vout) - (1.0 - 1.0 / E)) {
        emit!("tau", t = now(), vout = v(vout));
    }
}

StepSource is a mixed-signal module, and a tiny one. It has a piece of digital state, signal fired: Bit = false: a one-bit register that starts low. Its reaction, on at(t0) { fired <- true; }, means "when time reaches t0, set fired". The arrow <- is a commit. Its analog block reads that bit directly: v(p) +> … with one node means "from p to ground", and fired is a bool, so the if takes one branch or the other.

The bench has a reaction of its own this time, on a crossing again, and E is the constant e: the record is written when vout rises through 63.2 % of the step. A bench outside the manifest runs by its file name, with flags in place of the run table's keys:

piperine run benches/delayed.msl --analysis transient --stop 5e-6 --print-step 1e-6
E0150

  × an `analog` block reads the discrete carrier `fired` other than through `known`, `known_or`, `is_known` or `high`: it has no unknowns (§3.3.5)
    ╭─[delayed.msl:11:36]
 10 │ digital for StepSource { on at(t0) { fired <- true; } }
 11 │ analog for StepSource { v(p) +> if fired { v1 } else { 0.0 }; }
    ·                                    ──┬──
    ·                                      ╰── a discrete carrier read in `analog`
 12 │ 
    ╰────
  help: read it through `known()`, `known_or(d)`, or `high()` for a `Bit`

error: typeck failed with 1 diagnostic(s)

The listing above is what the command prints today: it refuses the file, because the front end still refuses a plain read of fired in analog (error E0150), which the language now allows. When the read is accepted, the run gives the table of the benches before it. One time constant after the step: vout is 1 − 1/e to the digit, at 0.07 ns before 2 µs. To keep the run, give it a table in Piperine.toml; every flag has a key of the same name:

[run.delayed]
bench = "benches/delayed.msl"
analysis = "transient"
stop = 5e-6
print_step = 1e-6

Three ways to write

You have now seen two of the three arrows. A model changes the world in exactly three ways, and each has its own operator. Learn these three and you can read any MSL file.

=
definition

y = a & b; drives a digital carrier from an expression, recomputed whenever its inputs change. Written in a module body.

+>
contribution

i(p, n) +> x; adds x to the current (or voltage) of a branch, at every Newton iteration. Written in analog.

<-
commit

q <- d; schedules a new value for a digital carrier at the next microstep. Written in a reaction, on ev { … }.

The third one, the definition, is how you write logic with no state. Here is a digital inverter with a propagation delay. It has no behavior block at all: its one line drives the output from the input, tpd seconds later.

module Inv(in a: Bit, out y: Bit) {
    tpd: f64 = 10p where tpd > 0.0;
    y = !a after tpd;
}

Bit is a logic signal. Digital ports have a direction, in or out; electrical ports have none, because current flows both ways.

Everything else writes nothing. let x = expr; only gives an expression a name. Nets are never "joined" by a statement: you pass the same carrier to two ports, as vout is passed to r1 and c1 above. Parameters have no assignment at all: models read them, and the host sets them.

What you get

One language covers the transistor-level analog model, the RTL-style digital block and the glue between them. A design is fixed at elaboration and then simulated by Piperine's native solver: DC, transient, AC and noise all come from the same source text. Every outcome is a function of the source and of what the host does. There are no races.

A bench, with its sources, its stimulus and its monitors, is written in MSL, as you just did. What stays outside the language is the host: the program that picks the top module, sets parameters, runs analyses and reads the results, streams included. The piperine command is one host. Sweeps over many runs, Monte Carlo and optimization live in a host too, as a Rust program built on the same library.

Status

The specification is complete. The front end that parses MSL and builds the object model (chapter 17) exists and is growing, and it parses and resolves the whole standard library and every complete file of this book. The solver and its device ABI are built (chapters 18 and 19). A first set of analog circuits (resistors, capacitors, sources, a diode with junction limiting) runs on it end to end from source, through an interpreter or as compiled Rust, and is compared with ngspice. Digital and mixed-signal models run from source as compiled Rust: a full-size digitally controlled buck converter starts and regulates with its loop closed (chapter 20). So a file with a digital block, like Monitor above, runs today. The piperine command runs all of it, and every listing of its output in this book is what it prints, checked against the program itself. Parts of the language beyond that are still refused by name with P0001: in analog, noise, idt and delay; elsewhere, generic modules, params of an integer type and resolved natures (a nature that implements Resolve). When a chapter's example needs one of them, its "Run it" box says so and shows the refusal. This book describes the language as specified.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0319let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout);r has no default, so it is required. Bind it: Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k }.
E0312let c1 = Capacitor(vout) { c: 1n };Every port must be connected. Ground is a node like any other: Capacitor(vout, gnd).
E0317Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 0.0 }The param's where r > 0.0 is false. The check did its job; give a positive value.
E0901println!("vout = {}", v(vout));There is no println!. Write a record: emit!("dbg", vout = v(vout));.
E0404fired <- true; in the module bodyA commit belongs in a reaction. Move it into digital for StepSource { on at(t0) { … } }.

Try it

A param can be set for one run without touching the source: piperine run step --param c1.c=2.2e-9. Before you run it, say at what time the trip record will appear. (The time constant is now 2.2 µs, so 2.2 µs · ln 2 = 1.525 µs.) Then add a second Monitor to the bench with level: 0.9 and find its record in the output.

Chapter 2

Language basics

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can read and write the expressions and functions of an MSL source: you know how its text is split into words and numbers, what type an expression has, what arithmetic does at its limits, how blocks, if and match yield values, and how constants and functions are declared.

MSL has two layers. The value language computes: numbers, flags, your own types, functions. Nothing in it has time. The hardware constructs describe what exists over time: modules, the signals between them, and the behavior that drives those signals.

The value language takes three chapters, and each builds on the one before it. This chapter has the ground rules: text, numbers, types, operators, statements and functions. Chapter 3 adds the types you build from those, and the patterns that take them apart. Chapter 4 adds traits and generics, which let one piece of code serve many types and sizes. Chapter 5 then shows where a value meets hardware, and the rest of the book takes the hardware constructs one at a time.

How to read the examples

Each rule comes with a file you can run. The files share one scaffold, which chapter 1 introduced, and three facts about it are enough for now:

  • module Name() {} declares a piece of hardware with no ports, so that the file has something to run.
  • digital for Name { on at(1u) { … } } holds statements that run once, one microsecond into a transient.
  • emit!("name", field = value, …) writes one record with the fields you name. The command prints it as a line that starts with @.

Read the statements between the braces of on at(1u). Chapter 5 explains the scaffold itself.

Source text

A source file is UTF-8 text. Spaces, tabs and line ends separate words and mean nothing else: no construct depends on indentation or on where a line ends. Statements end with ;, and blocks are delimited with { }.

  • Comments. // starts a comment that runs to the end of the line. /* … */ is a comment that may span lines, and one may sit inside another.
  • Doc comments. A comment that starts with /// documents the item that follows it: a module, a port, a param, a signal, a type, a function. It is kept with that item, and tools show it. //! at the top of a file documents the file.
  • Names. A name starts with a letter or _ and continues with letters, digits and _. Names are case-sensitive: vout and Vout are two names. By convention values, functions, signals and instances are written lower_case, types and modules UpperCase, and constants ALL_CAPS.
  • Keywords. The words of the table are reserved and cannot be names.
GroupKeywords
hardwaremodule nature signal on in out flip after extern
values and controllet mut const fn if else match for while loop break continue return as true false
typesstruct enum trait impl where Self self
names and filesmod use pub crate super

analog and digital are keywords only in front of for, where they open a behavior block; anywhere else they are ordinary names. A few more words are reserved although no construct uses them, among them type, static, async and unsafe. Seven of the hardware keywords are common words in a circuit: module, nature, signal, on, out, flip and after. When a design needs one of them as a name, for example a port called out, write it as r#out.

Literals

KindWrittenNotes
integer42 1_000 0xFF 0o17 0b1010Decimal, hexadecimal, octal or binary. _ may separate digits.
typed integer12u4 -1i8 250u8A suffix uN or iN fixes the type: unsigned or signed, N bits.
float2.5 1.5e-3 1e6A point with digits on both sides, or an exponent.
scaled float4.7k 10n 1MAn SI suffix multiplies by a power of ten. The result is always a float.
booleantrue false
string"overrange"Text for names and messages. Strings are constants: there is no string arithmetic.
four-state logicLogicA value whose levels may be 0, 1, x or z, like a Verilog wire. Chapter 5 is about it.

The SI suffixes are the ones on a schematic, and they are case-sensitive: m is milli and M is mega.

SuffixTGMkmunpfa
Multiplier1e121e91e61e31e-31e-61e-91e-121e-151e-18

A suffix is a multiplier and carries no unit: 10n is the number 1e-8 whether it stands for farads or seconds. A letter that continues into a type is read as the type, so 1u8 is the integer 1 in eight bits and 1u is one millionth.

// A line comment runs to the end of the line.
/* A block comment ends at its closing mark, /* and may nest */ like this. */

/// A doc comment belongs to the item that follows it: tools show it with the item.
module Literals() {}

digital for Literals {
    on at(1u) {
        let count = 1_000;              // an integer; `_` separates digits and means nothing
        let mask = 0xFF;                // hexadecimal; 0o17 is octal, 0b1010 binary
        let code = 12u4;                // a suffix fixes the type: a 4-bit unsigned integer
        let gain = 2.5;                 // a float: digits on both sides of the point
        let tiny = 1.5e-3;              // with an exponent
        let r = 4.7k;                   // an SI suffix scales by a power of ten: 4700.0
        let c = 10n;                    // 1.0e-8
        let ready = true;               // a bool: true or false
        emit!("literals", count = count, mask = mask, code = code,
              gain = gain, tiny = tiny, r = r, c = c, tau = r * c, ready = ready);
    }
}

Run it

Save the file as literals.msl, anywhere, and run it by its name. The line that starts with @ is the record: the time, the instance, the name given to emit!, then each field.

piperine run literals.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run literals: transient, top Literals, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Literals literals count=1000 mask=255 code=12 gain=2.500000e0 tiny=1.500000e-3 r=4.700000e3 c=1.000000e-8 tau=4.700000e-5 ready=true

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

Types

Every expression has exactly one type, and the type is fixed before anything runs. These are the types the language starts from:

KindTypesValues
booleanbooltrue and false.
unsigned integeru1 … u128uN holds 0 to 2N − 1. Any width is a type: a 3-bit duty word is a u3, a 10-bit converter code a u10.
signed integeri1 … i128iN holds −2N−1 to 2N−1 − 1, in two's complement.
floatf64, and f16, f32, f128IEEE 754 numbers, with infinities and NaN. Analog code computes in f64.
stringstrA literal text. It is never carried by a signal.
unit()One value, (): the type of a block that computes nothing.

usize is another name for u64, on every machine: it is the type of a count or of a position in a table, and a result never depends on the computer that runs the model. Chapter 3 adds the types built from these: tuples, arrays, Option, and the structs and enums you declare.

Four rules decide the type of what you write, and one fact about the numbers is worth knowing before the first register: a number has no default value. In many languages u8::default() is 0, and a variable left unset starts there. In MSL u8::default() does not exist, and neither does one for bool, for the other integers or for the floats, because zero is a value like any other and a register with no reset value has no meaning a number could express. A register of a number is always given its first value, signal n: Bit<u8> = 0;, and chapter 5 shows the types that do have a default.

  1. A literal takes the type its place asks for. In let a: u8 = 250; the literal is a u8. With nothing to decide it, an integer literal is an i32 and a float literal an f64. A literal too large for its type is the error E0909, found before the run.
  2. A let may leave the type out. let r = 4.7k; has the type of its right side. Items are different: the parameters and the result of a function, a constant, a param and a port always state their types, so a reader of the item needs nothing else.
  3. Nothing converts by itself. An integer is not a float, and a u8 is not a u16. 1 + 1.0 is a type error, and so is passing a u8 where a u16 is asked for. The conversion is always written, with as.
  4. as converts between numeric types, and never fails. The table says what each conversion keeps.
FromToThe result
an integera narrower integerThe low bits: 300 as u8 is 44.
an integera wider integerThe same number. A signed source extends its sign, so -1i8 as u16 is 65535.
an integera floatThe nearest float.
a floatan integerThe fraction is dropped, then the number is brought into the range of the target: 300.7 as u8 is 255, and NaN becomes 0.
boolan integer0 for false, 1 for true.

Operators and arithmetic

The operators, from the one that binds tightest to the one that binds loosest. Parentheses override the order.

OperatorsOperandsMeaning
x.name x.f(a) f(a) x[i]A field, a method call, a call, an element.
-x !xa number; a bool or an integerNegation; logical not, or every bit inverted.
x as TnumbersConversion.
* / %two of one numeric typeProduct, quotient, remainder.
+ -two of one numeric typeSum, difference.
<< >>an integer, and a countShift left, shift right.
& ^ |two of one integer type, or two boolBitwise and, exclusive or, or; in that order of binding.
== != < <= > >=two of one typeComparison; the result is a bool. Comparisons do not chain: write lo < x && x < hi.
&&two boolAnd. The right side is evaluated only when the left is true.
||two boolOr. The right side is evaluated only when the left is false.

Both operands of an arithmetic operator have the same type, and the result has that type too. What the numbers do at their limits is fixed by the language, so a result is the same on every machine and in every build:

  • Integers behave like registers. A result that does not fit keeps its low N bits, upward and downward alike. Overflow is never an error and never differs between a debug and an optimized build.
  • Integer division drops the fraction. Dividing an integer by zero is a fault: the analysis ends there, naming the instance and the time.
  • Floats follow IEEE 754. A float divided by zero is an infinity, and an operation with no numeric answer, such as 0.0 / 0.0, is NaN. Neither is a fault by itself. A value that is not finite and arrives at the analog solver ends the solve with an error, because no circuit has an infinite voltage.
  • Operands are evaluated left to right.
module Arith() {}

digital for Arith {
    on at(1u) {
        let a: u8 = 250;
        let wrapped = a + 10;           // 260 does not fit eight bits: 260 - 256
        let below = 0u8 - 1;            // the same rule downward
        let quotient = 7 / 2;           // integer division drops the fraction
        let ratio = 7.0 / 2.0;          // float division keeps it
        let shifted = 1u8 << 3;         // 1 * 2^3
        let narrow = 300 as u8;         // `as` to a narrower integer keeps the low bits
        let signed = -1i8 as u16;       // a wider target extends by the sign of the source
        let clamped = 300.7 as u8;      // float to integer: toward zero, then into range
        let half = a as f64 / 2.0;      // an integer joins float arithmetic through `as`
        let both = a > 100 && ratio < 4.0;
        let over_zero = 1.0 / 0.0;      // a float division by zero is an infinity
        emit!("arith", wrapped = wrapped, below = below, quotient = quotient, ratio = ratio,
              shifted = shifted, narrow = narrow, signed = signed, clamped = clamped,
              half = half, both = both, over_zero = over_zero);
    }
}

Run it

piperine run arith.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run arith: transient, top Arith, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Arith arith wrapped=4 below=255 quotient=3 ratio=3.500000e0 shifted=8 narrow=44 signed=65535 clamped=255 half=1.250000e2 both=true over_zero=inf

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

Every field is the value its comment predicts. The methods a number has, such as abs, min, sqrt, exp and checked_add, are a fixed list; chapter 13 has it.

Statements, blocks and control flow

A block is a sequence of statements between braces, run in order. Three rules cover most of what a body does.

  1. let names a value. let x = expr; evaluates the expression and gives the result a name, visible from that statement to the end of the block. The name stands for that value from then on. A second let of the same name makes a new variable that hides the first, which is how a value is refined in steps.
  2. A block is an expression. Its value is its last expression, written without a semicolon. So { let s = x * 10.0; s + 1.0 } is a number. The same holds for the body of a function: its last expression is the result.
  3. if and match are expressions too. Each branch is a block, the branches have one type, and the whole has the value of the branch that ran. An if used for its value needs its else. The condition of an if is a bool and nothing else: a number is not a condition.

match compares one value against a list of patterns, top to bottom, and runs the first arm that fits. In this chapter a pattern is a literal, several literals joined with |, or _, which fits anything. The arms must cover every value the type has. If they do not, the model is not accepted, so a case cannot be forgotten silently. Chapter 3 adds the patterns that take a value apart.

module Flow() {}

digital for Flow {
    on at(1u) {
        let level = 0.72;
        let level = level * 5.0;                    // a new `level` that hides the first
        let code = { let scaled = level * 10.0; scaled as u8 };   // a block is an expression
        let grade = if level > 3.0 { 2 } else { 1 };              // so is an `if`
        let step = match code {
            0 => 0.0,
            1 | 2 => 0.5,                           // either value
            _ => 1.0,                               // every other value
        };
        let inside = level > 0.8 && level < 2.0;
        emit!("flow", level = level, code = code, grade = grade, step = step, inside = inside);
    }
}

Run it

piperine run flow.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run flow: transient, top Flow, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Flow flow level=3.600000e0 code=36 grade=2 step=1.000000e0 inside=false

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

A local may be given a new value. An assignment, x = x + 1; or x += 1;, replaces the value of x with another of the same type. Every local can be assigned, and its let needs no extra word for it. Assigning one name never changes another, because each name holds its own value. For repetition the language has the loops for i in 0..n, while and loop with break and continue. Whether a loop's count must be known before the run depends on where the loop stands: in a function called from a reaction it need not be, and where a loop describes hardware or equations (chapters 7 and 8) it must. Only locals can be assigned this way. A parameter of a function is not a local: it is the caller's value, handed over, and the function yields a result instead of changing it. A signal is never assigned either: it is driven, and that is the subject of chapter 5.

Constants and functions

An item is a declaration, most often at the top level of a file. Items may be written in any order: a function may call one declared further down, and a constant may use a later constant. This chapter needs two kinds of item.

A constant, const NAME: T = expr;, names a value that is computed before the run. Its type is always written, and its value may use literals, operators, other constants and the functions of this chapter. A use of a constant is a copy of its value.

A function, fn name(a: T, b: U) -> R { … }, names a computation. Its header gives each parameter with its type and, after ->, the type of the result. Four rules describe it:

  1. The result is the last expression of the body, written without a semicolon, as in any block. return value; leaves the function early with that result. A function with no -> yields ().
  2. Arguments are passed in order, each of the type its parameter states. There is no second function of the same name with other parameters, and a parameter has no default value. Chapter 3 shows the one way to make a parameter optional.
  3. A function is computation and nothing more. It reads its arguments and yields a result. It keeps no state between calls, writes no signal and places no hardware, so the same arguments always give the same result.
  4. A function may call any function, itself included.
/// The supply every threshold is a fraction of.
const VDD: f64 = 3.3;
const HIGH: f64 = 0.7 * VDD;            // a constant may be computed from another
const LOW: f64 = 0.3 * VDD;

/// The two resistances `a` and `b` in parallel.
fn parallel(a: f64, b: f64) -> f64 { a * b / (a + b) }

/// The thermal voltage at `t` kelvin.
fn thermal_voltage(t: f64) -> f64 { K_B * t / Q }

/// The distance from `v` to the nearest threshold.
fn margin(v: f64) -> f64 {
    let nearest = if v >= HIGH { v - HIGH } else { LOW - v };
    nearest                             // the last expression is the result
}

/// True when `v` is a valid logic level.
fn is_valid(v: f64) -> bool { margin(v) >= 0.0 }

module Functions() {}

digital for Functions {
    on at(1u) {
        emit!("functions", high = HIGH, rp = parallel(1k, 3k), vt = thermal_voltage(300.0),
              margin = margin(2.8), valid = is_valid(2.8), between = is_valid(1.5));
    }
}

Run it

piperine run functions.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run functions: transient, top Functions, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Functions functions high=2.310000e0 rp=7.500000e2 vt=2.585200e-2 margin=4.900000e-1 valid=true between=false

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

The high threshold is 0.7 × 3.3 = 2.31, so 2.8 is a valid level with a margin of 0.49, and 1.5 lies between the thresholds. is_valid calls margin, which is declared above it; the other order would do as well.

K_B and Q come with the language. The prelude gives you the constants Q, K_B, EPS0, PI, E and inf. Chapter 13 lists them with their values, next to the methods a number has.

When a computation cannot continue

A computation that cannot continue is a fault. Integer division by zero is one; chapter 3 adds two, an index outside its array and unwrap() on a None. A fault in a constant or a param default is reported when the design is built. A fault during a run ends the analysis and names the instance and the time.

What the compiler runs today

The rules of this chapter are the language, and the compiler runs the function below: it updates a local in a loop. The type of the literal in let total = 0 is fixed by its later use: total += k with a u32 k makes total a u32.

/// The sum of the whole numbers below `n`.
fn sum_below(n: u32) -> u32 {
    let total = 0;
    for k in 0..n { total += k; }
    total
}

Status

Running today: bool, integers of up to 64 bits, f64, constants, functions, let and blocks, if, and match over literals. Everything the four files above use is in that list.

Parsed and checked, and not run yet: an item declared inside a block, % on floats, floats other than f64, and integers wider than 64 bits. A model that uses one of them is stopped before the run with the code P0001 and a message that names the form, so nothing is ever computed by a rule other than the ones above.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0180let x = 1 + 1.0;An integer and a float never mix by themselves. Write both as floats, 1.0 + 1.0, or convert: n as f64 + 1.0.
E0909let a: u8 = 300;The literal does not fit the type. Write a value the type holds, or a wider type.
E0905let c = 'a';There is no character type. Use a string, "a", or the number you mean.
E0903let mut x = 0;mut is a reserved word: a local needs no marker to be assigned. Write let x = 0;.
E0903let module = 2;A keyword is not a name. Choose another, or write r#module.

Try it

In arith.msl, change a + 10 to a + 100 and predict wrapped before you run. (94: 350 does not fit eight bits, and 350 − 256 is 94.) Then, in functions.msl, add a function series(a: f64, b: f64) -> f64 and emit series(1k, 3k) / parallel(1k, 3k). (The ratio is 4000 / 750, about 5.33.)

Chapter 3

Your own types

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can say that a value may be absent with Option, group values into tuples and arrays, declare enums and structs of your own, take any of them apart with a pattern, and give a type its own constants and functions with impl.

Chapter 2 computed with numbers and flags. A model soon needs more: a result that may be missing, two results at once, a table of coefficients, a state with a name, a reading with its validity. This chapter builds those types from the ones of chapter 2, one at a time, and then shows the two tools that work on all of them: patterns, which take a value apart, and impl, which gives a type its own functions.

A value that may be absent: Option

Some values do not always exist: a limit the user may leave unset, the time of a crossing that may never happen. MSL has no null and does not ask you to reserve a number such as −1 for "nothing". Absence is always an Option, wherever it appears: a parameter, a signal, a result. The type says it: an Option<T> is either Some(x), holding an x of type T, or None.

An Option<f64> is not an f64, so it cannot be added or compared by accident. You get to the value inside in one of three ways:

  • With a method. o.unwrap_or(d) is the value inside, or d when there is none. o.is_some() and o.is_none() are bool. o.unwrap() is the value inside and a fault on None: use it only where a None would be a defect of the model.
  • With match. The arm Some(v) => … runs when there is a value and names it v; the arm None => … runs otherwise. Both arms must be there.
  • With if let. if let Some(v) = o { … } else { … } runs its first block when there is a value, with v named inside it, and its else block otherwise.
/// The reading held to `limit`, or 0.0 when there is no reading.
fn held(reading: Option, limit: f64) -> f64 {
    match reading {
        Some(v) => v.min(limit),                    // binds the value inside to `v`
        None => 0.0,
    }
}

module Absent() {}

digital for Absent {
    on at(1u) {
        let slope = 1M;                             // a ramp of 1 V per microsecond
        let rising: Option = if slope > 0.0 { Some(1.5 / slope) } else { None };
        let never: Option = None;
        let late = if let Some(t) = rising { t > 1u } else { false };
        let (lo, hi) = (0.8, 2.0);                  // a tuple pattern names both parts
        emit!("absent", found = rising.is_some(), when = rising.unwrap_or(0.0), late = late,
              fallback = never.unwrap_or(-1.0), held = held(Some(5.0), 3.3), nothing = held(never, 3.3),
              inside = lo < 1.5 && 1.5 < hi);
    }
}

Run it

piperine run option.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run option: transient, top Absent, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Absent absent found=true when=1.500000e-6 late=true fallback=-1.000000e0 held=3.300000e0 nothing=0.000000e0 inside=true

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

The ramp reaches 1.5 V at 1.5 µs, which is later than 1 µs. never has no value, so unwrap_or(-1.0) yields the fallback and held takes its None arm.

Optional parameters

Option is also how a function gets a parameter the caller may leave out. Trailing parameters of type Option<T> may be left out, in which case the function sees None, or passed by name to skip the ones before them. Passing a plain T wraps it in Some, the one implicit conversion in the language:

/// Scales `x`; the gain is 2.0 unless given, and the result may be clamped.
fn scale(x: f64, k: Option, limit: Option) -> f64 {
    let y = x * k.unwrap_or(2.0);
    match limit { Some(l) => y.clamp(-l, l), None => y }
}
scale(v)                    // k and limit left out
scale(v, 3.0)               // k = Some(3.0)
scale(v, limit: 1.0)        // skips k by naming limit

The function decides what None means, usually with unwrap_or. The library uses this everywhere: idt(x) is an integrator, idt(x, 1.0) one with an initial condition, and idt(x, 1.0, reset: rst) one with a reset too.

Tuples

A tuple groups a fixed number of values, each with its own type. (1.5, true) is a value of the type (f64, bool). Its parts are read by position, pair.0 and pair.1, or named all at once by a tuple on the left of a let, as let (lo, hi) = (0.8, 2.0); did above. The common use is a function with two results:

/// The smaller and the larger of two values, in that order.
fn ordered(a: f64, b: f64) -> (f64, f64) {
    if a <= b { (a, b) } else { (b, a) }
}

/// The distance between two values, whichever is larger.
fn span(a: f64, b: f64) -> f64 {
    let (lo, hi) = ordered(a, b);
    hi - lo
}

Two tuple types are the same type when their parts are, in order. A tuple of one part is written (x,), and (), the unit of chapter 2, is the tuple of none. When the parts deserve names, declare a struct instead.

Arrays

An array holds a fixed number of values of one type. Its type is [T; N], and the length N is part of the type: [f64; 2] and [f64; 3] are different types, and no array grows or shrinks during a run.

/// The coefficients of a three-tap smoothing filter.
const TAPS: [f64; 3] = [0.25, 0.5, 0.25];

/// The filter's output for the last three samples, oldest first.
fn smoothed(x: [f64; 3]) -> f64 {
    let sum = 0.0;
    for k in 0..3 { sum += TAPS[k] * x[k]; }
    sum
}

/// The mean of a table of any length.
fn mean(xs: [f64; N]) -> f64 {
    let total: f64 = xs.iter().sum();
    total / xs.len() as f64
}
  • Writing one. [0.25, 0.5, 0.25] lists the elements, which have one type. [0.0; 16] repeats one value sixteen times.
  • Reading one. x[k] is the element at position k, counted from 0. A position outside the array is a fault. x.len() is the length, and for v in x { … } visits every element in order.
  • Any length. A function that should accept a table of any length gives the length a name of its own, <const N: usize>, and takes [f64; N]. It is called with the table itself, mean(TAPS), and N is read from the argument. Chapter 4 explains parameters written in angle brackets.
  • Passing one. An array is a value like a number: a function that receives one has its own copy, whatever its length.

The methods of arrays, such as iter, sum, map and contains, are a fixed list; chapter 13 has it.

Enums

An enum is a type with a fixed list of variants, each with a name. A value of the enum is exactly one of them. It is the right type for a state, a mode or a classification, where a number would let through values that mean nothing.

/// The supply every threshold is a fraction of.
const VDD: f64 = 3.3;

/// What a receiver makes of a voltage.
#[derive(PartialEq, PartialOrd, Repr)]
enum Level { Low, Between, High }

/// Classifies `v` against the two thresholds.
fn level_of(v: f64) -> Level {
    if v >= 0.7 * VDD { Level::High } else if v <= 0.3 * VDD { Level::Low } else { Level::Between }
}

module Levels() {}

digital for Levels {
    on at(1u) {
        let seen = level_of(2.8);
        let weight = match seen {
            Level::Low => 0,
            Level::Between => 1,
            Level::High => 2,
        };
        emit!("levels", seen = seen, high = seen == Level::High,
              above = seen > Level::Between, weight = weight);
    }
}

Run it

piperine run levels.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run levels: transient, top Levels, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Levels levels seen=High high=true above=true weight=2

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

The high threshold is 0.7 × 3.3 = 2.31, so 2.8 is High. The command prints an enum field as the name of its variant, so seen=High is High.

Three things in this file are new:

  • A variant is named through its enum, Level::High. A file that uses the variants often may write use Level::*; once and then name them bare.
  • The match has no _ arm. It lists the three variants, and that is every value a Level has. Add a fourth variant later and this match stops the build until it has an arm for it, so a new case cannot be forgotten silently.
  • The line above the enum is an attribute. #[derive(…)] asks the compiler to write standard behavior for the type that follows: PartialEq gives it == and !=, PartialOrd gives it < and > in the order of the declaration, and Repr gives it a layout in bits, which a signal needs to carry it. So Low < Between < High here. Each of these names is a trait; chapter 4 says what that means.

A variant may also hold values, and then the enum says which case it is and carries the data of that case. Option<T> is exactly such an enum, with the variants None and Some(T). Fields are positional, as in Circle(f64), or named, as in Rect { w: f64, h: f64 }, and a match names them as it takes the value apart:

/// The outline of a pad on a layout.
#[derive(PartialEq)]
enum Shape { Dot, Circle(f64), Rect { w: f64, h: f64 } }

/// The area a shape covers.
fn area(s: Shape) -> f64 {
    match s {
        Shape::Dot => 0.0,
        Shape::Circle(r) => PI * r * r,
        Shape::Rect { w, h } => w * h,
    }
}

When no variant has a field, each may be given the number that stands for it, enum Op { Add = 0b00, Sub = 0b01, Halt = 0b11 }, which fixes how a signal encodes the enum. Without one, the variants count from 0.

Structs

A struct is a type with named fields: several values that belong together and travel as one.

/// One sample of a converter: the voltage it saw and whether it was in range.
#[derive(PartialEq)]
struct Reading { volts: f64, valid: bool }

/// A voltage, kept apart from every other `f64` by its type.
struct Volts(f64);

/// The reading scaled by `k`, valid when the source was.
fn scaled(r: Reading, k: f64) -> Reading {
    let volts = r.volts * k;
    Reading { volts, valid: r.valid }
}
  • Building one. Reading { volts: 1.2, valid: true } names every field once, in any order. A local with the name of the field may stand alone: Reading { volts, valid: r.valid }.
  • Reading one. r.volts is the field. Fields are not assigned one at a time from outside: a function that changes a reading builds a new one, as scaled does.
  • Each declaration is a type of its own. Two structs with the same fields are still different types, so a Reading is never taken for another pair of a number and a flag.
  • Positional fields. struct Volts(f64); has one field without a name, built as Volts(1.2) and read as v.0. It makes a distinct type out of a plain number.

A struct whose fields are signals instead of values is a bundle of wires, which chapter 6 covers. This chapter's structs hold values only.

Patterns

You have used patterns three times already: in the arms of a match, after if let, and on the left of a let. A pattern describes a shape a value may have. It fits a value or it does not, and when it fits it gives names to the parts you want. This is the full list:

PatternFitsNames it introduces
_anythingnone
xanythingx, the value
0, true, 2.5, "idle"that valuenone
1 | 2either alternativenone
0..=9, 0..10, 10..a value in the range: through 9, below 10, from 10 upnone
n @ 0..=9what the pattern after @ fitsn, the whole value
Level::High, None, a constantthat variant, or the value of the constantnone
Some(v), Shape::Circle(r)that variant, when its fields fitwhat the fields introduce
Reading { volts, .. }a struct, field by field; .. stands for the fields not writtenwhat the fields introduce
(a, b)a tuple, part by partwhat the parts introduce
[first, .., last]an array, element by elementwhat the elements introduce

Patterns nest: Some((lo, hi)) fits an optional pair and names both parts. Where a pattern stands decides what it must cover:

  • In a match, the arms are tried top to bottom, the first that fits runs, and together they must cover every value of the type. An arm may add a condition, n if n > 4 => …, which is tried after the pattern fits.
  • In a let, the pattern must fit every value, because there is nothing else to run: let (lo, hi) = pair; and let Reading { volts, .. } = r; always fit. let Some(v) = o else { return 0.0; }; takes a pattern that may not fit, and its else block must leave the function.
  • In an if let, the pattern may or may not fit, and the else block is what runs when it does not.

Functions that belong to a type: impl

An impl block attaches constants and functions to a type you declared, so that what belongs to the type is found under its name.

/// A logic family.
#[derive(PartialEq, Repr)]
enum Family { Cmos, Ttl }

impl Family {
    /// The family a design uses unless it says otherwise.
    const DEFAULT: Family = Family::Cmos;

    /// The family a supply of `vdd` volts belongs to.
    fn of_supply(vdd: f64) -> Family {
        if vdd > 4.0 { Family::Ttl } else { Self::DEFAULT }     // `Self` is the type of the `impl`
    }

    /// The voltage at which a receiver of the family `f` switches, on a supply of `vdd` volts.
    fn threshold(f: Family, vdd: f64) -> f64 {
        match f {
            Family::Cmos => 0.5 * vdd,
            Family::Ttl => 1.4,
        }
    }
}

module Families() {}

digital for Families {
    on at(1u) {
        let family = Family::of_supply(5.0);
        emit!("family", ttl = family == Family::Ttl, switch = Family::threshold(family, 5.0),
              default_switch = Family::threshold(Family::DEFAULT, 3.3));
    }
}

Run it

piperine run family.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run family: transient, top Families, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Families family ttl=true switch=1.400000e0 default_switch=1.650000e0

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

Family::DEFAULT is an associated constant and Family::of_supply an associated function: each is named through the type, with ::, the way a variant is. Inside the block, Self is another name for the type.

A function of an impl whose first parameter is the bare word self is a method. self is the value the method is called on, and the call is written after that value, with a dot:

impl Family {
    /// The voltage at which a receiver of this family switches, on a supply of `vdd` volts.
    fn switches_at(self, vdd: f64) -> f64 {
        match self {
            Family::Cmos => 0.5 * vdd,
            Family::Ttl => 1.4,
        }
    }
}

/// True when `v` reads as high for the family `f`.
fn reads_high(f: Family, v: f64, vdd: f64) -> bool { v > f.switches_at(vdd) }

f.switches_at(vdd) and Family::switches_at(f, vdd) are the same call; the first reads in the order the data flows. A type may have several impl blocks. Two limits apply: the built-in types take no impl of yours, because their methods are a fixed list (chapter 13), and a module takes none either, because a module is hardware and what its blocks share is written in its body (chapter 7).

Paths and visibility

A name with :: in it is a path: Level::High is the variant High of Level, and Family::DEFAULT a constant of Family. The same notation reaches into a namespace, as in std::constants::PI. use path; gives the last name of a path a short name in the file that writes it.

Visibility has two levels. An item is visible in the file that declares it and in the namespaces nested in that file; the word pub in front of it makes it visible from outside. The same word in front of a field of a struct or a function of an impl does the same for that field or function, so a type can show its name and keep its fields to itself. Nothing lies between the two levels. A one-file model needs no pub at all; chapter 12 takes up files, namespaces and packages.

Values are copied, never shared

A value is data, and using it copies it. Passing a number, a flag or an enum to a function hands the function its own copy; whatever the function computes, the caller's variable is as it was. Two names never refer to one piece of storage, so no statement can change a value "at a distance". That is the whole memory model: there is no allocation, no pointer and nothing to free. Every size is known before the run, because the hardware a model describes does not grow while it runs.

Three consequences are worth knowing by name.

  • A function cannot modify its argument. A function that "updates" something takes the old value and yields the new one: fn bump(c: u8) -> u8 { c + 1 }. The caller keeps the result under the name it likes, often the same one: count = bump(count);. A method follows the same rule, because its self is a copy as well.
  • Every type is copied. A struct, an array and an enum with data are copied exactly as a number is, however large they are. Nothing has to be asked for, and reading a value never uses it up: a variable can be read as often as you like.
  • Nothing is lent. A function that takes a table of numbers takes the table, [f64; N], and is called with it. The language has no reference, no pointer and no view into another value, so & is only the operator on bits.

What a model does share is hardware. A signal, a node and an instance each exist once, and a port is a connection to that one thing, never a copy of it. Chapter 5 starts from there.

What a model may not do

A model describes hardware that is fixed before the run starts. The language therefore has no construct that would create something while the simulation runs, or let two pieces of code change one value behind each other's back. These are deliberate limits, and each one has a direct replacement.

Not in the languageWhat a model writes
Growable containers and strings; any allocationArrays and signals whose size is fixed when the design is built. Text is a literal.
References and pointers of any kind; the words mut, ref and moveValues, which are copied. A function yields the new value. State lives in signals.
Global variablesA const for a fixed value, a param for one the user sets, a signal for one that changes.
Threads and asynchronous codeReactions. Concurrency is what hardware does by nature; the simulator orders it.
A character typeStrings exist only whole, as literals of type str.
macro_rules!, println!, format!Only two built-in ! forms: emit! for output and include_str! for data (E0901).
Default values in a function signature; two functions of one nameTrailing Option<T> parameters, which may be left out or named.
A default value for a number (u8::default()); a register that starts at zero because nobody said otherwiseThe first value, written. A value that may be absent is an Option; one that may be undetermined is a Logic (chapter 5).
Type aliasesThe type, written out.
Visibility levels between private and publicpub or nothing.

The methods of the built-in types are a closed list, given in chapter 13. A method that the list does not name does not resolve.

What the compiler runs today

The rules of this chapter are the language. The compiler does not yet run every form they describe. This function pair is correct MSL, and it uses a form that is still on the way: a function over a table of any length.

#[derive(PartialEq)]
enum Range { Low, Mid, High }

/// Which third of 0..=255 a code falls in.
fn range_of(code: u8) -> Range {
    match code {
        0..=84 => Range::Low,
        85..=169 => Range::Mid,
        _ => Range::High,
    }
}

fn mean(xs: [f64; N]) -> f64 {
    let total: f64 = xs.iter().sum();
    total / xs.len() as f64
}

Status

Running today: Option as a local, a parameter and a constant, with its methods, match and if let; optional parameters; tuples, arrays and structs as values, built, read, assigned and matched; enums, with or without fields and with a number given to a variant, matched and compared (an enum a signal carries derives Repr); the constants of an impl and its functions that take no self; use and pub. Everything the three files above use is in that list.

Parsed and checked, and not run yet: functions generic over a length, and methods that take self. A model that uses one of them is stopped before the run with the code P0001 and a message that names the form, so nothing is ever computed by a rule other than the ones above.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0180let limit: Option<f64> = Some(3.3); let x = limit + 1.0;An Option<f64> is not an f64. Say what None means: limit.unwrap_or(0.0) + 1.0.
E0180a match on a Level with no arm for Level::BetweenThe arms cover every value. Add the arm, or a last arm _ => … for all the rest.
E0143fn scale(x: f64, k: f64 = 2.0)No default arguments. k: Option<f64> and k.unwrap_or(2.0).
E0140scale(v, gain: 3.0)A named argument must name an optional parameter. It is called k.
E0142scale(v, limit: 1.0, 3.0)A positional argument cannot follow a named one. scale(v, 3.0, limit: 1.0).
E0101fn mean(xs: &[f64])Nothing is lent: there is no reference. Take the table itself, fn mean<const N: usize>(xs: [f64; N]); every value is copied where it is used.
E0161#[derive(Clone, Copy)]Copying is not a trait: every value is copied where it is used. Delete the two names.
E0161let xs: Vec<f64> = Vec::new();No heap types, so the path does not resolve. Use an array: [f64; 8].
E0161Level::MidThe enum has no such variant, so the path does not resolve. Check the declaration.

One mistake has no special code because it is a plain type error: r: f64 = 100. An integer literal is never an f64; write 100.0.

Try it

In levels.msl, add a variant Over after High and run the file without touching the match. Read the message: it names the type whose values the arms no longer cover. Then add the arm. Next, in option.msl, change slope to 0.0 and predict found, when and late before you run. (false, 0 and false: rising is now None.)

Chapter 4

Traits and generics

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can declare a trait and provide it for a type, write a function or a type once for a whole family of types or sizes, say what such a family must be able to do, pass a function to another function, and explain what the compiler builds from all of it.

Hardware repeats itself with small differences: a register is the same circuit for 8 bits or for an enum, a filter is the same loop for 3 taps or for 64, a comparison is the same test for volts or for codes. Writing each case by hand is how copies drift apart. This chapter has the three tools that remove the copies. A trait names something several types can do. A generic item is written once with a placeholder, for a type or for a size. A closure is a small function written where it is needed and passed along like a value.

You have used all three from the outside already. Option<f64> is a generic type with f64 in its placeholder, #[derive(PartialEq)] provides a trait, and a signal of type Bit<u8> is a generic kind of wire. This chapter shows the inside.

Traits: what a type can do

A trait is a named list of functions and constants. A type implements the trait by providing them. The trait itself computes nothing: it is the agreement between code that needs a capability and the types that have it.

/// Something that turns a voltage into a logic level.
trait Receiver {
    /// How many levels the receiver tells apart.
    const LEVELS: u32;

    /// The voltage at which it switches, on a supply of `vdd` volts.
    fn switches_at(self, vdd: f64) -> f64;

    /// True when `v` reads as high. A type may keep this body or give its own.
    fn reads_high(self, v: f64, vdd: f64) -> bool { v > self.switches_at(vdd) }
}

/// A logic family.
#[derive(PartialEq, Repr)]
enum Family { Cmos, Ttl }

impl Receiver for Family {
    const LEVELS: u32 = 2;
    fn switches_at(self, vdd: f64) -> f64 {
        match self {
            Family::Cmos => 0.5 * vdd,
            Family::Ttl => 1.4,
        }
    }
}

/// A receiver that switches at a fixed fraction of its supply.
struct Ratio { fraction: f64 }

impl Receiver for Ratio {
    const LEVELS: u32 = 2;
    fn switches_at(self, vdd: f64) -> f64 { self.fraction * vdd }
}

Read it in three steps.

  1. The trait lists what is required. An item that ends in ; has no body: every type that implements the trait must give one. An item with a body, like reads_high, is a default: a type gets it as written unless it gives its own.
  2. impl Receiver for Family provides the trait for one type. It gives every item that has no default, with the signature the trait states. It may not add an item the trait does not list.
  3. The functions are then methods of the type. Family::Ttl.reads_high(2.0, 5.0) uses the default body, which calls the switches_at of Family. The constant is read through the type: Family::LEVELS.

Inside a trait and inside an impl, Self is the type the trait is implemented for. A trait may require another: trait Receiver: PartialEq { … } says that every receiver can also be compared with ==, and lets the trait's own bodies rely on that.

Three rules keep a design the same wherever it is compiled:

  • One implementation per pair. A trait is implemented at most once for a type.
  • Written next to one of the two. impl Tr for T stands in the crate that declares the trait or in the crate that declares the type, never in a third. (A crate is one tree of source files; chapter 12.)
  • A trait is not a type. There is no variable "of type Receiver" that could hold a Family now and a Ratio later. A trait is named in three places only: after impl, inside #[derive(…)], and as a requirement on a generic parameter, which the next part introduces. When a value must be one of several kinds while the model runs, the type for that is an enum.

The traits the language brings

A few traits come with the language, and the compiler writes most of them for you. That is what #[derive(…)] has been doing since chapter 3: each name in its list is a trait, and the attribute asks for the standard implementation.

TraitWhat a type gainsHow a type gets it
PartialEq== and !=.derived, or written
PartialOrd<, <=, >, >=: variants in the order declared, fields first to last.derived, or written
DefaultT::default(), the value of "nothing yet": None for an Option, x on every level for a Logic, and for a struct, a tuple or an array the default of each part. Numbers and bool have none.derived on a struct whose fields all have it
ReprA layout as bits, which is what allows a signal to carry the type. Chapter 5.derived, or written for a special encoding
NatureMarks a kind of signal. Chapter 6.by declaring a nature
Fn(A, B) -> RCan be called with those arguments. The last part of this chapter.every function and closure has it

The built-in types have what fits them: numbers and bool have the traits of the other rows but not Default, an Option<T> has Default whatever T is, and a tuple or an array has each trait when its parts do.

Operators are traits too. A type has the operator & when it implements BitAnd, | with BitOr, ^ with BitXor, ! with Not, the shifts with Shl and Shr, and == with PartialEq. Each trait names the type of its result, so an operator need not give back what it took: Logic is a library struct whose impl BitAnd for Logic<N> follows the four-state table, and its == gives a Logic because its PartialEq says so. A type of your own gets a & b the same way: impl BitAnd for Flags { fn bitand(self, r: Flags) -> Flags { Flags { bits: self.bits & r.bits } } }. Arithmetic and ordering have no trait; they belong to the numbers and to a derived PartialOrd.

Generic functions

A generic function has one or more type parameters, written in angle brackets after its name. Inside the function the parameter stands for a type the caller chooses.

/// One of two values, chosen by `sel`: a two-way multiplexer for any type.
fn pick(sel: bool, a: T, b: T) -> T { if sel { a } else { b } }

/// The larger of two values, for any type that can be ordered.
fn larger(a: T, b: T) -> T { if a > b { a } else { b } }

/// How far `v` lies above the switching point of any receiver.
fn overdrive(r: R, v: f64, vdd: f64) -> f64 { v - r.switches_at(vdd) }

pick asks nothing of T: it only passes values of that type along, so any type will do. The other two need more, and they say so with a bound.

  • A bound is a trait after a colon. T: PartialOrd reads "any type T that implements PartialOrd". Several traits are joined with +, as in T: PartialOrd + Repr.
  • The bound is all the body may use. Inside larger, a > b is allowed because of PartialOrd. Using a a second time needs no bound, since every value is copied. a + b would be an error in the function itself, whatever types callers have in mind, because nothing says a T can be added.
  • The bound is what every caller must meet. overdrive accepts a Family and a Ratio, because both implement Receiver, and no other type.

A call does not usually name the type. The compiler reads it from the arguments:

let v = pick(en, 3.3, 0.0);                     // T is f64
let level = pick(en, Family::Cmos, Family::Ttl);    // T is Family
let top = larger(3u8, 200);                     // T is u8, and 200 is a u8 too
let over = overdrive(Family::Cmos, 2.8, 3.3);   // R is Family
let wide = larger::(3, 200);               // the type, written out

Both arguments of pick have the one type T, so pick(en, 3.3, 0) is a type error: a float and an integer are two types. When nothing decides a parameter, or to say it for the reader, the arguments are written after :: as in the last line. The :: is there so that < is not read as a comparison.

Generic types

A struct or an enum takes parameters the same way, and then stands for a family of types.

/// A closed range of any ordered type.
struct Window { lo: T, hi: T }

impl Window {
    /// True when `x` lies in the window, ends included.
    fn holds(self, x: T) -> bool { self.lo <= x && x <= self.hi }
}

/// True when the supply `vdd` is inside its tolerance.
fn in_tolerance(vdd: f64) -> bool {
    let allowed = Window { lo: 3.0, hi: 3.6 };  // a Window
    allowed.holds(vdd)
}
  • A use fills the parameter. Window<f64> is a range of voltages and Window<u8> a range of codes. They are two different types that share one declaration. In an expression the compiler finds the argument from the fields, as in Window { lo: 3.0, hi: 3.6 }.
  • The bounds are stated once, on the declaration. An impl for the generic type repeats the names of the parameters, impl<T> Window<T>, and the bounds come with them.
  • An impl may also cover many types at once. impl<T: Receiver> Describe for T { … } provides the trait Describe for every type that implements Receiver.

Option is declared exactly this way, enum Option<T> { None, Some(T) }, which is why one declaration serves Option<f64>, Option<Level> and every other. A parameter may have a default, used when a use leaves it out: the digital wire is declared with T = bool, so Bit alone means Bit<bool>.

Sizes as parameters: const generics

A parameter may also stand for a number that is known before the run. It is written const N: usize, and it is what lets one function serve tables of every length, with the length still part of each type.

/// The sum of the products of two tables of one length.
fn dot(a: [f64; N], b: [f64; N]) -> f64 {
    let sum = 0.0;
    for k in 0..N { sum += a[k] * b[k]; }
    sum
}

/// The mean of a table, which may not be empty.
fn mean_of(xs: [f64; N]) -> f64 where N >= 1 {
    let sum = 0.0;
    for x in xs { sum += x; }
    sum / N as f64
}

/// The table with one more element, 0.0, at its end.
fn widened(xs: [f64; N]) -> [f64; N + 1] {
    let wide = [0.0; N + 1];
    for k in 0..N { wide[k] = xs[k]; }
    wide
}
  • N is a constant inside the item. It may size an array, bound a loop and take part in arithmetic, as N as f64 does.
  • The types carry the check. dot takes two tables of the same N. Calling it with a table of three and a table of four is a type error, found before the run, so no length test is needed in the body.
  • A use states the number or lets it be read. dot([1.0, 2.0], [3.0, 4.0]) has N = 2 from its arguments. Written out, an argument is a literal or a single name, mean_of::<4>(xs), and any other expression goes in braces, Fifo<u8, { DEPTH + 1 }>.
  • A const parameter is an integer or a bool. Type and const parameters mix freely: struct Fifo<T, const DEPTH: usize> { slots: [T; DEPTH], count: usize }.

where: a condition on the numbers

The header of mean_of ends in where N >= 1. A where clause is a condition over the const parameters of the item, checked each time the item is used with new arguments. mean_of of an empty table is refused when the design is built, instead of dividing by zero during a run.

where has this one meaning everywhere in the language: a condition that must be true. On a function or a module it is about constants and params, as in where N >= 1; on a single param it is about that param, as in r: f64 = 1k where r > 0.0 (chapter 7). A requirement on a type is never written there. It is a bound, and its place is in the angle brackets.

What the compiler builds from a generic item

A generic item is a recipe, and nothing of it exists until it is used. When the design is built, the compiler makes one concrete copy for each distinct list of arguments an item is used with. Take a design that contains these four calls:

The design callsThe compiler builds
pick(en, 3.3, 0.0)pick with T = f64
pick(en, Family::Cmos, Family::Ttl)pick with T = Family
pick(sel, vdd, 0.0)nothing new: T = f64 exists already
dot(taps, samples) with tables of 3dot with N = 3, whose loop has exactly three turns

Four consequences follow, and they are what you see as a user:

  1. Each copy is checked as if you had written it by hand. The bounds are checked at the use, and so is every where. An error is reported when the design is built and points at the use that caused it, never at a later moment of the run.
  2. Equal arguments are the same thing. Two uses with the same arguments share one copy, and Window<f64> written in two files is one type. Window<f64> and Window<u8> are different types, and [f64; 3] and [f64; 4] too.
  3. Nothing generic is left when the run starts. There is no lookup of a type or of a method while the model runs, so a generic function costs what the hand-written copy would cost. A size is a plain number by then: the loop of dot for three taps is three multiplications and three additions.
  4. A design that never uses an item never builds it. A library may offer a generic module for every width without any cost to the designs that place only one.

This is also why a trait is not a type. Every call of a trait's function is tied to one concrete type when the design is built, so the compiler always knows which body runs.

Generic hardware

The hardware constructs take parameters in the same brackets, with the same rules, and the chapters that introduce them use the forms of this one:

ConstructWrittenMeans
a kind of signalnature Bit<T: Repr = bool>A digital wire that carries any type with a layout in bits: Bit, Bit<u8>, Bit<Family>. Chapter 6.
a module over a typemodule Dff<T: Repr + Default>(in clk: Bit, in d: Bit<T>, out q: Bit<T>)One register for every type a wire can carry and that has a first value of its own. Chapter 9.
a module over a sizemodule Ladder<const N: usize>(a: Electrical, b: Electrical) where N >= 1A netlist with N sections, placed as Ladder::<4>(x, y). Chapter 7.

A generic module is built the way a generic function is: one concrete module for each list of arguments the design places, fixed before the run. The object model keeps the generic module as you wrote it (chapter 17).

Closures and function values

A function can be handed to another function. The shortest way to write one for that purpose is a closure: the parameters between two bars, then an expression. |v| v * gain is a function of one argument v, and it may read the names around it, here gain.

/// The reading held to `limit`, or 0.0 when there is no reading.
fn held(reading: Option, limit: f64) -> f64 {
    reading.map(|v| v.min(limit)).unwrap_or(0.0)        // the closure reads `limit` from around it
}

module Clip() {}

digital for Clip {
    on at(1u) {
        let gain = 2.0;
        let seen: Option = Some(2.1);
        let never: Option = None;
        let amplified = seen.map(|v| v * gain);         // Some(4.2)
        let shifted = seen.and_then(|v| Some(v - 0.1)); // the closure itself yields an Option
        emit!("clip", amplified = amplified.unwrap_or(0.0), held = held(amplified, 3.3),
              shifted = shifted.unwrap_or(0.0), nothing = held(never, 3.3));
    }
}

Run it

piperine run clip.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-6
run clip: transient, top Clip, backend rust
@1.000000e-6 Clip clip amplified=4.200000e0 held=3.300000e0 shifted=2.000000e0 nothing=0.000000e0

time
0.000000e0
2.000000e-6

map is a method of Option that takes a function: on Some(x) it calls the function with x and wraps the result in Some, and on None it calls nothing. So 2.1 is doubled to 4.2, then held to the limit of 3.3, and the None passes through both steps untouched.

Four rules describe a closure:

  • It captures by copy. A closure takes a copy of each outer name it reads, at the moment it is written. Nothing it does can change those names, and a later change of them does not reach it.
  • Its parameter types are usually read from where it is passed. They may be written, |v: f64| v * gain, and a body of several statements goes in braces.
  • It is computation, like a function. A closure writes no signal and places no hardware.
  • It is called by position. A call through a function value takes its arguments in order; the named and optional arguments of chapter 3 belong to functions called by their name.

To accept a function, a parameter needs a type, and there are two. Every closure has a type of its own that cannot be written down, so the general form is a generic parameter with a bound that says how it is called: F: Fn(f64) -> f64 reads "any function or closure that takes an f64 and yields an f64". The second is the function type fn(f64) -> f64, written without a generic parameter. It accepts the name of a function, and a closure that reads nothing from around it.

/// The value of `f` at the middle of the window `lo ..= hi`.
fn at_middle f64>(f: F, lo: f64, hi: f64) -> f64 { f(0.5 * (lo + hi)) }

/// The reading after the correction `fix`.
fn corrected(v: f64, fix: fn(f64) -> f64) -> f64 { fix(v) }

/// Removes the offset of the converter.
fn remove_offset(v: f64) -> f64 { v - 0.012 }

/// A ramp of `slope` volts per second, sampled at the middle of the first microsecond.
fn mid_ramp(slope: f64) -> f64 { at_middle(|t| slope * t, 0.0, 1u) }

/// A reading with the converter's offset removed.
fn clean(v: f64) -> f64 { corrected(v, remove_offset) }

The library takes functions in the same two ways: map and and_then above, the iteration methods of arrays (chapter 13), and limit, with which an analog model holds back the change of a voltage from one iteration of the solver to the next (chapter 8).

What the compiler runs today

Status

Running today: the generic types the language brings, such as Option<f64> and Bit<u8>, and a closure written directly as the argument of map or and_then. Everything clip.msl uses is in that list.

Parsed and checked, and not run yet: the methods of a trait, generic functions, generic structs, const parameters and generic modules. A model that uses one of them is stopped before the run with the code P0001 and a message that names the form, so nothing is ever computed by a rule other than the ones above. Every other listing of this chapter is correct MSL that waits for those forms. One check is also still missing: an impl is not yet compared with the trait it provides, so a missing or an extra item is found only where it is used.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0900fn over<R>(r: R) -> f64 where R: Receiverwhere holds a condition on constants, not a bound. Put the bound in the brackets: fn over<R: Receiver>(r: R).
E0900fn over(r: impl Receiver) -> f64A trait is not a type. Name a parameter for it: fn over<R: Receiver>(r: R).
E0101fn over(r: &dyn Receiver) -> f64No value picks its type during the run. Use a generic parameter, or an enum of the kinds you have.
E0900<Family as Receiver>::LEVELSAn item of a trait is reached through the type: Family::LEVELS.
E0905fn first<'a>(xs: &'a [f64]) -> f64There are no lifetimes, because nothing is lent: a table is passed as a value. Write fn first<const N: usize>(xs: [f64; N]) -> f64.
E0903type Volts = f64;There are no type aliases. For a distinct type write a struct, struct Volts(f64);, or use the type itself.

Try it

In clip.msl, change gain to 1.0 and predict amplified and held before you run. (Both 2.1: the reading is below the limit, so min leaves it.) Then read larger again and say why larger(3u8, 2.5) cannot be accepted. (Both arguments have the one type T, and a u8 and an f64 are two types.) Last, write the header of a function that takes a table of any length and any ordered type and yields its largest element.
