(fn largest<T: PartialOrd + Copy, const N: usize>(xs: [T; N]) -> T where N >= 1.)

Chapter 5

Values in hardware

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can say where a computation stops and hardware begins, report what a model does with emit!, make a type of your own travel on a signal, and say what a type may hold: a plain value, an Option for absence, a Logic for the x and z of digital logic.

A value is data with no time: a number, a flag, an enum, a struct. Chapters 2 to 4 gave the rules of values. Everything a model computes with is a value; what it connects to is hardware, which exists for the whole simulation. This chapter stands on the line between the two: how a value is driven onto a signal, how it is reported, which types a signal can carry, and what a signal holds before anything has driven it.

Where values meet hardware

Everything so far computes and then is gone. Hardware is different: it exists for the whole simulation and its parts react to each other.

The two are also passed around in opposite ways, and this is the one split of the language between "by value" and "by reference". A value is copied wherever it is used, so no two names ever share one. A piece of hardware exists once: a signal, a node and an instance each have an identity, and a port is a connection to that one thing, never a copy of it. Two modules joined to one signal see the same signal. Since that is the only sharing there is, it has a strict rule for change: what a signal holds changes only when a block drives it, with <- in a reaction or +> in an analog block, at the moment the simulator gives that statement. Reading a signal gives a value, a copy of what it holds at that moment.

Here is a complete file with a clock, a counter and a top that connects them. It is the "blinky" of this book.

/// A free-running clock of frequency `f`.
module Osc(out clk: Bit = false) {
    f: f64 = 1M where f > 0.0;
}
digital for Osc {
    on clk.changed() { clk <- !clk after 0.5 / f; }
}

/// Counts rising edges, wrapping at 256.
module Counter(in clk: Bit, out count: Bit = 0) {}
digital for Counter {
    on clk.rise() {
        count <- count + 1;
        emit!("edge", t = now(), count = count);
    }
}

module Blinky() {
    signal clk: Bit;
    pub signal count: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk) { f: 1M };
    let ctr = Counter(clk, count);
}

Read it with the value language in mind, and note each place where a familiar form means something new:

  1. module declares hardware. A module is a piece of hardware with ports. It is unrelated to mod, which names a file as a namespace.
  2. A port list is not a parameter list. out clk: Bit = false is a wire this module drives, with the value it starts at. in clk: Bit is a wire it only reads. A port exists for the whole simulation; it is not passed once.
  3. A param is declared like a field. f: f64 = 1M where f > 0.0; has a default and a check. There is no assignment to it anywhere: the placement Osc(clk) { f: 1M } binds it by name.
  4. on event { … } is not an if. It is a reaction: the body runs once each time the event fires, forever. clk.changed() fires whenever clk takes a new value; clk.rise() on a rising edge.
  5. <- is not =. count <- count + 1; does not change count now. It schedules the new value, and every read in the same reaction still sees the old one. With after d the new value arrives d seconds later: the oscillator toggles itself every half period, and each toggle is the change that fires the reaction again.
  6. A let in a module body places hardware. let ctr = Counter(clk, count); is an instance named ctr, not a call that returns.

The clock starts at false. That first value is itself a change (from "nothing yet" to low), so the oscillator's reaction fires at time zero and the clock rises for the first time half a period later, at 0.5 µs.

This is also the scaffold of the earlier examples, now readable: module Literals() {} is a piece of hardware with no ports and nothing inside, and on at(1u) { … } is a reaction to the event "the time is 1 µs", which fires once.

emit!: how a model reports

emit! is the output statement of the language. It takes the name of a stream, a string literal, and then any number of fields, each written name = value. Every time it runs, it writes one record to that stream. The line emit!("edge", t = now(), count = count); writes one record at each rising edge. The first three records of a transient look like this, laid out as a table for reading:

stream Blinky.ctr.edge
  instant        fields
  (0.5 µs, 0)    t = 5.0e-7   count = 0
  (1.5 µs, 0)    t = 1.5e-6   count = 1
  (2.5 µs, 0)    t = 2.5e-6   count = 2

Two things to notice. The stream is Blinky.ctr.edge: the name you wrote, under the path of the instance that wrote it, so two counters never mix their records. And the first record says count = 0 although the line above it committed count + 1: a commit takes effect after the reaction, so the record shows the count before the edge. If you want the new value in the record, emit the expression you committed: count = count + 1.

Run it

Save the file as blinky.msl, anywhere. A file with no project around it runs by its name; the flags say which analysis. The top is the one module without ports.

piperine run blinky.msl --analysis transient --stop 3e-6
run blinky: transient, top Blinky, backend rust
@5.000000e-7 Blinky.ctr edge t=5.000000e-7 count=0
@1.500000e-6 Blinky.ctr edge t=1.500000e-6 count=1
@2.500000e-6 Blinky.ctr edge t=2.500000e-6 count=2

time         Blinky.clk  Blinky.count
0.000000e0   false       0
3.000000e-6  false       3

The first three lines after the header are the three records of the table above, as the command prints them: one line each, live. The table that follows has a row for the start and one for the stop: the counter has counted three rising edges by 3 µs. The design has no analog node and the transient runs to its stop all the same.

With --print-step the table has a row every microsecond, each signal holding the value it had after the last instant at or before the row:

piperine run blinky.msl --analysis transient --stop 3e-6 --print-step 1e-6 --print count
run blinky: transient, top Blinky, backend rust
@5.000000e-7 Blinky.ctr edge t=5.000000e-7 count=0
@1.500000e-6 Blinky.ctr edge t=1.500000e-6 count=1
@2.500000e-6 Blinky.ctr edge t=2.500000e-6 count=2

time         Blinky.count
0.000000e0   0
1.000000e-6  1
2.000000e-6  2
3.000000e-6  3

These are the properties of a record, and of the statement that writes it:

PropertyRule
What you writeA stream name (a string literal) and named fields: name = value.
FormattingNone in the model. A field keeps its type, and whoever reads the record formats it.
Where it goesTo a named stream per instance, read by the host: the command line or your own program.
What is attachedThe instance path, the analysis and the instant, on every record.
When it runsWhen the code around it runs: once per firing in a reaction, at every evaluation in analog.
A step the solver takes backA reaction's record from that step does not exist.
An Option or a LogicThe field is None or the value, or the characters 0, 1, x, z.
Effect on the modelNone: no expression can read a stream.
Where it is allowedIn a reaction, in analog, and in a fn called from them.

Because fields are typed and named, a record is data, not text. A host can plot count against t, compare two runs field by field, or assert on a value, without parsing anything. A message with values in it is simply a record with a text field:

emit!("log", msg = "overrange", vin = v(inp));

The fields are your choice, with two limits: field names within one emit! are distinct, and a field of one name has the same type everywhere a stream is written. A record is its fields and the instance, the analysis and the instant: control takes a bool, so a site is reached or it is not.

Two stream names are reserved and global: sim and checks. You do not write to them directly. The standard library does it for you: finish() ends the analysis, and require(name, pass) records a check the host judges. Both are ordinary functions over emit!, and chapter 9 uses them. For a plain message there is log(Severity::Info, "text"), which writes to the instance's log stream.

Good to know

Every emit! in a design is known before the run starts: its stream, its field names and their types. A host lists them and chooses which streams it wants, so you can leave your debug records in a model and decide per run what to look at.

The hardware constructs: eleven concepts

Around the value language MSL has eleven concepts. Each one has a single rule. You have met most of them already; the chapter in the last column teaches each properly.

#ConceptIn one sentenceChapter
1value and fnData with no time. A function keeps no state, writes no carrier and places no instance.2 to 4
2natureSays what a carrier holds and how several drivers of it combine.6
3carrierA signal or port that exists over time. You connect it by passing it to a port.6
4driveWhat one module writes to one carrier. The carrier's value combines all its drives.6, 9
5paramA value fixed during a solve. The model reads it; the host sets it.7
6modulePorts, params and a netlist, fixed at elaboration. Calling a module places an instance.7
7let in a module bodyNames an expression. It is evaluated wherever the name is read; a let inside a block, as in chapter 2, is evaluated once, where it stands.7
8analog for Mprobe +> x adds to a branch's flow or potential at every Newton iteration.8
9digital for MA reaction on ev { q <- x; } runs once when ev fires; its commits apply at the next microstep.9
10eventA value of type Event that fires at instants: changed, at, cross, settle, combined with || and &&.9
11instant(t, n): at one time, microsteps run until nothing is pending, with the analog side solved in between.10

Three symbols write to something, and each belongs to one layer. = names a value, in a let, or defines what a signal always equals, in a module body. <- schedules a new value for a digital signal, in a reaction. +> adds a contribution to an analog branch, in an analog block.

Where a function may be called

A function has no time of its own, but its body may touch things that do: it may probe a voltage, read the clock of the simulation, or write a record. Here are a function that touches none of them, and one that yields an event:

/// The current of an ideal junction at a voltage `vd`.
pub fn diode_current(vd: f64, is: f64, nvt: f64) -> f64 { is * ((vd / nvt).exp() - 1.0) }

/// A request that is acknowledged in the same cycle.
pub fn handshake(req: Bit, ack: Bit) -> Event { req.rise() && ack }

You never declare where a function may be called. The compiler infers a class from the body: a pure function, like diode_current, works anywhere; one that probes a voltage or reads now() works where the solver is running; one that returns Event, like handshake, works where events go; and one that calls emit! works where emit! does. Inside analog, calls are differentiated automatically, so diode_current needs no hand-written derivative.

Good to know

Anything with state is a module, never a function. fn f(x: f64) -> f64 { ddt(x) } is an error, because ddt remembers the past; write module f(x: f64) -> f64 { ddt(x) }. Chapter 7 shows modules that return values.

Types a signal can carry: Repr

A signal is hardware, and what travels on it is bits. Only a type that implements the trait Repr can be carried by a signal. Repr lays a value out as a row of boolean levels, the bits a wire would carry. Primitives have it built in; your own types derive it:

#[derive(Repr, PartialEq)]
enum Zone { Under, Ok, Over }

A derived struct puts its fields side by side, first field lowest. A derived enum lays out a tag, then the widest payload. One-hot or Gray encodings are a hand-written impl Repr.

A digital signal names the type it carries in angle brackets: Bit<Zone> is a wire for a Zone, Bit<u8> a wire for eight bits, and Bit alone a wire for a bool.

A complete example: a window monitor

Put a type, a function and a module together. This file watches a node and reports, on every clock edge, whether its voltage is under, inside or over a window. The source is a ramp of 1 V/µs, so the node walks through all three zones.

#[derive(Repr, PartialEq)]
enum Zone { Under, Ok, Over }

/// Where `x` lies against the window `lo ..= hi`.
fn zone_of(x: f64, lo: f64, hi: f64) -> Zone {
    if x < lo { Zone::Under } else if x > hi { Zone::Over } else { Zone::Ok }
}

/// Samples node `a` on every rising clock edge and reports its zone.
module WindowMonitor(a: Electrical, in clk: Bit, out zone: Bit = Zone::Ok) {
    lo: f64 = 1.0;
    hi: f64 = 2.0 where hi > lo;
}
digital for WindowMonitor {
    on clk.rise() {
        let z = zone_of(v(a), lo, hi);
        zone <- z;
        if z != zone { emit!("zone", t = now(), v = v(a), from = zone, to = z); }
    }
}

/// A voltage that rises from 0 V at `slope` volts per second.
module Ramp(p: Electrical) { slope: f64 = 1M; }
analog for Ramp { v(p) +> slope * now(); }

/// A free-running clock.
module Osc(out clk: Bit = false) { f: f64 = 1M where f > 0.0; }
digital for Osc { on clk.changed() { clk <- !clk after 0.5 / f; } }

module MonitorTop() {
    signal x: Electrical;
    signal clk: Bit;
    pub signal zone: Bit;
    let src = Ramp(x);
    let osc = Osc(clk);
    let mon = WindowMonitor(x, clk, zone);
}

What is new here:

  • out zone: Bit<Zone> = Zone::Ok. A Bit<T> carries any Repr type, so the output of the monitor is the enum itself, not an encoding you have to remember.
  • let z = zone_of(v(a), lo, hi); A let inside a reaction names a value for the rest of the body. The reaction probes v(a), calls a pure function and gets a value.
  • if z != zone { emit!(…) } prints only when the zone changes. zone on the right still reads the old value (the commit one line up has not applied yet), which is exactly what makes from = zone, to = z correct.
  • A field can be an enum. Any value type is a field; the record keeps Zone::Under as that, not as a number.

The clock rises at 0.5, 1.5 and 2.5 µs, where the ramp is near 0.5, 1.5 and 2.5 V:

stream MonitorTop.mon.zone
  instant        fields
  (0.5 µs, 0)    t = 5.0e-7   v ≈ 0.5   from = Ok      to = Under
  (1.5 µs, 0)    t = 1.5e-6   v ≈ 1.5   from = Under   to = Ok
  (2.5 µs, 0)    t = 2.5e-6   v ≈ 2.5   from = Ok      to = Over

The values of v are "near" on purpose. A reaction that runs on a clock edge reads the analog solution the solver had just before the edge, as a real sampler does. Chapter 10 makes that precise.

Run it

Save the file as monitor.msl and keep only the stream you care about:

What a type may hold

A value is what its type says it is. A bool is true or false, a u8 is one of 256 numbers, an f64 is a real number, and the same holds for enums, structs, tuples and arrays made of them. Nothing hides behind a read: you use the value, and an if on it runs exactly one branch.

Digital design also needs two things that a plain number cannot say: "this wire is not driven" and "this value is not determined". MSL does not give every value a hidden mood for them. When a model needs one, the type says so in its declaration, and a reader of the declaration knows it. There are two such types, and each answers one question.

  • Option<T> answers "is there a value at all?" It is None or Some(x). Option<u8> and Option<f64> are different types, and Option<Logic<4>> is either wholly absent or a present four-state vector.
  • Logic answers "what does the wire carry?" Each level of a Logic is 0, 1, x (not determined) or z (not driven), as in a Verilog waveform. Logic alone has one level and Logic<4> has four. The type is not special to the language: it is a library struct, and its operators are written in the library as ordinary functions of the operator traits.

The operators of Logic follow four-state tables, level by level. In the tables, x stands for an x or a z operand, and a result is never z:

a & b01xa | b01xa ^ b01x
0000001x001x
101x1111110x
x0xxxx1xxxxx

A 0 still decides an & and a 1 still decides an |, whatever stands on the other side. !a turns 0 into 1, 1 into 0, and x and z into x. A shift moves the levels with their states and fills with 0.

A Logic and a binary value mix level by level: a & 0b1111u4 converts the binary operand first, and the conversion is total, because every number is four-state logic with no x in it. The way back can fail, so it returns an Option: a.to_bits() is Some(n) when every level is 0 or 1 and None otherwise.

A signal starts with a value

Every signal has a value from the first instant, and the source says which. Either you write it, signal en: Bit = false;, or the type has a default, a value that means "nothing yet", and the signal starts at it:

TypeStarts at, with no = value
Option<T>None
Logic<N>x on every level
a tuple, an array, or a struct deriving Default, whose parts all have a defaultthe default of each part
bool, u8 and every integer, f64 and every floatno default: the value must be written

The last row is a deliberate choice, and one place where MSL differs from languages in which u8::default() is zero. A register with no reset value has no meaning that a number could express, because zero is a value like any other. So signal en: Bit; is error E0187, naming the signal and the trait Default, and the fix is to write the first value. A register that may be absent writes Bit<Option<u8>>; one that may be undetermined writes Bit<Logic<4>> and starts at xxxx.

Catching an absent input with emit!

The classic bug: an input nobody drives. This adder says in its port type that b may be absent. It is placed in a top that gives a the constant 3 and takes b from an in port of its own, which nothing drives, so b starts at None. (Osc is the clock from the file above.)

/// Registers a + b on every clock edge, and says so when b is absent.
module Adder(in clk: Bit, in a: Bit, in b: Bit>, out sum: Bit = 0) {}
digital for Adder {
    on clk.rise() {
        match b {
            Some(v) => { sum <- a + v; }
            None => { emit!("absent", t = now(), a = a); }
        }
    }
}

module AdderTop(in b: Bit>) {
    signal clk: Bit;
    pub signal sum: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk);
    let add = Adder(clk, 3, b, sum);
}

An in port may be connected to an expression, here the literal 3. A top-level in port that nothing drives starts at its type's default, here None; for a type with no default, such as Bit<u8>, the run refuses to start and names the port. At each rising edge the match takes exactly one arm, and the record names what was missing:

stream AdderTop.add.absent
  instant        fields
  (0.5 µs, 0)    t = 5.0e-7   a = 3
  (1.5 µs, 0)    t = 1.5e-6   a = 3
  (2.5 µs, 0)    t = 2.5e-6   a = 3

The instant is (0.5 µs, 0): the reaction runs in the microstep of the clock edge, and sum stays at 0 because no commit is made.

Run it

Add the two modules to monitor.msl. The file now has two candidates for the top, so name the one you want. Its in port is left undriven, which is the bug under study. That is, once an Option can be carried on a signal. Today the command refuses the file and runs nothing:

piperine run monitor.msl --top AdderTop --analysis transient --stop 3e-6 --stream absent
compiling run `monitor` (top AdderTop) with rustc
error: E0105: a model panicked under known control in `AdderTop.add` at tick 500000000: `b` is a composite that holds no value yet, so it has no field or copy to read; drive it or give it an initial value first

Count the record lines from AdderTop.add when it runs: one at each rising edge, three in all.

Deciding on a Logic

An if, a while, a guard and the operands of && and || take a bool and nothing else. A Logic cannot decide by itself, so a model says how it wants to decide. a == b on two Logic values is itself a Logic: it is x when a compared level is x or z, since nobody can say whether they are equal. The four ways to turn a Logic into a decision are all functions that return a bool:

  • x.is_high(), x.is_low(), x.is_x() and x.is_z() are true exactly when every level is 1, 0, x or z.
  • same(a, b) is true when every level holds the same one of the four states in both: two x levels are the same, and an x is not a z.
  • match x.at(0) { … } covers the four states of level 0 with four arms.
  • x.to_bits() and x.to_bit() give an Option, which a match or if let opens.
module Peek(in clk: Bit, in a: Bit>, in b: Bit>) {}
digital for Peek {
    on clk.rise() {
        emit!("compare", eq = a == b, same = same(a, b), sure = (a == b).is_high());
    }
}

module PeekTop(in b: Bit>) {
    signal clk: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk);
    let peek = Peek(clk, logic("1010"), b);
}

logic("1010") writes a four-state value one character per level, highest level first, from 0, 1, x and z. The undriven b starts at xxxx, so each edge compares 1010 with xxxx: a == b prints x, same(a, b) is false and sure is false. A host prints a four-state cell as its characters, 0, 1, x, z, highest level first.

Run it

piperine run monitor.msl --top PeekTop --analysis transient --stop 3e-6 --stream compare

    ╰────

    ╰────

E0161

  × no method `is_high` on `bool`
    ╭─[monitor.msl:60:74]
 59 │     on clk.rise() {
 60 │         emit!("compare", eq = a == b, same = same(a, b), sure = (a == b).is_high());
    ·                                                                          ───┬───
    ·                                                                             ╰── `bool` has no such method (Appendix H)
 61 │     }
    ╰────

P0001

  × generic arguments on `Logic`, which has no generic parameter is not supported yet
    ╭─[monitor.msl:64:26]
 63 │ 
 64 │ module PeekTop(in b: Bit<Logic<4>>) {
    ·                          ────┬───
    ·                              ╰── generic arguments on `Logic`, which has no generic parameter is outside the M1 subset
 65 │     signal clk: Bit;
    ╰────

error: typeck failed with 4 diagnostic(s)

When it runs, each rising edge writes one record, eq=x same=false sure=false, three in all. Today the library type Logic is refused by name.

Analog code reads values

The analog solver computes with real numbers, so an analog block reads a discrete signal like any other expression when its type is a number or a bool:

module SampleHold(vin: Electrical, vout: Electrical, in clk: Bit) { signal held: Bit = 0.0; }
digital for SampleHold { on clk.rise() { held <- v(vin); } }
analog for SampleHold { v(vout) +> held; }

held is a real number from the first instant, so the contribution reads it as it is. A Logic is not a number, so it reaches an analog block through a function that returns one: x.to_volts(vdd) gives vdd for 1, 0 for 0 and half of vdd for x and z, and x.is_high() gives a bool. The model says what x means electrically, by choosing the function. Using a Logic where an f64 or a bool is required is error E0180.

Foreign functions

A function written in C is declared with extern "C" and no body. To use it inside analog, name its derivatives, one per f64 argument, because the compiler cannot look inside:

#[derivative(erfcx_dx)] extern "C" fn erfcx(x: f64) -> f64;
extern "C" fn erfcx_dx(x: f64) -> f64;

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0901println!("count = {}", count);Not a built-in form. emit!("dbg", count = count);
E0602emit!("edge {}", count);There is no format string, and a field needs a name: emit!("edge", count = count);
E0604emit!("edge", t = now(), t = count);A field name is used twice. Rename one.
E0601emit!("hello"); in a module bodyOutput runs when behavior runs. Put it in a reaction: on init() { emit!("hello"); }
E0187signal en: Bit;A bool has no default value. Write the first value: signal en: Bit = false;.
E0112out zone: Bit<Zone> with Zone not deriving ReprA carried type needs a layout. Add #[derive(Repr)] to the enum.
E0350fn slope(x: f64) -> f64 { ddt(x) }ddt is an instance with memory, and a fn has none. Make it a module slope(x: f64) -> f64.
E0123extern "C" fn erfcx(x: f64) -> f64; called in analogAdd #[derivative(erfcx_dx)] so the solver has a Jacobian.

Try it

In Counter, move the emit! line above the commit. What changes in the stream? (Nothing: both lines read the values from before the edge.) Then add a field half = count >= 128 and predict the time of the first record where it is true. (The 129th rising edge, at 128.5 µs.)

In PeekTop, replace logic("1010") with logic("10xz"). What does same(a, a) give? (True: each level holds the same state on both sides, even x and z.) And a & 0b1111u4, written as a Logic pattern? (10xx: the 1 and the 0 are decided, the x and the z are not.)

Chapter 6

Natures and carriers

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can pick the right kind of wire for a job, declare signals and ports of every kind, and build a shared open-drain line that several modules drive, with a monitor that prints every change of it.

A carrier is something that exists over time: a wire, a node, a register. You have used three already: the node vout, the clock clk and the register count. Every carrier has a nature, and the nature answers two questions: what does it hold, and what happens when several modules drive it.

Two questions, four answers

Think about real wires. A logic net driven by one gate holds one value and has one owner. An open-drain bus has many drivers and a rule for combining them. A circuit node has no "driver" at all: every element connected to it pushes or pulls current, and Kirchhoff's law settles the voltage. A block-diagram signal, such as the output of an ideal amplifier, is a voltage with one source and no current. MSL has exactly these four kinds:

discretediscrete that implements Resolvecontinuous with flowcontinuous, no flow
exampleBita nature you declare, such as AlarmLine belowElectricalVSignal
think ofa gate output, a registeran open-drain or tristate busa circuit nodea block-diagram arrow
driversexactly oneany number, combined by its resolve functionflows sum to zero (KCL)exactly one
written with=, <-=, <-+>+>
port directionin / outnonenonenone
computed bythe event enginethe event enginethe analog solverthe analog solver

How a nature is declared

Natures are ordinary declarations. These are the ones the standard library gives you, and the library gives no net with several drivers: you declare yours, as the example further on does.

nature Bit { value: T }                              // prelude; Bit is Bit
nature Electrical {
    potential: Voltage { access: v, units: "V", abstol: 1u },
    flow:      Current { access: i, units: "A", abstol: 1p },
}
nature Thermal {
    potential: Temperature { access: temp, units: "K", abstol: 1m },
    flow:      Power       { access: pwr,  units: "W", abstol: 1n },
}
nature VSignal { potential: Voltage { access: v, units: "V", abstol: 1u } }   // signal-flow

The body tells you the family. A discrete nature declares value: the type it holds. A continuous nature declares potential, and usually flow. A discrete nature accepts many drivers when it implements the trait Resolve, which holds one function from all the drives to one value; the next section says what that means.

What a nature can do: traits as capabilities

A trait names something a type can do, and a nature can implement traits like a type does. What a nature implements is a capability of every signal of that nature, and the language asks for a capability by name wherever it needs one. A signal of Thermal could call a method its nature gives it, t.celsius() for an impl Thermal that writes one, as a number can call its own methods.

The one capability the language itself asks for is Resolve, the capability of taking several drivers. A signal whose nature does not implement it has exactly one driver, and a second one is error E0220: its message names the nature and Resolve. A signal whose nature implements it takes any number of drivers, none included, and its value is always resolve of the drives that are present. resolve is a function and not a module, and the library declares no net nature, for one reason: what the drives mean is the model's business. A wired-AND, an open-drain line, a bus that allows one driver at a time and a net that adds currents are all a nature with a different impl Resolve, and none needs a special case in the language. Chapter 9 works four of them.

The access names become the probes you already used: v(a, b) reads a voltage and i(a, b) a current on Electrical; temp and pwr do the same on Thermal. units is a label for people and is never checked. abstol is the solver's tolerance for that quantity: a voltage is "converged" to about a microvolt, a current to a picoamp. There is no inheritance between natures; a high-voltage variant of Electrical is a second nature.

Signals

Inside a module, signal declares a carrier. An initial value is optional, and what it means depends on the family:

signal en:    Bit = false;                  // a bool has no default: write its first value
signal last:  Bit>;              // starts at None
signal count: Bit = 0;                  // starts at 0
signal level: Bit = 0.0;               // a real number that changes in steps
signal lines: [Bit; 8] = [false; 8];        // eight carriers, each with its own events
signal bus:   Bit> = logic("10xx");  // four-state, level by level
signal sda:   Sda;                          // resolved: any number of drivers, no value of its own
signal mid:   Electrical;                   // an internal node
signal vout:  Electrical = 0.0;          // where Newton starts; held there as a transient begins

A discrete signal has a value from the first instant: the one you write, or its type's default when it has one, and a type without a default must be given a first value (chapter 5). That value is committed at the first instant, and the commit is a change, so changed() fires for it. Until the signal changes again, prev() is that initial value. An edge is a change that ends at a level: rise() is a change that ends at true, so a clock that starts high has not risen. A model that wants to act at the start says so with init(). On a node, = 0.0 is where the solver begins its search for the operating point, and a transient analysis starts from that value.

An array of carriers costs what you write into it: storage for an element exists from its first commit, so [Bit<u8>; 1 << 32] is fine as a memory.

Ports

A port is a carrier that comes from outside. The port list decides what kind each port is, from its type:

PortWrittenConnected to
one-driver discretein a: Bit<T>, out q: Bit<T> = inita carrier; an in port also takes an expression
other naturesp: Electrical, sda: Sdaa carrier (a net)
bundletx: Stream<T>, rx: flip Stream<T>a bundle carrier or literal
value inputx: f64any expression

Only a one-driver discrete port has a direction, because only there does "who drives it" have a single answer: the module with the out port. That module also gives the initial value. A port of a nature that implements Resolve takes no direction: any module that sees it may drive it. An in port is read-only for the module.

gnd is the reference node of every continuous nature. It is not a global net you can abuse: a module reaches any other node only through its ports.

A complete example: one line, many drivers

Two sensors share one active-low alarm line. Each may pull it low; a pull-up keeps it high when nobody does. With a Bit this cannot be built: a Bit has one driver, and the second one is an error. So the file declares the nature of the line, AlarmLine, and gives it the capability of several drivers. What is driven is a level and a strength, Driven, from the library.

/// The nature of the alarm line: what is driven is a level and a strength.
pub nature AlarmLine { value: Driven }

impl Resolve for AlarmLine {
    /// The strongest drive wins; equal strongest drives that disagree give x.
    fn resolve(drives: [Driven; N]) -> Driven {
        Driven::strongest(drives)
    }
}

/// Pulls the line low while `req` is high, and lets go of it otherwise.
module OpenDrain(in req: Bit, line: AlarmLine) {
    line = if req { Driven::strong(false) } else { Driven::Z };
}

/// Reports every change of a line.
module LineWatch(line: AlarmLine) {}
digital for LineWatch {
    on line.changed() {
        emit!("line", t = now(), level = line.v, strength = line.s);
    }
}

/// The bench: the line, its drivers, and the two sensors as stimulus.
module Alarm() {
    signal door: Bit = false;
    signal window: Bit = false;
    signal alarm_n: AlarmLine;
    let pu = Pullup(alarm_n);
    let d0 = OpenDrain(door, alarm_n);
    let d1 = OpenDrain(window, alarm_n);
    let watch = LineWatch(alarm_n);
}
digital for Alarm {
    on at(1u) { door <- true; }
    on at(2u) { window <- true; }
    on at(3u) { door <- false; window <- false; }
}

Line by line:

  • line: AlarmLine has no direction. Any module that sees an AlarmLine may drive it, and nobody counts drivers. The impl Resolve above is what allows it.
  • line = if req { … } else { … }; is a definition, the = arrow of chapter 1: it is recomputed whenever req changes. What it defines is this module's drive of the line, not the line itself.
  • Driven is what a module drives into a line that resolves by strength: a level v, a Logic, and a strength s. Driven::strong(false) is a hard low; Driven::Z is "not driving". Pullup, from the library, works with any line whose nature implements Resolve<Driven> and drives a high of the weaker strength Pull: Driven::pull(true).
  • The line's value is the nature's resolve applied to all the drives that are present: the strongest wins, and equal strengths that disagree give x.
  • line.v and line.s read the two fields of the resolved value, like a struct.

The last block is the stimulus, and it is ordinary MSL: the bench owns the two sensor signals, starts them low, and three timed reactions raise door at 1 µs, raise window at 2 µs, and drop both at 3 µs. The monitor writes:

stream Alarm.watch.line
  instant      fields
  (0, 0)       t = 0        level = 1    strength = Pull
  (1 µs, 1)    t = 1.0e-6   level = 0    strength = Strong
  (3 µs, 1)    t = 3.0e-6   level = 1    strength = Pull

Three records, not four. At 2 µs the second driver joins, but the resolved value is still a strong low, so the line did not change and changed() did not fire. That is the question a debug print should answer: not "what did I write" but "what does the wire hold".

The first record is at time zero: the initial values of a design are themselves a change, so reactions on changed() run once at the start.

Run it

Save the file as alarm.msl. The bench Alarm is its one module without ports, so it is the top:

piperine run alarm.msl --analysis transient --stop 4e-6 --stream line

Count the record lines from Alarm.watch: three, at times 0, 1 µs and 3 µs, and none at 2 µs. That is, once a nature can implement Resolve. Today the command refuses the file and runs nothing:

Two continuous natures in one module

Continuous natures compose the same way. A resistor that heats itself has two electrical ports and one thermal port, and its behavior reads one world and writes the other:

/// A resistor whose value follows the temperature rise on `t`.
module HotResistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical, t: Thermal) {
    r0: f64 where r0 > 0.0;
    /// Temperature coefficient, per kelvin.
    tc: f64 = 4m;
}
analog for HotResistor {
    let r = r0 * (1.0 + tc * temp(t));
    i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r;
    pwr(t) +> -v(p, n) * v(p, n) / r;       // the dissipated power flows into `t`
}

/// A thermal resistance from `t` to ambient.
module ThermalR(t: Thermal) { rth: f64 where rth > 0.0; }
analog for ThermalR { pwr(t) +> temp(t) / rth; }

temp(t) is the potential of the thermal node, pwr(t) its flow to the reference. A contribution pwr(t) +> x takes x out of the node, so power into the node is negative. Connect HotResistor and ThermalR to the same thermal node and the solver finds the temperature at which the power in equals the power out.

Bundles

A struct whose fields are all carriers is a bundle: a cable. Directions are written once, from the producer's side, and flip gives the consumer's view. Here is a valid/ready stream and a register slice that sits in the middle of one. It reads rx as a consumer and drives tx as a producer:

struct Stream { out valid: Bit, in ready: Bit, out data: Bit }
impl Stream {
    /// A transfer happens on this clock edge.
    pub fn beat(self, clk: Bit) -> Event { clk.rise() && self.valid && self.ready }
}

module Slice(in clk: Bit, rx: flip Stream, tx: Stream) {
    signal full: Bit = false;
    signal buf: Bit;
    tx.data = buf;
    tx.valid = full;
    rx.ready = !full || tx.ready;
}
digital for Slice {
    on rx.beat(clk) { buf <- rx.data; full <- true; }
    on clk.rise() && !(rx.valid && rx.ready) && tx.ready { full <- false; }
}

For tx, a plain Stream, the slice drives valid and data and reads ready. For rx, flipped, it is the other way around, which is why the body may define rx.ready. A bundle can carry methods too: rx.beat(clk) is an event you can react to.

Analog and digital never touch directly

A Bit is never connected to an Electrical node, and no converter is ever inserted for you. Between modules you place one explicitly: D2A, A2D or Bidir from the standard library. Inside one module, behavior crosses freely: analog reads digital carriers, and reactions probe voltages, as Step and WindowMonitor already did. Chapter 10 is about this.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0220two instances with an out port on the same BitA Bit has one driver. One writer, or declare a nature that implements Resolve for the net.
E0222req <- false; where req is an in portA module cannot drive its own input. Drive it from the module that owns it.
E0310module M(clk: Bit)A one-driver port needs a direction: in clk: Bit. (And an Electrical port takes none.)
E0250Resistor(clk, gnd) { r: 1k } with clk: BitDiscrete and continuous never connect. Place a D2A between them.
E0206temp(p) with p: ElectricalA probe must be one the nature declares. On Electrical they are v and i.
E0240v(p, t) with p: Electrical, t: ThermalA branch joins two nodes of one nature.
E0210signal y: Bit<u8> = count;An initial value is a constant; it cannot read a carrier. For "follows count", write a definition: y = count;

Try it

Replace Pullup(alarm_n) with Pulldown(alarm_n) and predict the stream for the same stimulus. (One record at time zero, level = 0, strength = Pull; then at 1 µs the strength becomes Strong with the level still low, and at 3 µs it returns to Pull. A change of strength is a change.) Then remove the pull entirely: what are level and strength while nobody drives? (z, and HighZ: Driven::Z.)

Chapter 7

Modules and hierarchy

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can build a design out of smaller modules: place instances, bind params, generate a row of stages with a loop, look inside a child from its parent, and write a module that returns a value, such as a filter you can call inside an expression.

A module is ports, params and a netlist of signals and instances, all fixed at elaboration: the moment, before any simulation, when the design is unfolded into its final list of instances. Its behavior over time lives in separate analog for and digital for blocks, in the same file.

The body is a netlist

A module body is not a sequence of statements. It is a list of things that exist. Items can come in any order, and every name is visible in the whole body and in the module's blocks:

ItemExampleWhat it is
paramc: f64 = 1p where c > 0.0;a value the placer binds or the host sets
signalsignal mid: Electrical;a carrier owned by this module
instancelet r1 = Resistor(a, b) { r: 1k };a placed child module
named expressionlet half = r / 2.0;a name for an expression, evaluated where it is read
definitionbusy = st != Tx::Idle;a digital carrier driven from an expression
generationfor k in 0..N { … }, if cond { … }items repeated or chosen at elaboration

Params. A param without a default is required. One of type Option<T> may be left out. A where clause is a check over this param and earlier ones, for example n: f64 = 1.0 where (0.1..=10.0).contains(n);. A param that decides the shape of the design (a loop bound of generation, an array length, a const generic argument) is structural: it is fixed at elaboration, and a host cannot change it during a run.

A body let is not a variable. let half = r / 2.0; stores nothing. It is a name for the expression, and the expression is evaluated wherever the name is read. That is why a body let cannot be assigned: there is nothing to assign.

A complete example: an RC ladder

Build a three-section RC ladder out of the Resistor and Capacitor of chapter 1. The file has three levels of hierarchy: a section, a ladder of N sections, and a top with a source.

module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c > 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }

/// A constant voltage on `p`, referred to ground.
module Vdc(p: Electrical) { dc: f64 = 1.0; }
analog for Vdc { v(p) +> dc; }

/// One section: a series R from `a` to `b`, and a C from `b` to ground.
module RcSection(a: Electrical, b: Electrical) {
    r: f64 = 1k where r > 0.0;
    c: f64 = 1n where c > 0.0;
    let r1 = Resistor(a, b) { r: r };
    let c1 = Capacitor(b, gnd) { c: c };
}

/// `N` sections in a row, from `a` to `b`.
module Ladder(a: Electrical, b: Electrical) where N >= 1 {
    r: f64 = 1k;
    c: f64 = 1n;
    /// tap[0] is the input, tap[N] the output.
    pub signal tap: [Electrical; N + 1];
    let head = Short(a, tap[0]);
    let tail = Short(tap[N], b);
    for k in 0..N {
        pub let sec = RcSection(tap[k], tap[k + 1]) { r, c };
    }
}

module LadderTop() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    pub signal vout: Electrical;
    let src = Vdc(vin) { dc: 1.0 };
    pub let lad = Ladder::<3>(vin, vout) { r: 1k, c: 1n };
}

Take the new constructs one at a time.

  1. Placing an instance. let r1 = Resistor(a, b) { r: r }; has three parts: a name (r1), the connections in port order (a, b), and a param block that binds by name. In { r: r } the left r is the resistor's param and the right one is the section's. When both names are equal you may write just { r, c }, as the ladder does.
  2. A const generic. Ladder<const N: usize> takes the number of sections as a generic argument, and where N >= 1 refuses an empty ladder. The top picks it with Ladder::<3>(…).
  3. An array of nodes. signal tap: [Electrical; N + 1]; declares four nodes here. tap[k] names one of them.
  4. Generation. for k in 0..N { … } in a body is not a loop that runs during simulation. It is unrolled at elaboration: three sections are placed, each an instance of its own, named sec[0], sec[1] and sec[2].
  5. Short. A library module that makes two nodes one. It joins the ports to the two ends of the array.
  6. pub. A parent can reach into a child only through what the child marks pub. The ladder publishes its taps and its sections; it keeps head and tail to itself.

Every instance and carrier of the elaborated design has a path: the name of the top module, then the names of the instances from the top down, joined by dots. The capacitor of the middle section is LadderTop.lad.sec[1].c1, and its param is LadderTop.lad.sec[1].c1.c. A host uses these paths to probe a node or set a param, and a record's stream name starts with the path of the instance that wrote it.

Looking inside from the parent

Because the taps are pub, the top can watch them. Swap the DC source for the Step of chapter 1 and add a block that reports when each tap passes half of the step:

digital for LadderTop {
    for k in 1..4 {
        on cross(v(lad.tap[k]) - 0.5) { emit!("half", stage = k, t = now()); }
    }
}

Generation works in a digital block too: this is three reactions, one per tap. lad.tap[k] goes through the instance lad to its public array. The loop index is an ordinary value, so it can be a field. The stream gets one record per stage, in order, because the edge reaches the taps one after another:

stream LadderTop.half
  fields
  stage = 1   t = …
  stage = 2   t = …
  stage = 3   t = …

The times are left for you; the exercise at the end of the chapter gives an estimate to check them against.

Run it

Save the first listing as ladder.msl. An operating point prints the top's own signals; a DC sweep steps a param, named by its path below the top, and prints one row per point. Today the compiler does not take generic modules yet, so both commands stop at the ladder with this refusal and run nothing:

piperine run ladder.msl --analysis op
piperine run ladder.msl --analysis dc --sweep src.dc --from 0 --to 1 --step 0.5
P0001

  × generic module is not supported yet
    ╭─[ladder.msl:36:19]
 35 │     let src = Vdc(vin) { dc: 1.0 };
 36 │     pub let lad = Ladder::<3>(vin, vout) { r: 1k, c: 1n };
    ·                   ───────────────────┬───────────────────
    ·                                      ╰── generic module is outside the M1 subset
 37 │ }
    ╰────

error: typeck failed with 1 diagnostic(s)

Once it runs: at DC the capacitors are open, so the output equals the input at every point of the sweep. For the half stream, use the version with the step source and run --analysis transient --stop 20e-6 --stream half: three record lines, stage=1 to stage=3, in time order.

Modules that return a value

So far a module was something you wire up. A module may also take value inputs and end its body with a result, declared with -> T. Then you call it inside an expression, and every call is an instance with its own state. This is the tool for "a function with memory".

Here is a first-order low-pass you can apply to any expression:

/// `x` smoothed with time constant `tau`. A module, because it remembers.
module lowpass(x: f64, tau: f64) -> f64 {
    let y = idt((x - y) / tau);
    y
}

/// A buffer whose output follows its input through a 1 µs low-pass.
module SlowBuffer(a: Electrical, b: Electrical) {}
analog for SlowBuffer { v(b) +> lowpass(v(a), 1u); }

Three things happen in lowpass:

  • x: f64 and tau: f64 are value inputs. They are ports whose type is a plain value. At each instance they stand for the expressions the caller wrote, here v(a) and 1u.
  • idt(e) is the time integral of e, itself a library module that returns a value.
  • y reads itself. let y = idt((x - y) / tau); is the differential equation dy/dt = (x − y) / tau, written directly. An instance may read its own result when, as here, a state stands between the read and the write.

The last line, y with no semicolon, is the result. Every operator with memory in the language is built this way: ddt, idt, delay, cross, transition. The standard integrator itself is written in MSL:

pub module idt(x: f64, ic: Option, reset: Option) -> f64 {
    signal y: S;
    potential(y)                             // the result
}

analog for idt {
    let hold = reset.unwrap_or(false) || (ic.is_some() && analysis(Static));
    if hold { potential(y) +> ic.unwrap_or(0.0); } else { flow(y) +> ddt(potential(y)) - x; }
}

A bigger example of the same idea: a PI controller with output limits. Its integral reads its own result to stop winding up when the output saturates.

fn windup(u: f64, lo: f64, hi: f64) -> f64 { u.clamp(lo, hi) - u }

/// PI with output limits and back-calculation anti-windup: the integral reads its own result.
module PiAw(e: VSignal, y: VSignal) {
    kp: f64 = 0.5; ki: f64 = 2k; kt: f64 = 10k;
    lo: f64 = 0.0; hi: f64 = 1.0 where hi > lo;
    let xi = idt(ki * v(e) + kt * windup(kp * v(e) + xi, lo, hi));
}
analog for PiAw { v(y) +> (kp * v(e) + xi).clamp(lo, hi); }

Good to know

Every placement is one instance. let acc = idt(v(a)); names one integrator, and every read of acc is that same integrator. Two calls with equal arguments are two integrators. When you need one state read in two places, name it with a let.

Structure without behavior

Many useful modules have no behavior block at all. Here is a switched-capacitor integrator that is only a netlist of smaller parts. Chapter 8 walks through what the solver does with it.

module RSwitch(a: Electrical, b: Electrical, in ctl: Bit) { ron: f64 = 100.0; roff: f64 = 1G; }
analog for RSwitch { i(a, b) +> v(a, b) / if ctl { ron } else { roff }; }

module OpAmpIdeal(inp: Electrical, inn: Electrical, vout: Electrical) { a0: f64 = 1e5; }
analog for OpAmpIdeal { v(vout) +> a0 * v(inp, inn); }

/// Non-overlapping two-phase clock: pure structure.
module TwoPhase(in clk: Bit, out phi1: Bit = false, out phi2: Bit = false) {
    gap: f64 = 50p;
    phi1 = clk after if clk { gap } else { 0.0 };      // rises gap after clk, falls with it
    phi2 = !clk after if clk { 0.0 } else { gap };     // the mirror
}

/// Stray-insensitive SC integrator: structure only.
module ScIntegrator(vin: Electrical, vout: Electrical, in clk: Bit) {
    cs: f64 = 1p; cf: f64 = 4p;
    signal phi1: Bit;
    signal phi2: Bit;
    signal x: Electrical;
    signal y: Electrical;
    signal vg: Electrical;
    let ck = TwoPhase(clk, phi1, phi2);
    let s1 = RSwitch(vin, x, phi1);
    let s2 = RSwitch(x, gnd, phi2);
    let s3 = RSwitch(y, gnd, phi1);
    let s4 = RSwitch(y, vg, phi2);
    let csmp = Capacitor(x, y) { c: cs };
    let cfb = Capacitor(vg, vout) { c: cf };
    let amp = OpAmpIdeal(gnd, vg, vout);
}

Notice ron: f64 = 100.0, not 100: an integer literal never becomes an f64, while an SI literal like 1G always is one.

A parent may also add its own behavior on a child's public node. A SAR ADC hangs a parasitic capacitor on the output node of its DAC this way: analog for SarAdc { i(dac.core) +> ddt(cpar * v(dac.core)); }.

Templates: modules as values

A module with its params bound is a value, called a template: Name { params }. You can pass it as a param and place it later. That makes cells swappable. A memory array does not need to know which cell it is made of:

/// What a memory cell connects to: a word line and a bit line.
struct Cell { in wl: Bit, bl: Electrical }

/// One-transistor one-capacitor DRAM cell.
module Dram1T1C(c: Cell) {
    cs: f64 = 30f;
    ron: f64 = 5k;
    rleak: f64 = 1T;
    pub signal sn: Electrical = 0.0;     // storage node
}

module MemoryBank(in wl: [Bit; ROWS], bl: [Electrical; COLS]) {
    cell: C;
    for r in 0..ROWS { for c in 0..COLS { pub let cells = cell(Cell { wl: wl[r], bl: bl[c] }); } }
}
let bank = MemoryBank::<_, 64, 32>(wl, bl) { cell: Dram1T1C { cs: 30f } };   // in a parent's body

The param cell: C holds a template, and cell(…) places it. The bound C: Fn(Cell) accepts any module whose ports are one Cell. A function can also return a template, which is how device binning reads: the function picks the params, the caller places the result.

pub fn nch(l: f64, w: f64) -> Bsim4 {
    let vth0 = if l < 0.18u { 0.42 } else if l < 1u { 0.45 } else { 0.47 };
    Bsim4 { l: l, w: w, vth0: vth0 }
}
let m1 = nch(0.13u, 2u)(d, g, s, b);

Multiplicity and extern models

  • m and dtemp. Every instance has these two implicit params, and you bind them like any other: Resistor(a, b) { r: 1k, m: 4.0 } behaves as four resistors in parallel. m multiplies down the hierarchy; dtemp offsets the temperature and adds up.
  • Extern modules. A model compiled to OSDI, or another simulator by cosimulation, is declared with its ports and params and placed like any module:
#[link("bsim4.osdi", model = "bsim4")]
extern "osdi" module Bsim4(d: Electrical, g: Electrical, s: Electrical, b: Electrical) {
    l: f64 = 1u;
    w: f64 = 1u;
    vth0: f64 = 0.4;
}

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0320RcSection(a, b) { res: 1k }The block names no param of the module. It is called r.
E0312RcSection(a) { r: 1k }A port is left unconnected. Connections are positional: give both.
E0343v(lad.head.a), or a tap that is not pubA parent sees only what the child publishes. Mark the item pub in the child.
E0333let x = r1 * 2.0; where r1 is a resistor instanceAn instance without a result is not a value. Read a param or a node, not the instance.
E0903let mut acc = 0.0; in a module bodymut is a reserved word. A body holds no variables: state is a signal, and a name for an expression is a plain let.
E0340for k in 0..count { … } with count a signalGeneration runs at elaboration, so its range is a constant expression: a param or a const generic.
E0330a param c and let c = Capacitor(…)One name, declared twice in one module. Rename the instance: c1.
E0401analog for RcSection { … } in another fileA module and all its behavior blocks live in the same file.
E0304Ladder::<0>(vin, vout)The module's where N >= 1 is false. Ask for at least one section.

Try it

For the ladder with a 1 V step, estimate when each tap passes 0.5 V before you look at the half stream. A rough rule for an RC ladder is 0.69 times the Elmore delay, which here is 3, 5 and 6 times r · c for the three taps: about 2.1, 3.5 and 4.1 µs after the step. How close is the rule? Then change the top to Ladder::<5>. What else must change? (The loop bound in the top's digital block: 1..6.)

Chapter 8

Analog behavior

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can write a nonlinear device, build a diode clipper around it with a sine source, print its waveform and its clipping instants, and use a trace inside analog to see what Newton's method is doing when a circuit will not converge.

An analog block is the equations of a module. To find the voltages of a circuit, the solver guesses them, evaluates every analog block, corrects the guess and repeats: that is Newton's method, and each round is an iteration. So keep one picture in mind for the whole chapter: an analog block is straight-line code that runs at every Newton iteration and remembers nothing between iterations. The past comes only from instances such as ddt and idt, and from digital carriers.

Contributions and probes

A branch is a pair of nodes. A probe reads it: v(a, b) is the voltage from a to b, and i(a, b) the current this module puts through that branch. A contribution writes it, in one of two ways:

  • i(a, b) +> x; adds x to the branch's current. The branch behaves as a current source of value x, and x usually depends on v(a, b): that is a resistor, a diode, a capacitor.
  • v(a, b) +> x; adds x to the branch's voltage. The branch becomes a voltage source, and the solver finds the current it needs.

Contributions add up, which is why the arrow has a plus in it: two lines that contribute to the same branch are two elements in parallel. Leave out the second node and it is gnd.

module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c > 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }      // i = dq/dt

module Inductor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { l: f64 where l > 0.0; }
analog for Inductor  { v(p, n) +> ddt(l * i(p, n)); }      // v = dφ/dt

The two are mirror images. The capacitor contributes a current computed from its voltage. The inductor contributes a voltage computed from its own current: once a branch has a voltage contribution, i(p, n) reads the current the solver found for it.

You never write a derivative of your equations. Everything is differentiated for you, through function calls, value inputs and results, so there is no Jacobian to get wrong.

A complete example: a diode clipper

A 2 V sine drives a resistor into two diodes back to back. The output follows the input while it is small and is clipped near ±0.65 V.

module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

/// A sine voltage source of amplitude `amp` and frequency `f`.
module Vsin(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    amp: f64 = 1.0;
    f: f64 = 1k where f > 0.0;
}
analog for Vsin {
    v(p, n) +> amp * (2.0 * PI * f * now()).sin();
    bound_step(0.02 / f);                    // never fewer than 50 points per period
}

/// Junction diode: i = is · (exp(v / (n · vt)) − 1).
module Diode(a: Electrical, k: Electrical) {
    is: f64 = 1e-14 where is > 0.0;
    n: f64 = 1.0 where n > 0.0;
}
analog for Diode {
    let nvt = n * vt();
    i(a, k) +> is * (limexp(v(a, k) / nvt) - 1.0);
}

module Clipper() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    pub signal vout: Electrical;
    let src = Vsin(vin, gnd) { amp: 2.0, f: 1k };
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let d1 = Diode(vout, gnd);
    let d2 = Diode(gnd, vout);
}

The new pieces, in order:

  1. now() is the simulation time, so the source is a plain function of time. In an operating-point solve now() is 0 and the source sits at 0 V.
  2. bound_step(dt); is a statement only analog has. It caps the next time step. The solver picks steps from the circuit's own dynamics, and a smooth source into a stiff clipper could be stepped over; this line guarantees the sine is sampled finely.
  3. let nvt = n * vt(); A let inside analog is a local of this iteration. vt() is the thermal voltage kT/q at the simulation temperature, about 25.9 mV at the default 300.15 K.
  4. limexp(x) equals x.exp() once the solver has converged, but while it iterates it lets the argument move only a little per iteration. An exponential explodes when Newton overshoots; limexp is the standard cure and costs you nothing in accuracy.
  5. Diode(vout, gnd) binds no params, so both diodes take the defaults.

Printing the waveform: on settle

To see the clipper work you want vin and vout over time. The event for that is settle. It fires once per time point, the times at which the solver has an accepted solution, after everything else at that time has finished. A reaction on settle reads the solution of that time point:

digital for Clipper {
    on settle {
        emit!("wave", t = now(), vin = v(vin), vout = v(vout));
    }
    on cross(v(vout) - 0.6) {
        emit!("clip", t = now(), vin = v(vin));
    }
}

The wave stream is the table you would plot: one record per accepted time point, none for a step the solver tried and threw away. The clip stream answers a sharper question, "when does the clipping start, and how high is the input then?". The solver locates the crossing in time, so the answer does not depend on where the time points happened to fall:

stream Clipper.clip
  instant           fields
  (≈ 59 µs, 1)      t ≈ 5.9e-5    vin ≈ 0.72
  (≈ 1.059 ms, 1)   t ≈ 1.059e-3  vin ≈ 0.72
  …                 once per period

At 0.6 V the diode carries about 0.12 mA, which drops 0.12 V over the resistor, so the input is at 0.72 V: the record confirms the hand calculation.

Run it

Save the clipper with its digital block as clipper.msl. Two periods, the clip records on the terminal, the waveform as a picture:

piperine run clipper.msl --analysis transient --stop 2e-3 --print vin --print vout --stream clip --plot clipper.svg

Look for two clip lines, one per period, each with vin near 0.72. Then open clipper.svg in a browser: the sine and its clipped copy on one axis. To keep the wave records instead of printing them, send every record to a file with --records wave.jsonl, one JSON object per line. The command runs: it prints the two clip records, then the table of the rows, and writes the plot:

Looking inside Newton: emit! in analog

emit! is also allowed directly in an analog block, and there it behaves differently, on purpose. Add one line to the diode:

analog for Diode {
    let nvt = n * vt();
    let id = is * (limexp(v(a, k) / nvt) - 1.0);
    i(a, k) +> id;
    emit!("newton", vd = v(a, k), id = id);
}

This emit! runs when the code around it runs, and analog runs at every evaluation: every Newton iteration of every step, including iterations and whole steps that the solver later rejects. Each record also carries the iteration number. The stream is a trace of the path Newton took:

stream Clipper.d1.newton               one record per evaluation
  time    iteration   fields
  t1      0           vd = …   id = …
  t1      1           vd = …   id = …
  t1      2           vd = …   id = …     converged: the step to t1 is accepted
  t2      0           vd = …   id = …
  t2      1           vd = …   id = …     this step is rejected, and its records stay
  t2'     0           vd = …   id = …     the shorter retry
  …

The piperine command prints the records of reactions; a trace from analog is not among the lines it shows. Where a trace is available, it is exactly what you need when a circuit does not converge: you see the voltage a device was asked to evaluate at each iteration, and whether it is settling, oscillating or running away. It is also why a trace is never used for a result. Keep the two kinds of output apart:

You wantWriteRecords exist for
a waveformon settle { emit!(…) }each accepted time point, once
the moment something happenson cross(…) { emit!(…) }, or any eventeach firing, in accepted steps only
to see why Newton strugglesemit!(…) in analogevery evaluation, rejected ones included

Because a trace depends on the solver's path, the language keeps it away from anything that decides: analog cannot write to the reserved streams sim and checks, so finish() and require(…) are for reactions only. A trace is also skipped in an AC or noise sweep, where analog is linearized rather than executed.

Switching a branch

An if in analog can choose between contributions. This ideal switch is a short when closed and an open circuit when open:

module Switch(a: Electrical, b: Electrical, in ctl: Bit) {}
analog for Switch {
    if ctl { v(a, b) +> 0.0; } else { i(a, b) +> 0.0; }
}

It shows the one firm restriction of the chapter. A branch may change between being a voltage branch and a current branch, but only on something that is constant during a solve: params, digital carriers, now(), analysis(Static). A choice of kind that depends on a probe is an error (E0414), because the solver would have to change the equations while it is solving them. An if on a probe can still choose between expressions: i(a, b) +> v(a, b) * if v(a, b) > 0.0 { g1 } else { g2 }; is fine.

A few more shapes

Two patterns you will meet in other people's models. The first writes an equation rather than an element: a node of a helper nature holds a free unknown, and its flow is the residual the solver drives to zero. The second couples two branches inside one module.

/// An equation is a row: a nature whose "flow" is the residual gives it the right tolerance.
nature Servo {
    potential: Voltage { access: v,   abstol: 1u },
    flow:      Voltage { access: err, abstol: 1u },
}

/// Ideal op-amp (nullor): drives vout so that v(inp, inn) == 0.
module Nullor(inp: Electrical, inn: Electrical, vout: Electrical) { signal x: Servo; }
analog for Nullor {
    err(x) +> v(inp, inn);  // the row of x carries the equation, tested against 1 µV
    v(vout) +> v(x);        // v(x) is the free unknown
}

/// Two coupled inductors: coupling is inside one module, which writes only its own branches.
module Coupled(p1: Electrical, n1: Electrical, p2: Electrical, n2: Electrical) {
    l1: f64; l2: f64; k: f64 where k.abs() <= 1.0;
    let mu = k * (l1 * l2).sqrt();
}
analog for Coupled {
    v(p1, n1) +> ddt(l1 * i(p1, n1) + mu * i(p2, n2));
    v(p2, n2) +> ddt(l2 * i(p2, n2) + mu * i(p1, n1));
}

The operators

These are the tools of the trade. Most are library modules that return a value (chapter 7), so each use is an instance with its own state.

NameWhat it givesKind
ddt(x)time derivativecore module
idt(x, ic, reset), idtmodintegral, wrapped integralstdlib module
delay(x, td)x as it was td agocore module
transition, slewfinite edges, rate limitingstdlib module
laplace_nd …, zi_nd …s-domain and z-domain filtersstdlib module
noise(psd, name), white_noise, flicker_noisenoise sourcescore / stdlib
limit, limexp, pnjlim, fetlimNewton step limitingcore / stdlib
ddx(e, probe), ac_stim(mag, phase)partial derivative, small-signal stimuluscore fn
bound_step(dt)caps the next time stepstatement
temperature(), now(), freq(), analysis(a)the environmentcore fn

One block, every analysis

You do not write separate DC and AC models. One analog block serves Dc, Transient, Ac and Noise. In an equilibrium solve (a DC point, or the operating point every other analysis starts from) ddt is zero, so a capacitor is open and an inductor is a short, with nothing written by you. analysis(Static) is true during such a solve, which lets a model hold a state at its initial condition there, as idt does in chapter 7. For small-signal analyses the block is linearized around the operating point with ddt(x) read as jω·x:

ConstructAt the operating pointIn the AC sweep
ddt(x)0jω·x
idt with an icheld at ic1/jω
delay(x, td)xexp(−jω·td)·x
now()00
a digital carrierits settled valueconstant: no response

To drive an AC sweep, a source adds ac_stim to its contribution: v(p, n) +> dc + ac_stim(1.0, 0.0); is a DC source that becomes a 1 V phasor in the sweep and stays at dc everywhere else.

A noise source is an instance of noise. Read one instance in two places and the two are fully correlated; two instances are independent:

let n = noise(4.0 * K_B * temperature() / r, "thermal");
i(a, mid) +> n;
i(mid, b) +> -n;          // fully correlated

Walkthrough: the switched-capacitor integrator

Go back to ScIntegrator in chapter 7. It has no analog block of its own: all branches belong to its children. Here is what the solver sees.

  1. Operating point. Both clock phases start low, so all four switches are at roff and the capacitors carry no current. y and vg reach ground through s3 and s4; x sits at v(vin)/2 between s1 and s2. No node floats. That is what a finite roff buys.
  2. Sampling, phi1 high. x follows vin and y is grounded, so cs charges to v(vin), with a time constant of ron · cs = 0.1 ns per switch.
  3. Transfer, phi2 high. x is grounded and y joins the virtual ground vg. The charge cs · v(vin) moves onto cf.
  4. Per period. Charge is conserved at vg, so vout[n] = vout[n−1] + (cs/cf) · vin[n]: a non-inverting integrator with gain 0.25.
  5. Time points. Each phase edge changes a switch conductance that analog reads, so the solver lands exactly on all four edges per period. Capacitor charges stay continuous; only the currents step.
  6. DC and AC. DC solves the all-switches-off circuit above, not the sampled system. The AC sweep linearizes with both phases low. The sampled transfer function needs a periodic analysis.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0410vout +> 1.0;The left side must be a probe of a branch: v(vout) +> 1.0;
E0417i(p, n) +> 1;A contribution is an f64. Write 1.0, or an SI literal such as 1m.
E0404held <- v(vin); in analogA commit belongs in a reaction. analog writes only with +>.
E0180if flags { … } in analog, flags a Bit<Logic>A Logic is not a bool. Decide with a function that returns one: if flags.is_high() { … }
E0414if v(a, b) > 0.0 { v(a, b) +> 0.0; } else { i(a, b) +> 0.0; }A branch cannot change kind on a probe. Choose between expressions of one kind, or switch on a digital carrier set by a crossing.
E0241i(a, a) +> x;A branch joins two different nodes.
E0603require("ok", v(a) < 1.0); in analogChecks are decided by reactions, never by a Newton trace. Move it into on settle { … }.
E0503a run error naming a nodeThe solve is singular: typically a node that only capacitors touch, in a DC solve. Give it a DC path (a large resistor) or an initial value.
E0507a run error naming a contributionA non-finite value: a division by a voltage that is zero, or a bare exp that overflowed. Guard the expression, or use limexp.

Try it

Give d2 a different diode: Diode(gnd, vout) { n: 2.0 }. Which half of the output wave is clipped higher now, and at roughly what voltage? (The negative half: an ideality factor of 2 doubles the voltage for the same current, to about 1.3 V.) Add a second crossing reaction on above(-v(vout) - 1.2) to confirm it with a record.

Chapter 9

Digital behavior

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can write clocked logic and predict it edge by edge: a counter, a debounced button and a PWM generator, each with a debug stream that shows its state on every clock edge. You will also know the one rule that makes digital MSL free of races.

A digital block holds reactions. A reaction on ev { body } runs its body once, to completion, each time the event ev fires. Inside it, q <- x; is a commit: it writes this module's drive of q, and the write takes effect a moment later, never during the reaction.

The one rule: reads see the past

Start with a counter that also raises a flag when it is about to wrap. The first line of the reaction is a debug print of the state.

module Counter(in clk: Bit, out count: Bit = 0, out full: Bit = false) {}
digital for Counter {
    on clk.rise() {
        emit!("state", t = now(), count = count, full = full);
        count <- count + 1;
        full  <- count == 254;
    }
}

Why 254 and not 255? Because every read in a reaction sees the values from before the edge, exactly like the D inputs of real flip-flops. On the edge where count reads 254, the first commit schedules 255 and the second schedules full = true; both land together. So full is high exactly while count is 255. The stream shows it:

stream Count.ctr.state               (a 1 MHz clock: edges at 0.5, 1.5, 2.5 µs, …)
  instant         fields
  (0.5 µs, 0)     count = 0     full = false
  (1.5 µs, 0)     count = 1     full = false
  …
  (254.5 µs, 0)   count = 254   full = false     this edge commits 255 and full = true
  (255.5 µs, 0)   count = 255   full = true      this edge commits 0 and full = false
  (256.5 µs, 0)   count = 0     full = false

Each record is the state the reaction found. Put the emit! first, last or in the middle: it prints the same thing, because nothing changes while a reaction runs. This is the habit to build: one emit!("state", …) at the top of a clocked reaction gives you the whole history of the block, one row per edge.

Run it

The counter needs a clock. Put it in a file count.msl with the Osc of chapter 5 and a three-line bench:

module Count() {
    signal clk: Bit;
    signal count: Bit;
    signal full: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk) { f: 1M };
    let ctr = Counter(clk, count, full);
}
piperine run count.msl --analysis transient --stop 257e-6 --print full --records state.jsonl

The 257 state records are in state.jsonl, one object per line, ready for a script, and exactly one of them, the one at 255.5 µs, has full true. A table of the run, with --print-step, shows the same in its Count.full column.

The rules of a microstep

Time in digital MSL advances in microsteps: at one time t, the instants (t, 0), (t, 1), (t, 2), … follow each other until nothing is left to do. A commit made at microstep n applies at microstep n + 1. The second number in every record's instant is this counter. Five rules govern it:

  • Reads see the past. Every read sees the values at the start of the microstep. All the commits of a microstep apply together. That is why statement order inside a reaction rarely matters.
  • The last write wins. When two commits hit the same level in the same microstep, source order decides. All writers of one drive are in one module, so you can see the order in one file.
  • Modules never race. A one-driver carrier has one writer, and a resolved one combines its drives in a fixed order. No result depends on which module the simulator happened to run first.
  • Delays are transport. q <- x after d; applies d seconds later. A new commit from the same statement removes that statement's pending commits due at or after it. p <- true; p <- false after w; is a pulse of width w, in either order.
  • A reaction never waits. There is no wait, no blocking write and no #0. A sequence over time is visible state, such as a counter.

Definitions and commits

A module drives a carrier in one of two ways, never both: with one = definition in the body, or with commits from its reactions. Use a definition for combinational logic and delays, and commits for state. A definition is recomputed in the same microstep its inputs change, so it never lags. A set-reset latch needs no reaction at all:

/// Cross-coupled NOR gates with gate delay: pure structure.
module SrLatch(in s: Bit, in r: Bit, out q: Bit = false, out qn: Bit = true) {
    tpd: f64 = 10p where tpd > 0.0;
    q  = !(r | qn) after tpd;
    qn = !(s | q)  after tpd;
}

Without the after, the two definitions would form a loop with no delay in it, which is an elaboration error (E0339).

Events

The head of a reaction is an event. These are the ones you will use:

EventFires when
s.changed()s changed in this microstep
clk.rise(), clk.fall()an IEEE 1364 posedge / negedge (stdlib)
at(t)time reaches t
cross(e)e goes from ≤ 0 to > 0, located by the solver
above(e)like cross, and also at time 0 if e already starts above (stdlib)
every(t0, period), init()periodic times; the start (stdlib)
settleonce per time point, after the instant has gone quiet

Events combine with a || b (either fires) and ev && c, where c is a bool guard: the reaction runs when ev fires and c is true at that moment. A plain bool alone is not an event (E0430): on enable { … } has no "when". Inside a reaction, an event used as a bool tells you whether it fired in this microstep:

/// Three-state phase detector: two events in one reaction, each tested as a bool.
module Pfd3(in fref: Bit, in fvco: Bit, out up: Bit, out dn: Bit) {
    signal state: Bit = 0;
    up = state == 1;
    dn = state == -1;
}
digital for Pfd3 {
    on fref.rise() || fvco.rise() {
        let s = state;
        if fref.rise() && s < 1 { s += 1; }    // which one fired?
        if fvco.rise() && s > -1 { s -= 1; }   // both at once: up first, then down
        state <- s;
    }
}

Inside a reaction, a let local may be assigned, and it lives for one run of the body. Only carriers follow the "reads see the past" rule.

A complete example: a debounced button

A mechanical button bounces for a few milliseconds. The classic fix samples it with a slow clock and accepts a new level only after it has been seen on several consecutive samples.

/// A free-running clock.
module Osc(out clk: Bit = false) { f: f64 = 1M where f > 0.0; }
digital for Osc { on clk.changed() { clk <- !clk after 0.5 / f; } }

/// `clean` follows `raw` once `raw` has differed from it on `n` consecutive clock edges.
module Debounce(in clk: Bit, in raw: Bit, out clean: Bit = false) {
    n: u8 = 4 where n >= 1;
    /// Consecutive samples that differ from `clean`.
    signal run: Bit = 0;
}
digital for Debounce {
    on clk.rise() {
        emit!("state", t = now(), raw = raw, run = run, clean = clean);
        let level = raw;                        // the button, a plain bool: high or low
        if level == clean {
            run <- 0;
        } else if run + 1 >= n {
            clean <- level;
            run <- 0;
            emit!("flip", t = now(), to = level);
        } else {
            run <- run + 1;
        }
    }
}

/// The bench: a clock, the debouncer, and a button that bounces.
module Press() {
    signal clk: Bit;
    signal button: Bit = false;
    pub signal pressed: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk) { f: 1k };
    let db = Debounce(clk, button, pressed) { n: 4 };
}
digital for Press {
    on at(2.0m) { button <- true; }
    on at(2.2m) { button <- false; }
    on at(2.4m) { button <- true; }
}

Read the reaction as a hardware designer would read RTL:

  • signal run: Bit<u8> = 0; is the state register: how many samples in a row disagreed with the output.
  • let level = raw; reads the button as what it is, a bool: high or low. There is no third state to decide on, so the reaction compares it with clean and nothing else.
  • The three arms are: agreement resets the run; the n-th disagreement flips the output; any other disagreement extends the run. Each arm commits run exactly once, so there is nothing for "last write wins" to decide.
  • Two streams. state is the row-per-edge debug stream. flip records only the interesting moments. Keeping them apart lets a host listen to flip alone.

The bench plays the finger. Its three timed reactions are the stimulus: the button starts low, bounces high at 2.0 ms, low at 2.2 ms, and high for good at 2.4 ms. Three reactions may commit to the same signal because they are in the same module. The clock is 1 kHz, so it samples at 0.5, 1.5, 2.5 ms and so on, and never sees the bounce:

stream Press.db.state
  instant        fields
  (0.5 ms, 0)    raw = false   run = 0   clean = false
  (1.5 ms, 0)    raw = false   run = 0   clean = false
  (2.5 ms, 0)    raw = true    run = 0   clean = false
  (3.5 ms, 0)    raw = true    run = 1   clean = false
  (4.5 ms, 0)    raw = true    run = 2   clean = false
  (5.5 ms, 0)    raw = true    run = 3   clean = false     the fourth sample: this edge flips
  (6.5 ms, 0)    raw = true    run = 0   clean = true

stream Press.db.flip
  (5.5 ms, 0)    t = 5.5e-3   to = true

The run column lags by one edge, as it must: each row is the state the reaction found. When you debug a state machine this way, read a row as "given this state and these inputs, the block decided…" and look for the decision in the next row.

Run it

Save the file as debounce.msl and ask for the interesting stream only. One thing in it is not compiled yet, a param of an integer type (n: u8), so today the command refuses the file there and runs nothing:

piperine run debounce.msl --analysis transient --stop 7e-3 --stream flip
P0001

  × param of type `u8` is not supported yet
   ╭─[debounce.msl:7:8]
 6 │ module Debounce(in clk: Bit, in raw: Bit, out clean: Bit = false) {
 7 │     n: u8 = 4 where n >= 1;
   ·        ─┬
   ·         ╰── param of type `u8` is outside the M1 subset
 8 │     /// Consecutive samples that differ from `clean`.
   ╰────

error: typeck failed with 1 diagnostic(s)

Once it runs, it prints one record line: the output flipped once, at the fourth clean sample, @5.500000e-3 Press.db flip t=5.500000e-3 to=true. Run it again with --stream state to get the row-per-edge table above as seven record lines.

A complete example: a PWM generator

A counter that wraps every period clocks, and an output that is high while the count is below a duty word. Two details make it a good PWM: the duty word is latched once per cycle, so a change never splits a pulse, and the output is a register, so it never glitches.

/// A free-running clock.
module Osc(out clk: Bit = false) { f: f64 = 1M where f > 0.0; }
digital for Osc { on clk.changed() { clk <- !clk after 0.5 / f; } }

/// Counter-based PWM: `period` clocks per cycle, high for `duty` of them.
module Pwm(in clk: Bit, in duty: Bit, out pwm: Bit = false, out wrap: Bit) {
    period: u8 = 100 where period >= 2;
    signal count: Bit = 0;
    /// The duty word in use for this cycle.
    signal width: Bit = 0;
    wrap = count == 0;                          // a definition: high during the first count
}
digital for Pwm {
    on clk.rise() {
        let last = count == period - 1;
        let next = if last { 0 } else { count + 1 };
        let w = if last { duty } else { width };    // latch at the wrap
        count <- next;
        width <- w;
        pwm <- next < w;
        if last { emit!("cycle", t = now(), duty = w); }
    }
}

/// The bench: 25 % duty, changed to 60 % in the middle of the third cycle.
module PwmBench() {
    signal clk: Bit;
    signal duty: Bit = 25;
    pub signal pwm: Bit;
    signal wrap: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk) { f: 1M };
    let pulse = Pwm(clk, duty, pwm, wrap) { period: 100 };
}
digital for PwmBench {
    on at(250u) { duty <- 60; }
}
