The pattern in this reaction is worth copying. First compute everything the next state needs into locals (last, next, w), from the current state. Then commit. Because the output is computed from next and w, not from count and width, pwm changes in the same microstep as the counter it belongs to.

wrap is a definition, so it has no initial value of its own: it is always count == 0. With a 1 MHz clock the PWM runs at 10 kHz, and the cycle stream has one record per period carrying the duty word that period will use. The bench changes duty from 25 to 60 at 250 µs, in the middle of a cycle, and the stream shows the change taking effect at the next wrap, never earlier.

Run it

Save the file as pwm.msl; it carries its own clock. Its period is a param of an integer type, which the compiler does not take yet, so today the command refuses the file and runs nothing:

piperine run pwm.msl --analysis transient --stop 450e-6 --stream cycle
P0001

  × param of type `u8` is not supported yet
   ╭─[pwm.msl:7:13]
 6 │ module Pwm(in clk: Bit, in duty: Bit<u8>, out pwm: Bit = false, out wrap: Bit) {
 7 │     period: u8 = 100 where period >= 2;
   ·             ─┬
   ·              ╰── param of type `u8` is outside the M1 subset
 8 │     signal count: Bit<u8> = 0;
   ╰────

error: typeck failed with 1 diagnostic(s)

Once it runs, the cycle records come at 99.5, 199.5, 299.5 and 399.5 µs, with duty=25 twice and then duty=60: the change made at 250 µs shows up in the record at 299.5 µs, the next wrap. Add --plot pwm.svg to see the pulses widen: a digital signal is drawn as a step trace in a lane of its own.

From print to check

Once a debug print has shown you what "right" looks like, turn it into a check. The library's require(name, pass) is a function over emit! that writes a record to the reserved checks stream; the host reports each one as a pass or a failure, and there is no third verdict. finish() writes to the reserved sim stream and ends the analysis at that instant.

/// Checks a PWM from outside: the duty in use never exceeds a limit, and stops after `cycles`.
module PwmCheck(in clk: Bit, in wrap: Bit, in pwm: Bit) {
    limit: u8 = 90;
    cycles: u32 = 20;
    signal high: Bit = 0;       // high clocks seen in this cycle
    signal seen: Bit = 0;      // cycles completed
}
digital for PwmCheck {
    on clk.rise() {
        if wrap {
            require("duty_limit", high <= limit);
            high <- if pwm { 1 } else { 0 };
            seen <- seen + 1;
            if seen + 1 >= cycles { finish(); }
        } else if pwm {
            high <- high + 1;
        }
    }
}

Place it beside the generator in PwmBench with let chk = PwmCheck(clk, wrap, pwm);. A check is an ordinary reaction: it runs when its event fires and reads what any reaction reads. The library also has ready-made monitors built this way, such as Never(clk, bad) and Setup(clk, d) { ts: 100p }; chapter 13 lists them.

Run it

With the check placed in the bench, piperine run pwm.msl --analysis transient --stop 3e-3 prints each failing checks record as it is written (add --all-checks to see the passing ones too) and stops by itself at the twentieth cycle. In a project, give the run a table in Piperine.toml and let one command judge it:

piperine test

It runs every run of the project and prints one PASS or FAIL line each, with the failing check records under a FAIL.

Many drivers: resolved nets

Open-drain lines, tristate buses and wired logic are not language features, and the library does not declare a net for them. A model declares the nature of its net and gives it the capability of several drivers, an impl Resolve (chapter 6). The resolve function takes the drives that are present, as plain values, and returns the value the net holds. A clocked reader of such a line looks like any other reaction; it reads the resolved value:

pub nature IrqLine { value: Driven }

impl Resolve for IrqLine {
    fn resolve(drives: [Driven; N]) -> Driven { Driven::strongest(drives) }
}

module IrqSource(in req: Bit, irq_n: IrqLine) { irq_n = if req { Driven::strong(false) } else { Driven::Z }; }

module IrqController(in clk: Bit, irq_n: IrqLine, out pending: Bit = false) {}
digital for IrqController { on clk.rise() { pending <- irq_n.v.is_low(); } }

module Board(in clk: Bit, in req0: Bit, in req1: Bit, out pending: Bit) {
    signal irq_n: IrqLine;
    let pu = Pullup(irq_n);                  // wins only while every source is released
    let s0 = IrqSource(req0, irq_n);
    let s1 = IrqSource(req1, irq_n);
    let ic = IrqController(clk, irq_n, pending);
}

Driving with strength

Four nets show what a nature can say. Each has its nature, its impl and the module that drives it, and a table of what the net holds. The first two are the ones to learn first.

1. A plain bit into a wired-AND net. Nothing here needs a strength or a Logic. A module drives a bool, and the nature says that a driver that is missing reads high, which is the pull-up:

pub nature AndWire { value: bool }

impl Resolve for AndWire {
    fn resolve(drives: [bool; N]) -> bool { drives.iter().all(|d| d) }
}

module IrqSource(irq_n: AndWire, in pending: Bit) { irq_n = !pending; }   // drives a plain bool

module Board(in a: Bit, in b: Bit) {
    signal irq_n: AndWire;                 // no value of its own: it is resolve of its drives
    let s0 = IrqSource(irq_n, a);
    let s1 = IrqSource(irq_n, b);
}
abirq_n
falsefalsetrue
truefalsefalse
truetruefalse
no source at alltrue

irq_n is low when either source pulls it low and high when both release it or nobody drives it. A third module that drove a Logic into it is error E0180: AndWire implements Resolve for bool only, and a drive picks its impl by the type of the value it drives.

2. An open-drain line with a pull-up. Now the net holds a level and a strength. The pull-up is a module of the library, and it works with any net whose nature implements Resolve<Driven>:

pub nature Sda { value: Driven }

impl Resolve for Sda {
    fn resolve(drives: [Driven; N]) -> Driven { Driven::strongest(drives) }
}

module OpenDrainPin(sda: Sda, in pull_low: Bit) {
    sda = if pull_low { Driven::strong(false) } else { Driven::Z };
}

module I2cBus(in m: Bit, in s: Bit) {
    signal sda: Sda;
    let master = OpenDrainPin(sda, m);
    let slave = OpenDrainPin(sda, s);
    let rp = Pullup(sda);                  // Driven::pull(true)
}
masterslaverpsda
ZZpull 11 at Pull
strong 0Zpull 10 at Strong
strong 0strong 0pull 10 at Strong
ZZ(no Pullup instance)Driven::Z: sda.v.is_z()

A pull wins only while every stronger drive is released: Strong beats Pull. Without the pull-up the line, left alone, reads Z, and a reader that decides on it says how, with sda.v.is_z() or sda.v.to_volts(vdd), which gives half of vdd for Z. The five strengths, HighZ below Weak, Pull, Strong and Supply, are the smallest set under which every module of the library keeps its meaning; a model that needs more declares its own enum and nature.

3. A tri-state bus with two drivers and a keeper. The same nature serves a bus. The Keeper of the library remembers the last level that was driven and holds it weakly, so a released bus does not float:

pub nature TriBus { value: Driven }

impl Resolve for TriBus {
    fn resolve(drives: [Driven; N]) -> Driven { Driven::strongest(drives) }
}

module Bus(in d0: Bit, in oe0: Bit, in d1: Bit, in oe1: Bit) {
    signal line: TriBus;
    let a = Tribuf(line, d0, oe0);
    let b = Tribuf(line, d1, oe1);
    let k = Keeper(line);
}

With one buffer enabled the line is its level at Strong. With none, the keeper's Weak level holds the last one. Before any buffer has driven, the keeper's memory is x, and the line reads x at Weak: the model says it was never driven, which a bool could not.

4. A conflict is a value; an instability is an error. Enable both buffers of the bus with d0 true and d1 false: two Strong drives that disagree. Driven::strongest gives x at Strong, a reader sees line.v.is_x(), line.v.to_bit() is None, and nothing stops. That is a conflict, and it is a value like any other. An instability is a different thing: a loop of drives through others(), with no reaction and no after in it, that never stops changing, such as two modules that each drive the inverse of what the other drives. After its bounded passes the run stops with error E0511, naming the two drives.

The result of resolve need not have the type that is driven. A bus that allows one driver at a time drives an Option and holds a type of its own, which is why the nature takes two types, Resolve<X, V>, the type driven and the type held:

#[derive(Repr, PartialEq)]
pub enum Data { Free, Byte(u8), Contended }

pub nature DataBus { value: Data }

impl Resolve, Data> for DataBus {
    fn resolve(drives: [Option; N]) -> Data {
        drives.iter().fold(Data::Free, |a, x| match (a, x) {
            (y, None) => y,
            (Data::Free, Some(b)) => Data::Byte(b),
            _ => Data::Contended,
        })
    }
}

Each drive of a net has one type, and the nature picks its impl by that type: two modules that drove different types into one DataBus would be error E0225, naming both.

Walkthrough: a flip-flop with async reset

module Dff(in clk: Bit, in rst_n: Bit, in d: Bit, out q: Bit) { reset: T; }
digital for Dff {
    on clk.rise() || rst_n.fall() { if !rst_n { q <- reset; } else { q <- d; } }
}
  1. Drives. Dff holds the one drive of q, by commits. q has no initial value, so it starts at Default::default() of T, which is why T is bound by Default.
  2. Events. If the clock rises and reset falls together, the reaction still runs once.
  3. Reset low from the start. A rst_n that starts low has not fallen: fall() is a change that ends low, and the initial value is where the signal begins. A model that must reset at the start adds init() to the event list.
  4. A clock edge. The reaction reads rst_n and d as settled in that microstep; the commit applies in the next.
  5. One path. if !rst_n takes exactly one arm, because rst_n is a bool. A reset that may be x or z is a Bit<Logic>, and the model says how to read it, for example with rst_n.is_low().

Pipelines fall out of "reads see the past". One reaction can describe all three stages of a multiply-accumulate pipeline, in any statement order, because every right-hand side reads the registers as they were before the edge:

module MacPipe(in clk: Bit, in a: Bit, in b: Bit, in valid_in: Bit,
               out acc: Bit = 0, out valid_out: Bit = false) {
    signal a1: Bit = 0;
    signal b1: Bit = 0;
    signal v1: Bit = false;
    signal p2: Bit = 0;
    signal v2: Bit = false;
}

digital for MacPipe {
    on clk.rise() {
        a1 <- a; b1 <- b; v1 <- valid_in;                    // stage 1
        p2 <- (a1 as i32) * (b1 as i32); v2 <- v1;           // stage 2
        if v2 { acc <- acc + p2 as i40; } valid_out <- v2;   // stage 3
    }
}

Status

Digital MSL runs from source, and the piperine command runs it. A digital block is type checked, elaborated and compiled to Rust (chapter 20); its reactions, commits, events and emit! records run on the solver and are tested against the rules above. One form of this chapter is not compiled yet and is refused with P0001: a param of an integer type, such as n: u8. That stops the debouncer and the PWM for now, as their "Run it" boxes show; the counter runs. A transient of a design with no analog node runs to its stop like any other and gives its table.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0430on clk { … }A Bit is a level, not an event. Say which change: on clk.rise() { … }
E0221wrap = count == 0; and also wrap <- true;One carrier, one way of driving it. Keep the definition or the commits.
E0338out wrap: Bit = false with wrap = count == 0;A carrier driven by an undelayed definition has no initial value: the definition is its value.
E0339q = !(r | qn); qn = !(s | q);A loop of definitions with no delay. Break it with after.
E0432count <- count + 1.0;The committed type must be the carrier's. count is a u8: commit count + 1.
E0434count <- 0; directly in digital for M { … }A digital block holds only reactions. For a start value use = 0 on the signal, or on init() { … }.
E0350on clk.rise() { let e = above(v(a)); … }An instance cannot be placed inside a reaction. Name it in the body, let e = above(v(a));, and read e.
E0501on clk.changed() { clk <- !clk; }A run error: with no delay the microsteps at one time never go quiet. Add after 0.5 / f.

Try it

Change the bench of the debouncer so that button goes high at 2.0 ms and low again at 4.0 ms. Write down the run column of the state stream up to 6.5 ms, and say whether a flip record appears. (run reads 0, 0, 0, 1, 2, 0, 0: the samples at 2.5 and 3.5 ms extend the run, the low sample at 4.5 ms resets it. No flip.) Then make Pwm emit the time of each falling edge of its output, using a second reaction on pwm.fall().

Chapter 10

Mixed-signal

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can connect the two worlds in both directions: a comparator with hysteresis that feeds a counter, and a small closed loop, a buck converter whose digital controller samples an analog output and steers a power stage. You will be able to say, for any record in your debug streams, which analog solution it saw.

Mixed-signal in MSL is not a separate sublanguage. One module can have an analog block and a digital block, and they share the module's names. You have done it since chapter 1: Step had both. Two rules connect the worlds:

  • Analog reads digital directly, and a Logic through a function such as to_volts(vdd) or is_high(). A digital carrier is a constant of each solve. When it changes, the analog side sees a jump and is solved again.
  • Digital reads analog by probing in a reaction, or by an event on an analog expression: above(v(vin) - vth). A crossing is located in time by the solver.

A complete example: counting the cycles of a sine

Turn a sine wave into pulses and count them. The analog-to-digital step is a comparator with hysteresis: it switches high above one threshold and low below a lower one, so a noisy input that lingers near the threshold cannot make it chatter. The library has it as A2D, and its whole source is four lines:

module A2D(a: Electrical, out d: Bit = false) { vth: f64 = 0.6; hyst: f64 = 50m; }
digital for A2D {
    on above(v(a) - vth - hyst / 2.0) { d <- true; }
    on above(vth - hyst / 2.0 - v(a)) { d <- false; }
}

Two reactions, each on a crossing: the first fires when v(a) rises through vth + hyst/2, the second when it falls through vth − hyst/2. Here is the complete design around it:

/// A sine voltage source.
module Vsin(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { amp: f64 = 1.0; f: f64 = 1k where f > 0.0; }
analog for Vsin {
    v(p, n) +> amp * (2.0 * PI * f * now()).sin();
    bound_step(0.02 / f);
}

/// Counts rising edges of `pulse`.
module EdgeCounter(in pulse: Bit, out count: Bit = 0) {}
digital for EdgeCounter {
    on pulse.rise() {
        count <- count + 1;
        emit!("pulse", t = now(), n = count + 1);
    }
}

module CycleCounter() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal hit: Bit;
    pub signal count: Bit;
    let src = Vsin(vin, gnd) { amp: 1.0, f: 1k };
    let cmp = A2D(vin, hit) { vth: 0.0, hyst: 0.2 };
    let ctr = EdgeCounter(hit, count);
}

The node vin goes to the converter's electrical port; the converter's Bit output goes to the counter. Nothing else connects the two worlds. With a threshold of 0 V and 0.2 V of hysteresis, the comparator goes high when the sine rises through +0.1 V, which first happens 15.9 µs into each period:

stream CycleCounter.ctr.pulse
  instant            fields
  (15.9 µs, 2)       t ≈ 1.59e-5    n = 1
  (1.0159 ms, 2)     t ≈ 1.0159e-3  n = 2
  (2.0159 ms, 2)     t ≈ 2.0159e-3  n = 3

The times are not multiples of the solver's step: each one is the located crossing. And the microstep is 2, not 0. That number is the story of the instant, which is the next section.

Run it

Save the design, with the comparator above it, as cycles.msl:

piperine run cycles.msl --analysis transient --stop 3e-3 --print-step 1e-3 --print count --stream pulse

It prints three record lines from CycleCounter.ctr, with n=1, n=2 and n=3, about 15.9 µs into each millisecond. --print-step 1e-3 keeps the table to one row per millisecond, and --print count to the one signal that matters, so its last row shows CycleCounter.count at 3. The record times are the located crossings, not rows of the table, and bound_step keeps every step of the sine at most 20 µs:

run cycles: transient, top CycleCounter, backend rust
@1.594214e-5 CycleCounter.ctr pulse t=1.594214e-5 n=1
@1.015942e-3 CycleCounter.ctr pulse t=1.015942e-3 n=2
@2.015942e-3 CycleCounter.ctr pulse t=2.015942e-3 n=3

time         CycleCounter.count
0.000000e0   0
1.000000e-3  1
2.000000e-3  2
3.000000e-3  3

The instant

Time in MSL is a pair (t, n): a real time and a microstep. At one time, microsteps run until nothing is pending. For a time the solver lands on, the analog side is solved once, the first time the digital side goes quiet; a change made after that is seen by the next step.

flowchart LR
  A["land on time t
collect what is due"] --> B["microstep n
commits apply,
definitions settle,
reactions run"]
  B --> C{"anything pending
at n + 1?"}
  C -- "yes" --> B
  C -- "no, first time" --> J["solve analog at t,
fire located crossings"]
  J --> C
  C -- "no, already solved" --> S["settle reactions,
then next time point"]

Follow the first pulse of the cycle counter through that loop:

InstantWhat happens
15.9 µsThe solver, stepping along the sine, finds that v(a) − 0.1 changes sign and lands on the crossing.
(15.9 µs, 0)Nothing digital is due. The analog side is solved at this time. The crossing fires for the next microstep.
(15.9 µs, 1)A2D's first reaction runs and commits d <- true.
(15.9 µs, 2)The commit applies: hit rises. EdgeCounter's reaction runs, emits the record and commits count.
(15.9 µs, 3)count becomes 1. Nobody reacts to it, nothing is pending: the loop ends.

All four microsteps happen at the same time t. The record's (15.9 µs, 2) tells you it was written two hops after the crossing: one for the comparator, one for the counter.

Which analog value does a reaction read?

This is the question behind every mixed-signal debug print, and the answer has two cases.

The reaction runs because of…A probe in it reads…Think of it as…
a digital event at a scheduled time: a clock edge, at, a delayed committhe last accepted solution, from just before this timea sampler: it captures the value the edge found
a located crossing, or anything that follows it in later microstepsthe solution at this timea comparator: the expression has just crossed
settlethe solution at this time, with the final digital statean oscilloscope: one sample per time point

The first row is why a flip-flop that samples a comparator output on the same instant gets the old value, as hardware does. A reaction that probes also makes its time a time point: the solver lands on it exactly instead of stepping across it. The opposite also holds: a change to a digital carrier that analog does not read is not a jump, and the analog solution is not recomputed for it. Falling clock edges that nothing listens to cost the solver nothing.

A comparator without hysteresis

When you want the plain comparator, name the two crossings in the body and react to either:

/// Comparator, right from the operating point: the event says which way it went.
module Comparator(inp: Electrical, inn: Electrical, out q: Bit = false) {
    ttol: f64 = 1p;
    let up = above(v(inp, inn), ttol);
    let dn = above(-v(inp, inn), ttol);
}
digital for Comparator { on up || dn { q <- up; } }

let up = above(…); places one crossing detector and names it. In the head, up || dn fires on either; in the body, up read as a bool says whether it was the rising one. ttol is how precisely the solver must locate the crossing in time.

Between modules: converters

Across a module boundary, a Bit and an Electrical never connect. You place a converter from the standard library, and so the electrical details of every crossing are a visible, named instance with params: A2D is the threshold with hysteresis above; D2A drives a level through an output resistance with finite edges; Bidir is a pad on a net whose nature implements Resolve<Driven>.

module D2A(in d: Bit, a: Electrical, vdd: Electrical) {
    rout: f64 = 100.0; tr: f64 = 20p;
    let ramp = transition(if d { 1.0 } else { 0.0 }, 0.0, tr, tr);
}
analog for D2A { i(a) +> (v(a) - ramp * v(vdd)) / rout; }

Look at what it reads: d is a bool, so the output goes to the supply or to ground. A converter for a Logic input makes the other choice in its own text, d.to_volts(1.0), which gives half the supply for x and z: the model says what "not determined" means electrically, and the language does not guess. transition turns the stepped level into a ramp of duration tr, so the output has a real edge.

A complete example: a buck converter's control loop

Now close a loop through both worlds. A buck converter steps 12 V down to 3.3 V by switching: a high-side switch connects an inductor to the input for a fraction d of each period, and the output settles near d · 12 V. A digital controller measures the output once per period and adjusts d. This is a simplified version of a real digital controller: one integrator instead of a full compensator, and a pulse timed directly instead of a counter-based PWM.

module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c > 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }

module Inductor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { l: f64 where l > 0.0; }
analog for Inductor { v(p, n) +> ddt(l * i(p, n)); }

module Vdc(p: Electrical) { dc: f64 = 1.0; }
analog for Vdc { v(p) +> dc; }

/// A switch: `ron` while `ctl` is high, `roff` otherwise.
module RSwitch(a: Electrical, b: Electrical, in ctl: Bit) { ron: f64 = 50m; roff: f64 = 1M; }
analog for RSwitch { i(a, b) +> v(a, b) / if ctl { ron } else { roff }; }

/// Half bridge and LC filter. `hs` high connects the switch node to `vin`, low to ground.
module PowerStage(vin: Electrical, vout: Electrical, in hs: Bit) {
    l: f64 = 22u;
    c: f64 = 100u;
    pub signal sw: Electrical;
    let high = RSwitch(vin, sw, hs);
    let low = RSwitch(sw, gnd, !hs);
    let ind = Inductor(sw, vout) { l };
    let cap = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c };
}

/// The controller. Once per switching period it samples `vout`, integrates the error
/// into the duty cycle and starts a high-side pulse of that width.
module BuckControl(vout: Electrical, out hs: Bit = false) {
    vref: f64 = 3.3;
    fsw: f64 = 100k where fsw > 0.0;
    /// Integral gain: change of duty per volt of error, per period.
    ki: f64 = 1m where ki > 0.0;
    signal duty: Bit = 0.0;
    let tick = every(0.0, 1.0 / fsw);
}
digital for BuckControl {
    on tick {
        let err = vref - v(vout);
        let d = (duty + ki * err).clamp(0.0, 0.9);
        duty <- d;
        hs <- true;
        hs <- false after d / fsw;
        emit!("loop", t = now(), vout = v(vout), err = err, duty = d);
    }
}

module Buck() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    pub signal vout: Electrical;
    signal hs: Bit;
    let supply = Vdc(vin) { dc: 12.0 };
    pub let power = PowerStage(vin, vout, hs);
    let load = Resistor(vout, gnd) { r: 3.3 };
    let ctl = BuckControl(vout, hs);
}

The power stage is a netlist you can already read, with one new trick: RSwitch(sw, gnd, !hs) passes an expression to an in port. The low-side switch is driven by the complement of the high-side command, with no extra signal.

The controller is where the two worlds meet. Line by line:

  1. let tick = every(0.0, 1.0 / fsw); places a periodic event: time 0, then every 10 µs. It is an instance, so it is named in the body and read in the reaction's head.
  2. let err = vref - v(vout); is the analog-to-digital crossing: a probe inside a reaction. The tick is a scheduled time, so by the table above the probe reads the output as it was just before the tick. That is a sample-and-hold, written in one expression.
  3. let d = (duty + ki * err).clamp(0.0, 0.9); is the integrator: the old duty plus a small step in the direction of the error, limited to a safe range. duty is a Bit<f64>: a real number that changes only at events.
  4. hs <- true; hs <- false after d / fsw; is the pulse. Two commits from two statements: one raises the command at the next microstep, the other lowers it d periods later. This is the digital-to-analog crossing: RSwitch reads hs in its analog block, so each edge is a jump and the solver lands on it.
  5. emit!("loop", …) is the window into the loop: one record per period with the sample, the error and the duty just decided. duty = d prints the new value; duty = duty would print the old one.

Run a transient and the loop stream is the start-up of the converter, period by period:

stream Buck.ctl.loop
  instant         fields
  (0, 1)          t = 0        vout = 0.000   err = 3.300   duty = 0.0033
  (10 µs, 0)      t = 1.0e-5   vout ≈ 11.56   err ≈ -8.26   duty = 0
  (20 µs, 0)      t = 2.0e-5   vout ≈ 10.79   err ≈ -7.49   duty = 0
  …                            the output falls, the duty waits at 0
  (70 µs, 0)      t = 7.0e-5   vout ≈ 2.00    err ≈ 1.30    duty ≈ 0.0013
  …                            the duty climbs, the output follows
  (≈ 7 ms, 0)                  vout ≈ 3.300   err ≈ 0.000   duty ≈ 0.28

The first line is the only one that reads zero, and that is the sample before the first tick acts. The tick at time 0 fires inside the operating point, the solve that finds where the circuit rests before the transient starts, so its hs <- true is already applied when that solve ends. It is what a SPICE .op gives with the switch on: the output rests near 11.8 V, not at 0, and the transient starts from there. The next sample reads 11.56 V, the error is negative, and the clamp holds the duty at 0 while the output falls through the filter. Once it drops under 3.3 V the error turns positive and each period adds ki · err to the duty. At the end the error is zero and the duty sits where 12 V times the duty, less the small drop in the switch, equals 3.3 V: about 0.28. What happens in between, how fast the loop settles and whether it rings, is what the stream is for. Plot vout against t from the records and you have the loop's step response without touching a waveform viewer.

With the print in place, the acceptance test is one more reaction in the top. It checks, at every time point after start-up, that the output stays within 50 mV of the target:

digital for Buck {
    on settle {
        if now() > 10m { require("regulated", (v(vout) - 3.3).abs() < 50m); }
    }
}

Run it

Save the converter, with the digital for Buck check, as buck.msl. Its switch reads the bit ctl in analog directly, which the front end still refuses with error E0150, as the listing at the end of this box shows; when the read is accepted, the commands below run. First watch the loop start, one record per switching period:

piperine run buck.msl --analysis transient --stop 12e-3 --print vout --print-step 1e-3 --stream loop

Read the duty field down the lines: it stays at 0 while the output falls from the 11.8 V of the operating point, then climbs and settles near 0.28, while the Buck.vout column of the table starts at 11.8 and falls to 3.3. Then draw it, and keep the records for a script:

piperine run buck.msl --analysis transient --stop 12e-3 --print vout --plot startup.svg --records loop.jsonl

The plot draws the rows of the run. This command has no --print-step, so there is a row for every accepted step and the switching ripple is visible on vout; add --print-step 1e-3 and the plot would draw only the table's rows, one per millisecond. With the check in the file, every time point after 10 ms is tested, and the run completes with every regulated record passing. Leave the digital for Buck block out and the converter runs the same, loop records and all, with nothing to check it.

E0150

  × an `analog` block reads the discrete carrier `ctl` other than through `known`, `known_or`, `is_known` or `high`: it has no unknowns (§3.3.5)
    ╭─[buck.msl:15:46]
 14 │ module RSwitch(a: Electrical, b: Electrical, in ctl: Bit) { ron: f64 = 50m; roff: f64 = 1M; }
 15 │ analog for RSwitch { i(a, b) +> v(a, b) / if ctl { ron } else { roff }; }
    ·                                              ─┬─
    ·                                               ╰── a discrete carrier read in `analog`
 16 │ 
    ╰────
  help: read it through `known()`, `known_or(d)`, or `high()` for a `Bit`

error: typeck failed with 1 diagnostic(s)

Good to know

A full-size version of this loop adds what a product needs: a counter-based PWM with dead time, an ADC with a conversion delay, a PID in fixed point, soft start, current limits and a fault state machine. Every piece is a module of the kinds you have now seen, and its debug streams are written the same way.

Walkthrough: a first-order delta-sigma modulator

One module, both blocks: an integrator in continuous time, a one-bit quantizer sampled by a reaction, and feedback through a digital carrier.

module DeltaSigma1(vin: Electrical, in clk: Bit, out bit: Bit = false) {
    r: f64 = 100k; c: f64 = 1p; vref: f64 = 1.0;
    signal x: Electrical = 0.0;
}

analog for DeltaSigma1 {
    let fb = if bit { vref } else { -vref };
    i(x) +> ddt(c * v(x)) - (v(vin) - fb) / r;
}

digital for DeltaSigma1 {
    on clk.rise() { bit <- v(x) > 0.0; }
}
  1. The equation. x has one branch, so KCL gives c · dv(x)/dt = (v(vin) − fb) / r.
  2. Start. In an equilibrium solve ddt is zero, so x has no DC path. Its = 0.0 holds it at 0 V through the transient's operating point. On the first step the hold is released, with no jump.
  3. A rising clock edge at (t, 0). The reaction probes, so t is a time point. It reads v(x) as it was just before the edge and commits bit.
  4. At (t, 1). If bit changed, analog reads the change: a jump. The capacitor charge stays; the feedback current steps by 2 · vref / r.
  5. Falling edges fire no reaction, and analog never reads clk, so they cost the solver nothing.
  6. DC, AC and noise. Outside a transient's operating point nothing is held, so a Dc solve is singular and names x. That is the truth about an integrator with no DC path. Ac and Noise fail the same way; the loop's response needs a periodic analysis.

To watch the modulator, add emit!("q", t = now(), x = v(x), bit = v(x) > 0.0); to the reaction: one record per clock with the integrator's value and the decision taken from it.

Bigger systems are just composition. A SAR ADC places a sample-and-hold, a DAC, the Comparator above and a binary-search state machine, and adds one branch of its own on a public node of its DAC:

module SarAdc(vin: Electrical, in clk: Bit, in start: Bit, out code: Bit, out done: Bit) {
    cpar: f64 = 50f;
    signal vh: Electrical;
    signal vdac: Electrical;
    signal cmp: Bit;
    let sh = SampleHold(vin, vh, start);
    let dac = Dac8(code, vdac);
    let comp = Comparator(vh, vdac, cmp);
    let logic = SarLogic(clk, start, cmp, code, done);
}
analog for SarAdc { i(dac.core) +> ddt(cpar * v(dac.core)); }    // the parent's own branch on a public node

Good to know

The guidance in short: read a digital carrier in analog only when the analog behavior depends on it; prefer known times (at, after, every) to crossings; sample at a clock edge with a reaction, not with cross on the clock's analog image; give edges a duration with transition when edge rate matters.

Status

Mixed-signal designs run from source as compiled Rust, on the same solver that runs analog MSL. A full-size digitally controlled buck converter (12 V in, 200 MHz digital PWM, PID, ADC, comparators and a supervisor) starts from its .msl and regulates at 0.8 V within 3 mV with its loop closed, and the piperine command runs it (chapter 15). The front end parses and resolves every complete file of this chapter. The math methods of f64 such as sin (the sine sources) run.

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0250EdgeCounter(vin, count) with vin: ElectricalA node cannot go to a Bit port. Put an A2D in between.
E0430on v(vin) > 0.1 { … }A comparison is a bool, true for a whole stretch of time. The event is the crossing: on above(v(vin) - 0.1) { … }
E0336hit = v(vin) > 0.1; in a module bodyA definition is digital and cannot read a probe. Use a reaction on a crossing, as A2D does.
E0316EdgeCounter(v(vin) > 0.1, count)The same mistake through a port: an in port takes a digital expression only.
E0180i(a, b) +> v(a, b) / if flags { ron } else { roff };, flags a Bit<Logic>A Logic is not a bool. Say how to read it: flags.is_high().
E0442on at(v(vin)) { … }at takes a time computed from digital values, not a probe. For "when the voltage reaches…" use above.
E0350on every(0.0, 10u) { … } written inside another reaction, or every called in a fnEvents with state are instances. Name them in the module body: let tick = every(0.0, 10u);
E0504a run error at a digital edgeThe step after a jump did not converge: usually an ideal step into a stiff circuit. Give the edge a duration with transition.

Try it

In the cycle counter, set hyst: 0.0 and add a small 50 kHz ripple to the source (a second Vsin in series). Predict what the pulse stream does near each zero crossing, then restore the hysteresis and choose the smallest value that gives one pulse per cycle. In the buck, raise ki to 20m and look at the loop stream: does duty still settle, or does it swing around 0.28? (It swings: the loop now reacts faster than the LC filter can follow.)

Chapter 11

Inside a simulation

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can say what the solver is doing at any moment of a run: how a circuit becomes equations, how those equations are solved at rest and over time, how the analog and digital sides take turns, and why a given run is fast, slow or stuck. You do not need to know how to build a solver. You need to know what yours is doing with your model.

You have now written analog, digital and mixed models. This chapter shows what the simulator does with them, slowly, with a picture for every idea and one small circuit that is run from the first section to the last. Chapter 19, in Part 2, is the reference for the same ground: each rule of the solver in a few lines, and every option of a run with its default. Read chapter 19 when you want the rule; read this one when you want to see why the rule is there.

The chapter's circuit

Everything below runs on one file: a 5 V source, two resistors that divide it, and a capacitor on the middle node. A small monitor watches that node. Save it as divider.msl.

/// Ideal resistor.
module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    r: f64 where r > 0.0;
}

analog for Resistor {
    i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r;
}

/// Ideal capacitor.
module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    c: f64 where c > 0.0;
}

analog for Capacitor {
    i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n));
}

/// A constant voltage from `p` to `n`, and 1 V of small signal for an `ac` run.
module Vsource(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    dc: f64 = 1.0;
}

analog for Vsource {
    v(p, n) +> dc + ac_stim(1.0, 0.0);
}

/// Writes a record when `a` rises through `level`.
module Monitor(a: Electrical) {
    level: f64 = 2.0;
}

digital for Monitor {
    on cross(v(a) - level) {
        emit!("half", v = v(a));
    }
}

/// 5 V into 1 kΩ over 4 kΩ, with 10 nF on the middle node. `b` starts a transient at 0 V.
module Divider() {
    signal a: Electrical;
    signal b: Electrical = 0.0;
    let v1 = Vsource(a, gnd) { dc: 5.0 };
    let r1 = Resistor(a, b) { r: 1k };
    let r2 = Resistor(b, gnd) { r: 4k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(b, gnd) { c: 10n };
    let mon = Monitor(b);
}

From a circuit to equations

A simulator does not "follow the current around". It writes down what must be true of the circuit, and then looks for numbers that make all of it true at once.

What must be true is Kirchhoff's current law: at every node, the currents that leave add up to zero. Charge has nowhere else to go. Each device says how much current it carries for given voltages, which is exactly what a contribution i(p, n) +> … states. So the questions the solver asks are the node voltages.

The unknowns are therefore the voltage of every node except ground, and one extra current for each branch that fixes a voltage instead of a current. A voltage source is such a branch: v(p, n) +> dc does not say what current flows, so that current becomes an unknown and the statement "this voltage is dc" becomes its equation. The divider has two nodes and one source, which makes three unknowns and three equations.

Written out, the three equations are a table of numbers: a matrix. Each device adds its own few entries, and no device needs to know about any other.

A circuit of resistors and sources is linear: the table does not depend on the answer, so one solve of the table is the whole job. Ask for it with an operating point:

piperine run divider.msl --analysis op
run divider: op, top Divider, backend rust

signal     value
Divider.a  5.000000e0
Divider.b  4.000000e0

Four fifths of 5 V, as the divider rule says. The third unknown, the current through v1, is −1 mA: add i(v1) to the run's print list to see it. The sign follows the direction of the branch, from p to n; a source that delivers current has it flowing the other way inside itself.

Where the capacitor went

At rest nothing changes, so every ddt is zero and a capacitor carries no current. An operating point solves the circuit with its capacitors open. That is why the matrix above has no entry for c1, and why the answer does not depend on its 10 nF.

Circuits that are not straight lines: Newton's method

Put a diode in the circuit and the table is no longer fixed: the current of a diode depends exponentially on its own voltage, so the entry it would write depends on the answer. There is no formula to solve such a system in one go. The solver uses the oldest trick there is: guess, replace every curve by a straight line at the guess, solve the straight-line circuit, and use its answer as the next guess.

That is Newton's method, and it is the engine under every analysis in this chapter. In each iteration every device is asked two things at the current guess: its current, and how fast that current changes with each voltage (the slope of its curve). The compiler derived those slopes from your model's expressions; you never write them. The answers fill the matrix, the matrix is solved, and the result is the next guess.

Near the solution Newton is very fast: each iteration roughly doubles the number of correct digits. Far from it, a tangent can point almost anywhere, as the first guess in the figure shows. Two things keep that under control. The solver starts each solve from the best guess it has, usually the previous solution. And a model of a junction can limit its own step with limit or limexp (chapter 8), so that one wild tangent does not throw the voltage to a place where the exponential overflows.

When is it done?

Newton never lands exactly on the answer; it gets closer until someone says "close enough". The solver says so when two tests pass together.

  • The answer stopped moving. Every unknown changed, from the last iteration to this one, by no more than reltol times its own size plus a small absolute amount: vntol for a voltage, abstol for a branch current. With the defaults a 4 V node may still move by about 4 mV; a node near 0 V by 1 µV.
  • The currents balance. At every node, what is left over when the device currents are added up is within tolerance of zero. This test catches a solution that moved little only because the iteration was crawling.

The relative part is why reltol is the tolerance that matters most: it is the number of digits you are asking for. The absolute parts exist only so that a value that should be zero is not asked to be accurate to a fraction of nothing.

When Newton does not get there

Some circuits defeat a direct attempt: several junctions that all depend on each other, a starting guess far from the truth. Before giving up, the solver changes the problem into one it can solve and walks back to yours.

  1. Gmin stepping. A conductance is added from every node to ground, large enough at first (0.1 S) that every node is pinned and the circuit is easy. That circuit is solved. The conductance is then lowered step by step, each solve starting from the answer of the one before, and finally removed. If a step fails, the conductance is raised again and lowered more gently.
  2. Source stepping. If that is not enough, every source is switched off, where the answer is simply zero everywhere, and the sources are turned up in stages to full strength. A stage that fails is retried with a smaller increase.

These are the same two rescues ngspice uses, in the same order. Both are invisible when they work: you get the operating point of your circuit, not of the easier one. A model can see them happen through gmin() and source_scale() (chapter 18), which is how a junction model follows the ramp.

If the direct attempt and both rescues fail, the run stops with E0505 and names the unknowns that would not settle. This circuit has no operating point at all: a device that pushes current out above 0.5 V and pulls it in below.

/// Ideal resistor.
module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    r: f64 = 1k;
}

analog for Resistor {
    i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r;
}

/// Pushes 1 mA out of `p` above 0.5 V and pulls 1 mA in below it: no voltage satisfies it.
module Chatter(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {}

analog for Chatter {
    i(p, n) +> if v(p, n) > 0.5 {
        1m
    } else {
        -1m
    };
}

module Stuck() {
    signal n: Electrical;
    let r1 = Resistor(n, gnd);
    let c1 = Chatter(n, gnd);
}
piperine run stuck.msl --analysis op
compiling run `stuck` (top Stuck) with rustc
error: the op analysis failed: Newton: E0505: Newton failed to converge after 500 iterations: node of Stuck.r1.p, Stuck.c1.p

Read it from the right. The names at the end are where to look: the node that r1.p and c1.p share never passed the two tests. The number in the middle is the bound: one solve is allowed 500 iterations. The code says which kind of failure it was. The cure is in the model, almost never in the tolerances: here the step in Chatter has no slope for Newton to follow. Chapter 14 lists the run errors and the usual cause of each.

A design with digital parts adds one loop around all of this: solve the analog side, let the digital side react to that solution, and if a reaction changed something an analog model reads, solve again. Chapter 19 gives the bound on those rounds and the error that names the nets when they never agree.

Moving through time

A transient analysis asks how the circuit moves. It begins with an operating point, then advances in time steps: from a moment where everything is known, to a slightly later moment where everything is solved again.

The capacitor is what makes this harder than a sequence of operating points. Its current is ddt(c * v): it depends on how fast the voltage is changing, and "how fast" involves the past. The solver cannot evaluate a derivative at a single moment. What it can do is assume a shape for the curve between two moments, and under that assumption a derivative becomes ordinary arithmetic on the values at those moments. That assumption is the integration rule.

So each time step is again "solve a circuit with Newton", where every ddt has been replaced, for the length of that step, by a conductance and a source that remember the previous points. Shorter steps make the assumed shape more accurate, and cost more solves.

The rule: TR-BDF2

Piperine has one integration rule, called TR-BDF2, and you never choose it. It takes each step in two stages.

The first stage uses the trapezoidal rule, which is accurate but has a known weakness: after an abrupt change it can leave a small oscillation that flips sign at every step and never dies. The second stage uses a backward-difference rule that damps exactly that. Together they are as accurate as the trapezoidal rule alone, without the ringing. The price is two Newton solves per step.

How long should a step be?

Nobody tells the solver the step size. It measures, after every step, how much the curve bent inside it.

The measurement is made on every quantity under a ddt, the charge of each capacitor, and scaled by the tolerances so that a value of 1 means "the error of this step is just what reltol and abstol allow". This is the local truncation error. A circuit with no ddt at all has nothing to measure, and its steps simply grow.

The step-size controller then does what a careful driver does with the accelerator:

  • Quiet stretch: grow. When the measured error is under 1, the next step is longer, by at most a factor of 1.5.
  • Getting busy: shrink. When it is over 1, the next step is shorter, by at most a factor of 5. The step that was just taken is kept.
  • Too much: reject. When the error exceeds trtol, 7 by default, the step is thrown away. Everything returns to the checkpoint taken before it, and the step is tried again at one eighth of its size. A step in which Newton fails is treated the same way.

The gap between "aim for 1" and "reject above 7" is deliberate. The controller steers well inside the limit, so that throwing work away is rare.

Two bounds frame all of this. The first step of a run is one thousandth of stop, and no step is longer than one hundredth of stop, unless the run gives max_step: then max_step is both the first step and the longest. At the other end, the shortest step is one femtosecond.

Run the divider for 40 µs. The node b starts at 0 V, because its declaration says so, and charges toward 4 V.

piperine run divider.msl --analysis transient --stop 40e-6 --print b --print-step 10e-6
run divider: transient, top Divider, backend rust
@5.544857e-6 Divider.mon half v=2.000000e0

time         Divider.b
0.000000e0   0.000000e0
1.000000e-5  2.853923e0
2.000000e-5  3.671685e0
3.000000e-5  3.905948e0
4.000000e-5  3.973061e0

The table shows five rows because --print-step asked for one every 10 µs. The solver took many more steps than that. Without --print-step the table has one row per accepted step, 112 rows for this run, and those are the dots of the next figure.

The first step is 40 ns, a thousandth of the 40 µs. The steps then grow as the controller finds the curve easy, and from about 3.6 µs on they sit at 400 ns, a hundredth of stop: for this gentle curve it is the cap, not the error, that sets the step.

When a step cannot be completed

A step that keeps failing keeps shrinking. At the smallest step there is nothing shorter to try: a Newton failure there ends the run with E0508, or with E0504 when the step is the one right after a digital change, which then names the carrier that changed. Both carry the time. The usual cause is a model that jumps: an if on a voltage or on now() whose two sides do not meet. Give the jump a slope, or a duration with transition.

Events: stopping exactly where something happens

The monitor in the divider has a reaction on cross(v(a) - level). It must fire at the moment the voltage passes 2 V, and the solver's steps do not know that moment in advance. A step simply finds, when it is done, that the watched expression was negative at its start and is positive at its end.

So the solver backs up. It estimates the crossing time from the points it has, returns to the checkpoint, and takes a shorter step that ends on the estimate. It repeats this until the step ends within ttol of the estimated crossing (1 ps unless the cross sets its own) or the expression is within etol of zero. Only then is the step accepted and the event fired, so the reaction reads the solution at the crossing and not somewhere past it. There is one exception: in the step right after a jump (next section) a crossing is not located, and it fires at the end of that step.

In the run above the record is stamped 5.544857 µs. The formula for this circuit, 8 µs · ln 2, puts the crossing at 5.545177 µs. The 0.32 ns between them is not an error of the locating: it is the accuracy of the waveform itself, which was computed to reltol. The section on tolerances below shows it shrink.

above is located the same way; it also fires at the start of a run when its expression is already positive. Times that are known beforehand need none of this. An at(t), an after or an every is put in a queue, and when it matters to the analog side the step is simply made to end there.

Analog and digital take turns

A mixed-signal design has two kinds of time. The analog side moves in steps whose length it chooses. The digital side does nothing at all between instants, the moments when a commit is due or an event fires, and at an instant it may do a great deal without the clock moving. Time is counted in whole ticks of one femtosecond, unless the run sets resolution, so "the same moment" means the same on both sides.

The two sides never call each other. A third part, the owner of time, runs this loop:

  1. Propose. The analog side says how far it would like to step.
  2. Let digital run ahead. The digital side runs its instants up to that time, one by one. Most of them concern only digital state: a counter counts, a state machine moves. It stops before the first instant that touches the analog side, and puts itself back as it was just before it. That instant is a landing: the step is shortened to end exactly there.
  3. Solve. The analog side solves the step up to the landing, with the values the digital side had just before that instant. If Newton fails or the error is too large, both sides return to the checkpoint and the loop starts again with a shorter step. If a crossing is found inside, the step is retaken to end on it.
  4. Accept and react. The step is accepted. Crossings become events. Now the instant at the landing runs for real, and its reactions read the solution that was just accepted.
  5. Deliver. What the instant changed is handed to the analog side. If an analog model reads it, that is a jump: the next step starts short, at most a tenth of the last step, and with no memory of the slope before, because a slope from before the jump says nothing about after it.

Two properties of this order are worth holding on to. The digital side goes first, so an edge is never discovered late: no analog work is thrown away because a clock ticked in the middle of a step. And a reaction always reads an accepted solution: a model never sees a value from a step that was later rejected.

What makes an instant a landing

An instant is a landing when it changes a carrier that an analog model reads, or when a reaction that runs in it can reach the analog side: it probes a voltage, or it commits without a delay to something an analog model depends on. The second half is decided from the structure of the design, before the run. It does not look at whether the value actually changes this time.

The other direction: digital reads analog

The loop above shows the digital side shaping the analog steps, and a jump is the digital side acting on the analog one. The loop runs the other way too, and it is the reason the digital side cannot simply run free until the end. A reaction can depend on the analog side in two ways: it can sample a continuous value, with v(a) in its body, or it can wait for an analog event, such as cross or above.

Take sampling first. The digital side keeps no copy of the waveform. A voltage exists only as the solution of an accepted step, so a reaction that reads v(a) at time t needs a step that ends exactly at t. That is why such an instant is a landing. In step 2 the digital side stops before it, and in step 3 the analog side solves a step that ends there. Only after the step is accepted does the instant run, and the reaction reads the voltage as it was at that time, not as it was at some earlier step. Which reactions sample is known from the structure of the design before the run starts, so the solver never has to guess. The price is a shorter step at every such instant: a clock whose edge makes a reaction sample a node ends one analog step per edge, and a clock whose reactions only count does not.

An analog event is the case where the digital side does not even know when to look. Whether and when v(a) - level changes sign is a property of the waveform, so the analog side is the one that notices, while it takes a step. This is the locating of the section above: the step is taken, the sign has changed inside it, the solver goes back to the checkpoint and retakes the step so that it ends at the crossing, on the nearest tick. That is also a landing, and it is the same mechanism as the other one: the step is made to end where the digital side needs a solution. When the step is accepted, the crossing becomes an event at that instant and the reactions that wait for it run, reading the solution at the crossing. In divider.msl this is how the Monitor gets its value: on cross(v(a) - level) runs in the instant the step ended on, and v(a) in its body is the solution there.

Put the two directions together and the order in an instant is always the same. Solve, then react. The analog side is solved up to the instant first, using the values the digital side had before it; then the reactions run, reading what was just solved. If a reaction commits something that an analog model reads, that value is not applied behind the analog side's back. It is delivered after the instant, and the next analog step reads it. It is the jump of step 5, with its short first step, and it is what closes the loop: the analog side raises the instant, the digital side answers in it, and the answer reaches the analog side in the step after.

Inside one instant: microsteps and settle

An instant is not one action. A reaction commits a value; another reaction is waiting for that value to change; it commits something else. All of this happens at one time on the clock, in rounds called microsteps.

The rule that keeps this orderly: a commit made in one microstep is seen in the next, never in the same one. Every reaction of a microstep reads the same picture of the world, so the order in which they run cannot matter. The instant ends when a microstep leaves nothing due. A loop of commits with no delay never gets there; chapter 19 gives the bound and the error.

When the instant belongs to a time point, settle fires once at the very end, after everything has gone quiet. That is why on settle is the place to print or to check: it sees the analog solution and the final digital values of that moment, and nothing half-done.

AC: the response to a small signal

An AC analysis answers a different question: if a small sine wave is added at the input, how large is it, and how late, at the output? It does not step through time at all.

It works in two moves. First the operating point is found, as above. Then every device is replaced by its tangent at that point. For a small enough signal the tangent is the device, and the circuit is linear again, as in the first section. What changes with frequency is the capacitors: at a frequency f a capacitor behaves like a conductance of size 2π f c that also shifts the phase by a quarter turn. So for each frequency of the sweep the solver fills one table of complex numbers and solves it once. There is no Newton and nothing to converge.

The signal comes from the sources that declare one: the ac_stim(1.0, 0.0) in Vsource is 1 V at phase 0, and it is ignored by every other analysis.

piperine run divider.msl --analysis ac --from 1e3 --to 1e6 --per-decade 1 --print b
run divider: ac, top Divider, backend rust

freq        mag(Divider.b)  phase(Divider.b)
1.000000e3  7.989913e-1     -2.877578e0
1.000000e4  7.147811e-1     -2.668661e1
1.000000e5  1.560959e-1     -7.874827e1
1.000000e6  1.591235e-2     -8.886029e1

At low frequency the capacitor does nothing and the output is the divider's 0.8. Above the corner the capacitor wins, and the output falls by ten for every tenfold increase of the frequency while the phase heads for −90 degrees.

What "small" hides

An AC result is about the tangent, so it cannot show anything the tangent does not have: clipping, slewing, a comparator that flips. The amplitude you give ac_stim does not make the signal large; it only scales the answer. If the question is "what happens with a real 1 V swing", it is a transient question.

Noise: many small sources at once

Every resistor and every junction is a tiny random source of its own. A noise analysis asks how much of all that arrives at one output, and which device is to blame.

It reuses the machinery of AC. The circuit is linearized at the operating point, and at each frequency every noise source reaches the output through a gain of its own. Random sources do not add as voltages; their powers add. So the output noise at a frequency is the sum, over all sources, of the source's strength times the square of its gain. Finding the gain from every source to one output looks like one solve per source, but one solve of the table "seen from the output" yields all of them together, so the cost is one solve per frequency whatever the number of sources.

The result is a curve of noise density over frequency, each source's share of it, and the total over the band, which is the area under the curve.

Status

The solver has this analysis. The piperine command does not offer it yet: its analyses are the eight of chapter 15.

What this means for you

Why a fast clock makes a run slow

The cost of a transient is, to a good approximation, the number of steps. A purely analog circuit takes as many steps as its waveforms need. A mixed design takes at least one step per landing, and a clock can make every one of its edges a landing.

The buck converter of chapter 15 is the worked case. It switches at 500 kHz, so its analog waveforms have something to show every 2 µs. But its controller runs on a 200 MHz clock, and the reactions on that clock can reach the gate drivers, so today every clock edge is a landing: one every 2.5 ns. Measured on the first 100 µs of its startup run, that is about 40 000 landings. Only a few hundred of those edges change anything the power stage reads. After each landing the step grows back by at most 1.5 times per step, and the next edge arrives before it has grown far. The run settles at roughly three steps per clock edge, about 128 000 accepted steps per 100 µs with an average step under a nanosecond, and never comes near its own max_step of 10 ns.

What you can do about it today, in order of effect:

  • Clock only as fast as the question needs. The step count follows the clock rate, so a study of the loop's response is a candidate for a slower counter clock and a coarser PWM.
  • Keep fast digital logic away from analog readers. An instant that only moves digital state costs little: the digital side runs it while running ahead, and no analog step ends on it. It is the path from a reaction to a carrier that an analog model reads which makes its instants landings.
  • Prefer known times to crossings. An at or an every costs one landing. A cross costs the retakes that locate it.
  • Simulate the time you need. Step count is proportional to simulated time. A startup that takes milliseconds is thousands of switching periods whatever the solver does.

What the knobs actually do

KeyWhat it changesWhat it does not
print_stepThe rows of the table: one per multiple of it, each value interpolated between the two steps around it.The steps. The solver takes exactly the steps it would take without it, so it makes a run neither faster nor more accurate.
max_stepThe longest step, and the first one. It guarantees a waveform is sampled at least that finely.It does not shorten steps the error control already keeps short. Where it does bind, cost grows in proportion.
reltolThe accuracy asked of every unknown in Newton, and of every step in time. Smaller means more iterations and shorter steps.It cannot repair a model that jumps. A run that fails to converge at 1e-3 rarely converges at 1e-6.
vntol, abstolThe floors under reltol, for voltages and for currents. They matter for signals near zero: microvolts, picoamperes.Signals of ordinary size. For those, reltol decides.
trtolHow far the step error may exceed its target before the step is thrown away.The accuracy the controller aims for, which stays the same. Lowering it adds steps without aiming any closer.
max_stepsThe budget of attempts after which a transient gives up and says so.Anything about the solution. It turns a run that would crawl forever into an error you can read.

The divider shows the difference between the two knobs people reach for. Each line is the same 40 µs run:

RunRows, one per stepCrossing of 2 V reported atDistance from 5.545177 µs
defaults1125.544857 µs0.32 ns early
--max-step 100e-94035.545401 µs0.22 ns late
--reltol 1e-54375.545173 µs0.005 ns early

Both cost between three and four times the steps. Capping the step spread that effort evenly over the whole run and barely moved the answer. Tightening reltol spent it where the curve bends and gained almost two digits. When you want more accuracy, ask for accuracy; use max_step when the problem is that something narrow is being stepped over.

Reading a failure

CodeWhat the solver was doingWhere to look
E0503Solving the table, and the table has no single answer.The node or branches it names: a node with no DC path to ground, or two voltage sources in a loop.
E0505Looking for an operating point; the direct attempt and both rescues failed.The unknowns it names, and the device between them: an exponential with no limiting, or a characteristic with a jump.
E0507Evaluating a model, and the result was not a number.The instance and term it names: a division by something that reached zero, an exp that overflowed.
E0504Taking the first step after a digital change, at the smallest step.The carrier it names: an ideal edge into a circuit that cannot follow it. Use transition.
E0508Taking a time step, at the smallest step.The time it gives: something in an analog block jumps there.
E0501Running one digital instant that never went quiet.The nets it names: a loop of commits with no delay.
E0506Alternating analog solves and digital reactions at the operating point.The nets it names: a comparator and what it drives, each undoing the other.

One more message has no code: a transient that uses up max_steps stops and prints where it was, how small its step had become and how many attempts were accepted and rejected. Read those numbers before raising the budget. Many rejections at a tiny step mean a jump the solver cannot land on. Few rejections and steady progress mean the run is simply long.

Try it

Run the divider's transient without --print-step and write the table with --csv steps.csv. Find the 40 ns first step and the 400 ns steps in the time column. Then change --stop to 400e-6. What are the first step and the largest step now, and how many rows cover the first 40 µs, where the curve does all its bending? (400 ns and 4 µs: both scale with stop. The first 40 µs now have 41 rows instead of 112. With the cap out of the way the error control alone sets the step, and at the default reltol that is all this curve needs: the short run was being held finer than its tolerance asked.)

Chapter 12

Files, modules and packages

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can split a design over several files, share modules between them with mod, use and pub, write the manifest of a package, and depend on someone else's package.

Until now every example was one file. Real designs are not, and MSL organizes code in three levels: a file is a namespace, a tree of files is a crate, and a directory with a manifest is a package. The example shows all three, and the rules follow it.

A complete example: one design, three files

Take the RC low-pass of chapter 1 and give it a proper home. The parts go in one file, the filter built from them in another, and the crate root declares both and holds the top.

lowpass/
├── Piperine.toml
└── src/
    ├── lib.msl            the crate root
    ├── parts.msl          mod parts;
    └── filter.msl         mod filter;
// src/parts.msl
/// Ideal resistor.
pub module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

/// Ideal capacitor.
pub module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c > 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }

/// A constant voltage on `p`, referred to ground.
pub module Vdc(p: Electrical) { dc: f64 = 1.0; }
analog for Vdc { v(p) +> dc; }
// src/filter.msl
use crate::parts::{Capacitor, Resistor};

/// First-order low-pass with corner frequency `fc`.
pub module LowPass(a: Electrical, b: Electrical) {
    fc: f64 = 1k where fc > 0.0;
    r: f64 = 1k where r > 0.0;
    let r1 = Resistor(a, b) { r };
    let c1 = Capacitor(b, gnd) { c: 1.0 / (2.0 * PI * r * fc) };   // the capacitor of this corner
}
// src/lib.msl
pub mod parts;              // loads src/parts.msl
pub mod filter;             // loads src/filter.msl

use crate::filter::LowPass;
use crate::parts::Vdc;

/// The top: a source and the filter.
pub module Bench() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    pub signal vout: Electrical;
    let src = Vdc(vin) { dc: 1.0 };
    pub let lp = LowPass(vin, vout) { fc: 10k };
}
[package]
name = "lowpass"
version = "0.1.0"

[run.op]
analysis = "op"

What each new line does:

  • pub mod parts; in the crate root declares a namespace and loads it from parts.msl. A file that no mod names is not part of the crate.
  • pub module Resistor makes the module visible outside its file. Without pub it is private to parts.
  • use crate::parts::{Capacitor, Resistor}; brings two names into scope. crate is the root of this crate.
  • analog for Resistor stays next to module Resistor. This rule is about hardware only: a hardware module and all its behavior blocks are in the same file, so you never hunt for the equations of a device.
  • The manifest needs only a name and a version. std is not listed: every crate has it. The [run.op] table is for the command line: a run with no bench key takes its top from the library itself.

Splitting a design changes nothing about its instances: the capacitor is still Bench.lp.c1, and a stream written inside the filter is still named under Bench.lp. Paths of instances follow the hierarchy of the design, not the files it is written in.

Run it

Commands work from any directory inside the project; the nearest Piperine.toml upward is the project. With one run in the manifest, the name may be left out:

piperine check
piperine run
run op: op, top Bench, backend rust

signal      value
Bench.vin   1.000000e0
Bench.vout  1.000000e0

check reads every file, resolves every name and type-checks the top of each run without simulating; it ends with one ok line, or with the diagnostics. Once the design has more than one test circuit, move Bench out of the library into benches/, as the project of chapter 1 does, and import the parts with use lowpass::….

Status

Crates and packages are implemented: the object model resolves names across crates, and piperine-project reads Piperine.toml, locates path and git dependencies and writes the lockfile. The piperine command runs a project of several files, as the box above shows.

A bigger project on disk

adc/
├── Piperine.toml          the manifest
├── Piperine.lock          written for you: pinned git commits
└── src/
    ├── lib.msl            the crate root
    ├── frontend.msl       mod frontend;
    ├── logic/
    │   ├── mod.msl        mod logic;
    │   └── sar.msl        pub mod sar;  (in logic/mod.msl)
    └── data/
        └── diode_iv.tbl   read by include_str!

The crate root is always src/lib.msl, and sources end in .msl. mod name; loads name.msl or name/mod.msl below the declaring file, exactly like name.rs and name/mod.rs. Having neither, or both, is E0161.

Namespaces: mod, use, pub

// src/lib.msl
pub mod frontend;           // src/frontend.msl
pub mod logic;              // src/logic/mod.msl

use util::filters::Rc;      // `util` is a dependency named in Piperine.toml
use crate::logic::sar::SarLogic;

A path names an item through the namespaces that hold it, with :: between the segments. use gives an item a short name in the namespace that writes it, pub makes an item visible outside its namespace, and pub use passes a name on under the namespace that re-exports it. crate is the root of your crate, self is the current namespace and super the one above it. From another crate, an item is visible only when it and every namespace on the way to it are pub. A hardware module and all its behavior blocks must be in the same file.

The manifest

[package]
name = "adc"
version = "0.1.0"
authors = ["Mixed-Signal Team"]
description = "A SAR ADC and its building blocks"

[dependencies]
util = { path = "../util" }
pdk  = { git = "https://example.com/acme/pdk.git", tag = "v1.4.0", version = "1.4" }

A manifest with dependencies looks like the one above. That is the whole format: [package] with name and version (required), authors and description (optional), and [dependencies]. Any other table or key is E0171.

Dependency keyMeaning
pathA directory, relative to this manifest. Exactly one of path and git.
gitA repository URL; the package is at its root.
branch / tag / revAt most one, with git only. None means the default branch.
versionA check, by the caret rule of semantic versioning: "1.4" admits >=1.4.0, <2.0.0. It does not pick a version.
packageThe real package name when the key differs: a rename.

A package name and a dependency name are identifiers: no hyphens, no keywords, never std. The key you write under [dependencies] is the name you use in paths, and it needs no other declaration in the sources. A path never begins with ::.

flowchart LR
  subgraph P["package adc (Piperine.toml)"]
    L["src/lib.msl
crate root"] --> F["frontend.msl"]
    L --> G["logic/mod.msl"] --> S["logic/sar.msl"]
  end
  P -- "util = { path }" --> U["package util
src/lib.msl"]
  P -- "pdk = { git, tag }" --> K["package pdk
src/lib.msl"]
  P -. "always" .-> STD["std
embedded"]
  U -. "always" .-> STD
  K -. "always" .-> STD

The lockfile

Piperine.lock sits beside the root manifest and records the commit each git dependency resolved to. While a dependency's URL and requested reference stay the same, that commit is used; an explicit update resolves again. A lockfile inside a dependency is ignored. Path dependencies are not recorded: their location pins them.

version = 1

[[package]]
name = "pdk"
version = "1.4.0"
source = "git+https://example.com/acme/pdk.git?tag=v1.4.0"
commit = "3f1c9a0e5b7d2c4e8a6f1b0d9c3e7a5f2b8d4c61"

No search paths, no textual include, no macros

A crate reaches another crate only through a dependency named in its manifest. No implementation may find one through a search path, a registry or an environment variable. There is no -I and no package registry. There is no textual include of source either: code is shared through mod and use, and the only ! forms are emit! and include_str!.

include_str! is for data. It is allowed only in a const initializer, its path is relative to the source file, and the file is read once, at elaboration. A run never opens a file:

const IV:  [(f64, f64); 64] = parse_table(include_str!("diode_iv.tbl"));   // whitespace columns
const ROM: [u32; 1024]      = parse_hex(include_str!("boot.hex"));         // $readmemh format

module MeasuredDiode(a: Electrical, k: Electrical) {}
analog for MeasuredDiode { i(a, k) +> table_model(v(a, k), IV, "3E"); }   // cubic, error outside

The standard library and the prelude

std is a crate that ships inside Piperine. Every crate depends on it, no manifest names it, and no dependency may be called std. The prelude, the pub items of std::prelude, is implied in every file, which is why Bit, Electrical, idt and above need no use. A handful of core names need nothing at all: gnd, Nature, m, dtemp, str and the core primitives like ddt and cross. Chapter 13 tours both.

When names collide

The first segment of a path is looked up in this order, and the first hit wins:

  1. the enclosing blocks and declarations;
  2. the items and imports of the enclosing namespace;
  3. the dependency names of the crate;
  4. the prelude;
  5. the core names.

So in an ordinary path, a local item beats a dependency of the same name, silently. In a use, the same clash is an error, never a silent choice:

mod util;                  // a local namespace called util
use util::cells::Cell;     // E0163: the local `util` or the dependency `util`?

Only a name that can head a path clashes: a namespace, a type, a nature or a module. A local fn util or const util lives in the value namespace, so util::cells::Cell still reaches the crate.

Two ways out: rename the dependency in the manifest, or name the local one through crate.

[dependencies]
utilx = { path = "../util", package = "util" }    # now `utilx::cells::Cell`
use crate::util::cells::Cell;   // the local namespace, unambiguous

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0161use crate::parts::Resistor; with module Resistor not pubThe path names a private item. Mark it pub module Resistor.
E0161mod filters; with the file named filter.mslmod name; loads name.msl or name/mod.msl. Match the names.
E0401analog for Resistor { … } in filter.mslA behavior block lives in the file of its module. Move it to parts.msl.
E0162use crate::parts::Resistor; beside a local module ResistorOne name, two declarations in one namespace. Rename one, or import with as.
E0163mod util; and a dependency util, then use util::…Ambiguous first segment. crate::util, or rename the dependency.
E0171[dev-dependencies] in Piperine.tomlNot a table of the format. Only [package] and [dependencies] exist.
E0175a package with src/main.msl onlyThe crate root is always src/lib.msl.
E0610let t = include_str!("iv.tbl"); in a moduleinclude_str! is for const items: const IV: … = parse_table(include_str!("iv.tbl"));

Try it

Move Vdc out of parts.msl into a new file src/sources.msl. Which lines change? (Three: add pub mod sources; to the root, change the root's import to use crate::sources::Vdc;, and move the module with its analog block. Nothing in filter.msl changes.)

Chapter 13

Core and standard library

Goal

By the end of this chapter you know where every name you did not declare comes from, you can find the library module for a job instead of writing it again, and you have a small debugging kit built from emit!, log, require and fatal.

This chapter is a tour, not a lesson: read the first two parts, then skim the tables and come back when you need a name. Each part has one small module that uses a few of its names, and every one of those modules is a complete file.

Every name you write in a model comes from one of three places: your own files, the core, or the standard library. The core is what only the simulator can provide: reading a voltage, taking a time derivative, locating a crossing. Everything else, idt, transition, Setup, is ordinary source code written in the language, shipped as the crate std, and you can open it and read it. This chapter is the tour that tells you which is which.

What is core

A core name needs no import and cannot be redefined: a namespace item with the same name is error E0457. The list is short on purpose.

Core nameWhat it is
gndThe reference node, the same net everywhere.
NatureThe bound that makes a declaration a nature.
m, dtempThe multiplicity and the temperature offset every instance carries. They are in scope only inside a module.
strThe type of text. It is how a model reads a name or a file at elaboration.

The core primitives come in kinds, and the kind says where each one may appear:

KindNamesWhat they do
Probespotential, flow, inflowRead the voltage or current of a branch. Your nature gives them their short names, v and i.
Solver functionsddx, ac_stimA partial derivative, and the small-signal stimulus of an AC sweep.
Environmenttemperature, simparam, analysis, now, freq, gmin, source_scaleThe temperature, a session parameter, which analysis is running, the time, the frequency of a small-signal sweep, and the solver's continuation variables: the node shunt and the source scale.
Core modulesddt, delay, limit, noise, cross, atTime derivative, a delayed copy of a signal, Newton step limiting, a noise source, and the two ways to schedule an event: located by the solver (cross) or at a known time (at). You place them like any module.
Eventssettle, changedsettle fires when a time point has nothing left to do; s.changed() fires when a carrier changes.
Statementsbound_stepCaps the next time step.
Carrier methodsprev, others, levelThe previous value of a carrier, what the other drivers put on it, and the known flag of one level.

Two more names end in ! and are the only forms of their kind: emit! writes a record to a stream, and include_str! reads a text file once, at elaboration. There are no user macros.

std::prelude

The prelude is the namespace std::prelude. It re-exports every pub item of the other eight namespaces, and it is implied in every file of every crate, so you write Electrical and transition with no use. You can still write the long path, std::analog::transition, when you want to be explicit. A name you declare yourself, or a dependency name, hides a prelude item of the same name.

module Tour(p: Electrical, in clk: Bit) {}
analog for Tour { i(p) +> 0.0; }
digital for Tour { on clk.rise() { emit!("seen", t = now()); } }

Each part below is one namespace. The names in a table are the exports; the example under it uses a few of them.

std::constants: physical constants

Six f64 constants, in SI units.

QThe elementary charge.
K_BBoltzmann's constant.
EPS0The vacuum permittivity.
PIThe circle constant.
EEuler's number.
infPositive infinity.
const VT_300K: f64 = K_B * 300.0 / Q;     // the thermal voltage at 300 K

std::carriers: natures and the short

The natures every analog and digital connection is typed with, and one module that joins two nodes.

BitThe discrete carrier. Bit is a bit, Bit<u8> a byte, Bit<f64> a real-valued event stream; any of them starts at the value you write, or at its type's default.
ElectricalVoltage across, current through, with the accessors v and i.
ThermalTemperature and power, with temp and pwr.
VSignalA voltage-only nature, for signals that carry no current.
ShortA module that makes its two nodes the same node.
module Rc(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    r: f64 = 1k;
    c: f64 = 1p;
    signal mid: Electrical;
}
analog for Rc {
    i(p, mid) +> v(p, mid) / r;
    i(mid, n) +> ddt(c * v(mid, n));
}

std::values and std::logic: four-state logic, bit patterns and clocks

The four-state Logic type and the tools around it, and three small time sources.

Logic, LogicStateFour-state logic on N levels, each 0, 1, x or z, with the four-state operators, is_high, is_low, is_x, is_z, to_bits and to_volts.
castReads the same levels as another type of the same width, and gives an Option: None when the target type rejects them.
sameTrue when two values have identical levels: on a Logic the four states are compared literally.
matches_bitsCompares a Logic to a pattern of 0, 1, x and z.
logicBuilds a Logic from such a pattern.
initAn event that fires once, at the start of the analysis.
everyAn event that fires at a start time and then every period.
sinceThe time elapsed since a carrier last changed.
