The methods rise() and fall() of Bit are defined here too, which is why clk.rise() works everywhere. A rise() is a change that ends at true, so a Bit that starts high has not risen.

module Latch(in clk: Bit, in d: Bit<u8>, out q: Bit<u8> = 0) {}
digital for Latch { on clk.rise() { q <- d; } }

std::analog: continuous-time building blocks

The functions and modules a behavioral analog model is made of. Most are modules written over the core primitives, so each has a body you can read.

StateA unitless nature for internal states.
vtThe thermal voltage at the current temperature.
white_noise, flicker_noise, noise_tableNoise sources with a flat, a 1/f and a tabulated spectrum.
limexpAn exponential that is safe for Newton: it grows linearly past a limit.
pnjlim, fetlim, limvds, deltalimThe step limiters that keep a junction, a gate voltage and a drain voltage from jumping too far between iterations.
aboveA rising crossing that also fires at the start when the expression is already positive.
absdeltaFires each time an expression has moved by a given amount.
last_crossingThe time of the last rising crossing.
idtThe time integral.
idtmodThe integral wrapped into a range, for phases.
slewFollows a signal with a bounded rate of change.
transitionTurns a piecewise-constant input into a signal with a delay and finite rise and fall times.
zi_nd, zi_np, zi_zd, zi_zpDiscrete-time filters, given as numerator and denominator, numerator and poles, zeros and denominator, or zeros and poles.
laplace_nd, laplace_np, laplace_zd, laplace_zpThe same four forms for continuous-time filters H(s).
table_modelInterpolates a table of samples.
db10, db20A power ratio and an amplitude ratio in decibels.
normalOne step of a random generator with a normal distribution.
module DacRamp(in code: Bit<u8>, vout: Electrical) {
    vref: f64 = 1.0; tedge: f64 = 100p;
    let ramp = transition(vref * (code as f64) / 256.0, 0.0, tedge, tedge);
}
analog for DacRamp { v(vout) +> ramp; }

std::nets: nets with more than one driver

The library declares no net. The model declares the nature of its net and gives it impl Resolve (chapter 6); this section holds what such a nature needs, and the switch-level parts, each generic over any nature that implements Resolve<Driven>.

StrengthHow hard a driver drives: HighZ, Weak, Pull, Strong, Supply, in that order.
Driven, Driven::strongestWhat a module drives into a net that resolves by strength: a level and a strength, including the high-impedance Driven::Z. strongest is the resolution: the strongest drive wins, and equal strengths that disagree give x.
sumThe resolution of a real-valued net whose drivers add.
TribufA tristate buffer.
Pullup, PulldownA weak driver that holds a net high or low while nobody else drives it.
KeeperHolds the last driven level, weakly.
TranA bidirectional pass gate.
TgateA CMOS transmission gate: two Tran in parallel.
pub nature IrqLine { value: Driven }

impl Resolve for IrqLine {
    fn resolve(drives: [Driven; N]) -> Driven { Driven::strongest(drives) }
}

module IrqSource(in req: Bit, irq_n: IrqLine) { irq_n = if req { Driven::strong(false) } else { Driven::Z }; }

module Board(in req0: Bit, in req1: Bit) {
    signal irq_n: IrqLine;
    let pu = Pullup(irq_n);
    let s0 = IrqSource(req0, irq_n);
    let s1 = IrqSource(req1, irq_n);
}

std::convert: crossing between analog and digital

A Bit is never connected to an Electrical node for you; you place a converter.

D2ADrives a node from a Bit: a level through a resistance, with finite edges.
A2DReads a node into a Bit with hysteresis, right from the operating point.
BidirA pad: one resolved net and one node, driving and reading both.
module Pad(in d: Bit, out q: Bit, node: Electrical, vdd: Electrical) {
    let drv = D2A(d, node, vdd);
    let rcv = A2D(node, q);
}

std::monitor: temporal checks and delays

Monitors only report: each is a module that calls require when the property it watches fails. They never write what they watch.

AckWithinEvery request is acknowledged within a number of clock cycles.
NeverA condition never holds at a clock edge.
StableA signal does not change while it is enabled.
SetupA data signal was stable for a given time before the clock edge.
HoldA data signal stays stable for a given time after the clock edge.
MinPulseA pulse is at least a given width.
inertialAn inertial delay: a change passes only after the input has been stable for the delay, so short glitches vanish.
TribufDelayedA tristate buffer with an inertial gate delay, slower to release than to drive.
module Reg(in clk: Bit, in d: Bit, out q: Bit = false) {
    let chk = Setup(clk, d) { ts: 100p };
}
digital for Reg { on clk.rise() { q <- d; } }

std::io: records, checks and data files

Everything a model writes to a stream is a record; these are the functions that write the common ones, and the two readers for data files.

SeverityInfo, warning or error.
logWrites a message with a severity.
finish, fatalEnd the analysis, and end it as failed with a message.
requireA check that must pass. A failed one is recorded, not hidden.
coverRecords that a situation was reached.
targetRecords how much margin a design has against a goal.
parse_table, parse_hexRead a table of numbers or a hex memory image from text, at elaboration.
module Probe(in clk: Bit, in ok: Bit) {}
digital for Probe { on clk.rise() { require("ok_at_edge", ok); log(Severity::Info, "edge"); } }

None of these is magic. Each is one line over emit!, which is why they follow its rules: allowed in reactions and in analog, stamped with the instance and the instant, and with no effect on the simulation except the one finish asks for.

CallIs the same asStream
log(sev, msg)emit!("log", severity = sev, msg = msg)the instance's log
finish()emit!("sim", finish = true)sim, global, reactions only
fatal(msg)emit!("sim", finish = true, failed = true, msg = msg)sim, global, reactions only
require(name, pass)emit!("checks", kind = "require", name = name, pass = pass)checks, global, reactions only

Together with your own streams they make a debugging kit. This watchdog uses three of them: a record with the numbers, a message, and an end to a run that is going nowhere.

/// Ends the run when `busy` stays high for more than `limit` clock edges.
module Watchdog(in clk: Bit, in busy: Bit) {
    limit: u16 = 1000;
    signal run: Bit = 0;
}
digital for Watchdog {
    on clk.rise() {
        if !busy {
            run <- 0;
        } else if run >= limit {
            emit!("stuck", t = now(), edges = run);
            fatal("busy was never released");
        } else {
            run <- run + 1;
        }
    }
}

busy is a bool, so each edge takes exactly one arm. The stuck record carries the time and the count; fatal ends the analysis at that instant and marks it failed.

Implemented by the engine

Five library items have no body to read, because their job is to read the characters of a str, which the language itself cannot do. They are declared extern "intrinsic": you see the signature and the contract, and the engine supplies the body. Only std may declare one; the same line in your crate is error E0125. Like every other library item they are pub and reach your code through std::prelude, by their single names.

ItemWhat the engine does for it
matches_bitsCompares a value to a pattern text.
logicBuilds a value from a pattern text.
table_modelReads the control text that picks the interpolation and the extrapolation.
parse_tableReads whitespace columns of numbers.
parse_hexReads a hex memory image.

An implementation may also run any other item natively, as long as the result is observably the same as the written definition. That is a speed-up, not a different meaning.

Replacing a library item

A library item has no privilege. Declare an item with the same name in your own namespace and yours is the one the names in that scope find; the library's stays untouched. Only the core names of this chapter are off limits.

pub fn vt() -> f64 { 0.0259 }          // this crate's vt, fixed at room temperature

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
E0457fn ddt(x: f64) -> f64 { … }A core name cannot be redefined. Pick another name; only library items can be replaced.
E0125extern "intrinsic" fn my_parse(text: str) -> f64;Only std declares engine items. Write the function in MSL, or use extern "C".
E0603require(…), finish() or fatal(…) in analogThe reserved streams take records from reactions only. Move the call into on settle { … } or another reaction.
E0605emit!("checks", name = 3);On a reserved stream the host's fields have fixed types: name is text. Use require, cover or target.
E0161x.cbrt(), or another method the library does not listThe library of the built-in types is a closed list. Write the expression another way: x.powf(1.0 / 3.0).
E0702logic("10q1")A pattern holds only 0, 1, x and z, one character per level of the type.

Try it

Without looking at the tables: which library module turns a stepped Bit<f64> level into a ramp, which one tells you how long ago a signal last changed, and which one checks that two commands are never high together? (transition, since and Never.) Then place a Watchdog in the Press bench of chapter 9 with limit: 3, its busy port on button, and say at which clock edge the run ends if the button is held down. (The fourth edge with the button down: the first three count, the fourth finds run at 3.)

Chapter 14

Reading errors

Goal

By the end of this chapter you can read any diagnostic, tell from its code which part of the language it is about and when it was found, and you have a method for the harder case: a model that compiles and still does the wrong thing.

Every error condition has one code, Eppnn, where pp names the area of the language the rule belongs to. E01xx covers values, files and packages, E04xx behavior, E09xx the lexer, and so on.

A diagnostic carries a code, a message, a primary label on the source span, optional secondary labels and an optional help line. Here is how one reads in a terminal:

error[E0210]: mismatched types: expected `f64`, found `an integer literal`
  --> sample_hold.msl:2:29
   |
 2 |     signal held: Bit = 0;
   |                             ^ expected `f64`, found `an integer literal`
error[E0163]: `util` names both a dependency and a local item
  --> src/lib.msl:2:5
   |
 1 | mod util;
   | --------- the local namespace `util`
 2 | use util::cells::Cell;
   |     ^^^^ ambiguous first segment
   |
   = help: rename the dependency with `package = "util"` under another key,
           or write `crate::util` for the local one

These two renderings are illustrative. The exact wording is the tool's, but the parts are fixed: code, message, labeled spans and help.

When errors happen

An error belongs to one of five phases: package (reading manifests and dependencies), parse, elaboration (names, types, params, the netlist), run (an analysis fails, naming the instance and the instant) and host (a host request is refused, and the analysis goes on). Most of what you meet while writing a model is elaboration.

A worked example: three errors in one file

Here is a first attempt at the sample-and-hold of chapter 5, written the way a newcomer would. It parses, so the listing below is legal syntax, and it has three mistakes that elaboration finds.

module SampleHold(vin: Electrical, vout: Electrical, clk: Bit) {
    signal held: Bit = 0;
}
digital for SampleHold {
    on clk { held <- v(vin); }
}
analog for SampleHold {
    v(vout) +> held;
}
  1. E0310 on clk: Bit. A one-driver port needs a direction. The module only reads the clock: in clk: Bit.
  2. E0210 on = 0. The initial value of held must be an f64, and an integer literal is not one: nothing converts by itself (chapter 2). Write = 0.0.
  3. E0430 on on clk. The head of a reaction is an event, and clk is a level. The sampler acts on the rising edge: on clk.rise().

Diagnostics come sorted by file and position, so you fix a file from top to bottom. Each fix here is local: one line, at the place the label points to.

Run it

Put the faulty listing in a project and place it in a bench, a module without ports, because checking starts from a top and works its way down. Then check the project without running anything:

piperine check

The diagnostics come on the error output, each with its code, the file, the line and the source excerpt, as in the two renderings at the top of this chapter, and the command exits with status 1. Fix the three lines and the same command prints one ok line and exits with 0.

When it compiles and is still wrong

A diagnostic tells you that a rule was broken. It cannot tell you that your counter wraps one edge late. For that, the method of this book is always the same: make the model say what it sees, then compare with what you expected.

SymptomWhat to emitChapter
A state machine takes a wrong turn.emit!("state", …) with the state and the inputs, first line of the clocked reaction. Find the first row where the next row surprises you.7
An output has a value you did not expect, and you do not know which input gave it.A reaction on q.changed() that emits the inputs of q. An input that may be absent or undetermined says so in its type, None or x, so the field names the culprit.3, 5
Something happens at the wrong time.t = now() in a reaction on the event itself, or on a crossing with above.1, 8
A record appears that "cannot happen".Emit the values the guard read, with the same names: control takes a bool, so a record has exactly one cause.2, 3
A waveform has the wrong shape.on settle { emit!("wave", …) } with the nodes in question, and plot the fields.6
A sampled value is "one step old".Nothing: a reaction on a clock edge reads the solution from just before the edge. Check the instant of the record.8
The solver fails to converge, or crawls.emit! inside the suspect device's analog block, and read the iterations of the failing step.6
Two things you expected in one microstep are in two.Compare the microstep numbers of their records: each commit is one hop.7, 8

When the surprise is gone, keep the useful prints and turn the expectation into a require, so the next change that breaks it is caught without anyone reading a stream.

The codes you will meet first

CodeWhat happenedUsual fix
E0900A syntax error.Read the token the message names.
E0901A macro other than emit! or include_str!.A function, or a typed emit! record.
E0161A path, use or mod that does not resolve, or a private item.Check spelling, pub, and the dependency name.
E0162A name declared twice in one namespace.Rename one.
E0163A use whose first segment is both a dependency and a local item.package = … rename, or crate::.
E0143A default value in a fn signature, or an overload.A trailing Option<T> parameter.
E0187A signal, an out port or a drive with no first value whose type has no default.Write the first value, or use a type with a default.
E0220A second drive of a one-driver carrier.One writer, or a nature that implements Resolve.
E0221A definition and a commit to the same carrier in one module.Pick = or <-.
E0222Writing something this module may not drive, such as an in port.Drive it from its owner.
E0312A port left unconnected, or too many connections.Match the port list.
E0319A required param not bound.Bind it in { … } or give a default.
E0336A definition reads a probe or now().Use a reaction, or an analog block.
E0339A combinational loop through one-driver carriers.Break it with after.
E0350An instance placed in a fn, closure or reaction body.Name it with a body let and read the name.
E0404A statement in the wrong kind of block, like <- in analog.Move it.
E0414A branch switches kind on a probe.Switch on a param or digital carrier.
E0430The head of on is not an Event.s.changed(), clk.rise(), or a guard ev && c.
E0171A manifest key the format does not allow.See chapter 12.
E0175A package without src/lib.msl.Create the crate root.
E0250A digital carrier connected to an analog port, or the reverse.Place a converter: A2D, D2A.
E0310A Bit port with no in or out, or a direction where none belongs.Add or remove the direction.
E0317A param value that fails its where.Give a value inside the range.
E0338An initial value on a carrier driven by an undelayed definition.Drop the = value.
E0343inst.name reaches an item that is not pub.Publish it in the child.
E0410The left side of +> is not a probe.v(node) +> … or i(a, b) +> …
E0432A commit of the wrong type.Commit a value of the carrier's type.
E0601emit! outside a reaction, analog or a fn.Move it into behavior.
E0602emit! without a literal stream name, or with a field that is not name = value.Name the stream and every field.
E0603A check or finish reached from analog.Call it from a reaction.

The codes that appear only while a simulation runs name the instance and the instant:

CodeWhat happenedUsual cause
E0501The microsteps at one time never go quiet.A feedback loop of commits with no delay.
E0503A solve is singular; the report names the node or branches.A node with no DC path, or a loop of voltage sources.
E0504The step after a digital change does not converge.An ideal edge into a stiff circuit; use transition.
E0505The operating point does not converge.A device with no limiting; use limexp or limit.
E0507A non-finite value in a solve.A division by a quantity that reached zero.
E0508A transient step cannot be completed at the smallest step.A discontinuity in an analog expression.

Try it

Fix the three errors of the worked example, then break it in a new way: declare held without any first value, signal held: Bit<f64>;. Which error do you expect, and what does it name? (E0187, naming held and the trait Default: a real number has no default value, so the first value must be written.)

Chapter 15

The command line

Goal

By the end of this chapter you know every command of piperine, every key of a run table and the flag that overrides it, how results and emit! records are printed and saved, and what each exit code and each of the tool's own error codes means.

You have used piperine run since chapter 1. This chapter is the reference behind those boxes: one program, seven commands, and one idea, the run, which is a top module plus an analysis.

CommandWhat it does
piperine new <name>Creates a project that runs: the RC low-pass of chapter 1.
piperine checkReads, resolves and type-checks the project without simulating.
piperine run [name]Runs one run of the project, or a lone file, and prints records and results.
piperine test [name…]Runs every run and judges its checks: one PASS or FAIL line each.
piperine buildCompiles the top of every run ahead of time.
piperine fmtFormats the sources, keeping every comment.
piperine cleanRemoves what the tool wrote.

piperine --help lists them, and piperine --version prints the version.

A project, its benches and its runs

rc/
├── Piperine.toml          [package], [dependencies], and one [run.] table per run
├── .gitignore             target/
├── src/
│   └── lib.msl            the library crate
├── benches/
│   └── step.msl           a bench: a crate of its own
└── target/                written by the tool; removed by `piperine clean`

This is the project piperine new rc writes, the RC low-pass of chapter 1, file for file. Piperine.toml:

[package]
name = "rc"
version = "0.1.0"

[run.op]
bench = "benches/step.msl"
analysis = "op"

[run.step]
bench = "benches/step.msl"
analysis = "transient"
stop = 5e-6
print_step = 1e-6

src/lib.msl, the parts:

/// Ideal resistor.
pub module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    r: f64 where r > 0.0;
}

analog for Resistor {
    i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r;
}

/// Ideal capacitor.
pub module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    c: f64 where c > 0.0;
}

analog for Capacitor {
    i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n));
}

/// A constant voltage from `p` to `n`.
pub module Vsource(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) {
    dc: f64 = 1.0;
}

analog for Vsource {
    v(p, n) +> dc;
}

benches/step.msl, the circuit with its monitor:

use rc::{Capacitor, Resistor, Vsource};

/// Writes a record when `a` rises through `level`.
module Monitor(a: Electrical) {
    level: f64 = 0.5;
}

digital for Monitor {
    on cross(v(a) - level) {
        emit!("trip", v = v(a));
        require("crossed_upward", v(a) >= level);
    }
}

/// A 1 V source into R and C. `vout` starts at 0 V, so a transient is the step response.
module Step() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal vout: Electrical = 0.0;
    let v1 = Vsource(vin, gnd) { dc: 1.0 };
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c: 1n };
    let mon = Monitor(vout);
}

Before running anything, ask whether the project is sound. piperine check reads every file, resolves every name and type-checks the top of each run:

piperine check
ok: rc (2 crates, 2 runs)

The two crates are the library and std, the standard library every crate depends on. A bench is loaded by the run that names it.

Finding the project. A command uses the nearest Piperine.toml found upward from the directory you are in, so it works anywhere inside a project. --project <dir> names the project directly and searches nowhere else. With no manifest, a command that needs a project fails with E0170.

A bench is a module without ports that places a design with its sources, its stimulus and its monitors. It is MSL, in a file under benches/. Each bench file is the root of a small crate named bench, which depends on your package under the package's name and on each of the package's direct dependencies under theirs. So a bench of the package rc begins with use rc::…, and sees what the library marks pub.

The top. A run simulates one top module. It is the module the run's top key names, or else the one module without ports of the run's root crate: the bench file when the run has a bench, the library when it has none. If there is no such module, or more than one, the run fails with P0011 and the candidates are listed. A top may have digital in ports; they are left undriven and read as unknown. A top with an analog port is refused.

The run table

A run is a table [run.<name>] in Piperine.toml. This one has every key; a real run uses a handful.

[run.step]
bench      = "benches/step.msl"   # the root crate is this file; without it, the library
top        = "Step"               # needed only when the choice is not unique
analysis   = "transient"          # op | dc | ac | transient | tf | pz | sens | pss
stop       = 10e-6                # transient
max_step   = 10e-9                # transient, optional
print_step = 1e-6                 # transient, optional
sweep      = "v1.dc"              # dc: the path of a param
from       = -1.0                 # dc, ac; transient: the first time kept
to         = 0.8                  # dc, ac
step       = 0.05                 # dc
per_decade = 10                   # ac, or:
points     = 41                   # ac, linear
print      = ["vout", "i(v1)"]    # what the table shows
streams    = ["trip", "checks"]   # keep only these record streams
params     = { "r1.r" = 2000.0 }  # set before the analysis
backend    = "rust"               # rust | interp
csv        = "out/step.csv"       # the table, as a file
plot       = "out/step.svg"       # the table, as a picture; not for op
records    = "out/step.jsonl"     # the records, as a file
all_checks = true                 # print the passing checks too
reltol     = 1e-3                 # the solver's options, for every analysis:
abstol     = 1e-12                #   tolerances,
vntol      = 1e-6
trtol      = 7.0
temp       = 27.0                 #   the temperature, in degrees Celsius,
resolution = 1e-15                #   the digital engine's tick,
maxrounds  = 20                   #   and the bounds the solver gives up at
maxmicro   = 1000
max_steps  = 2000000
nodeset    = { "vout" = 0.5 }     # op, dc: the first guess of the operating point
input      = "v1"                 # tf, pz: the source that excites
output     = "vout"               # tf, pz: the node that is measured
wrt        = ["r1.r"]             # sens: the params to differentiate by
period     = 4e-6                 # pss
tstab      = 8e-6                 # pss: the transient before the orbit

That listing shows the keys side by side; it is not a run you could execute, because each analysis reads only its own numbers and a key outside its analysis is an error. Numbers are TOML numbers: write 10e-6, not 10u.

KeyFlagRead byMeaning
bench--benchallThe bench file, relative to the project.
top--topallThe top module, by name.
analysis--analysisallop, dc, ac, noise, transient, tf, pz, sens or pss. Required.
stop--stoptransientThe end time, in seconds. Required, greater than 0.
max_step--max-steptransientThe largest time step the solver may take.
print_step--print-steptransientOne table row per multiple of it, instead of one per solver step.
from--fromtransientThe first time the result keeps, 0 by default. The solver still runs from 0; steps before it are computed and not kept.
sweep--sweepdcThe param to step, by path.
from, to--from, --todc, ac, noiseThe ends of the sweep: param values, or frequencies in hertz.
step--stepdcThe increment: points are from + k · step while not past to.
per_decade--per-decadeac, noiseA logarithmic grid with this many points per decade.
points--pointsac, noiseA linear grid with this many points in all. Exactly one of the two.
print--print, repeatableallThe signals of the table, in order.
streams--stream, repeatableallThe record streams to keep.
params--param path=value, repeatableallParams to set before the analysis.
backend--backendallrust or interp.
csv--csvallWrite the table to this file.
plot--plotdc, ac, transientWrite the result as an SVG picture.
records--recordsallWrite the records to this file.
all_checks--all-checksallPrint the passing checks records too. A failing check always prints.
reltol--reltolallThe relative tolerance of the solver, 1e-3 by default.
abstol--abstolallThe absolute tolerance of a current, in amperes.
vntol--vntolallThe absolute tolerance of a voltage, in volts, 1e-6 by default.
trtol--trtoltransientThe factor on the truncation error a step may have and be accepted, 7 by default.
temp--tempallThe temperature of the run, in degrees Celsius; 27 by default.
tnom--tnomallThe nominal temperature the models' parameters are given at, in degrees Celsius; 27 by default. It does not move temp.
resolution--resolutionallThe tick of the digital engine, in seconds.
maxrounds--maxroundsallThe most rounds an operating point may take before it fails, 20 by default.
maxmicro--maxmicroallThe most microsteps one instant of the digital engine may take before it fails.
max_steps--max-stepstransient, pssThe budget of attempts a transient may make before it fails naming it, 2000000 by default.
simparam--simparam name=value, repeatableallA session parameter: a name and a number. A name that is an option, such as reltol or tnom, is refused.
nodeset--nodeset node=value, repeatableop, dcSeeds the first guess of an operating point. A flag replaces the table's entry of the same node.
input, output--input, --outputtf, pz, noiseThe voltage source that excites, by instance, and the node that is measured. A noise run requires output, a node, v(a, b) or i(instance), and refers the noise to input when it is given.
wrt--wrt, repeatablesensA param to differentiate by, by path.
period--periodpssThe period of the drive, in seconds. Required.
tstab--tstabpssThe length of the transient that comes before the orbit, 0 by default.

Three ways to ask for a run

piperine run step                          # the run named step, as the manifest describes it
piperine run step --stop 20e-6 --stream trip    # the same run, two settings changed
piperine run benches/delayed.msl --analysis transient --stop 5e-6    # a file, described by flags alone
  • By name. With exactly one run in the manifest the name may be left out. With none or several, or a name that is no run, the command lists the run names and exits with status 2.
  • With flags. Every key is a flag, and a flag wins over the key of the same setting. Nothing is written back to the manifest.
  • A lone file. A file run by its path needs at least --analysis. Where the file is decides what it is: with no Piperine.toml above it, it is a crate by itself; inside a project but outside src/, it is a bench of that project; under src/ it is refused, because it is already part of the library. The header shows the file's name without its extension as the run's name.

What a run prints

The project has two runs. The operating point first:

piperine run op
run op: op, top Step, backend rust

signal     value
Step.vin   1.000000e0
Step.vout  1.000000e0

Then the step response:

piperine run step
run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

Always in this order: a header line, run <name>: <analysis>, top <Top>, backend <backend>; the record lines, each printed as its record is written; one empty line; the table. If anything fails before the analysis starts, nothing is printed here at all, only the error. If the analysis fails midway, the records already printed stay and no table follows.

The shape of the table depends on the analysis:

AnalysisRowsColumns
opone per printed signalsignal, value
dcone per point of the sweep; each point sets the param and solves an operating point. A point of a sweep has no time to stamp a record with, so a dc run of a design that writes records is refused with P0010; run op at each value insteadthe swept param's full path, then one per signal
acone per frequencyfreq, then mag(<path>) and phase(<path>) per signal, the phase in degrees
transientone per accepted solver step, or one per print_steptime, then one per signal
tfthree: gain, input_impedance, output_impedancequantity, value
pzone per pole and zerokind, real, imag
sensone per wrt paramparam, then d(<path>) per signal
pssone per print_step across one period of the orbittime, then one per signal

With print_step, an analog value is interpolated linearly between the two solver steps around the row's time, and a digital value is the one it held at that time. Use it whenever a run takes many steps: the solver still computes all of its steps, but the table, the plot and the memory of the run hold only the rows of the grid.

A long run with many unknowns would hold a great deal if it kept every step, so it does not: a run writes each row of its table as the solver reaches it and keeps none of the steps, which makes the memory of a run the size of its table, however long the run is. from trims the start of that table: the solver integrates from 0 as always, and the table starts at from. Records are not affected; every one prints. With print_step too, the first row is the first multiple of print_step at or after from, interpolated between the solver steps around it. The buck converter at the end of this chapter prints its whole start on a grid, with no from at all.

What you can print. A node gives its voltage, a digital signal its value, and i(<instance>) the current through an instance such as a source. Each is named by its path, written in full or relative to the top: vout and Step.vout are the same signal. A column is always headed by the full path. With no print, the table shows the top module's own signals and ports, in the order they are declared.

How values are written. A real has six decimals and an exponent, 7.500000e0; a bool is true or false; an integer is decimal; a four-state Logic is its characters, 0, 1, x and z, highest level first, as in 10xz; an absent Option is None; a text field is in double quotes. The same input gives the same characters every time: nothing on this output depends on the clock, on your directories or on what was cached.

Records: your emit!, live

emit! is the print statement of MSL, and the command line is where its output lands. The monitor of the RC bench has one in its reaction:

digital for Monitor {
    on cross(v(a) - level) {
        emit!("trip", v = v(a));
        require("crossed_upward", v(a) >= level);
    }
}

Every emit! executed by a reaction prints one line, the moment the record is written:

@   = = …

For the monitor that is this line of piperine run step: the time of the crossing, the instance mon under the top Step, the stream trip and the one field v.

run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

The fields come in the order you wrote them. Lines appear in the order the records are written, never one from a later time before one from an earlier time, and all of them before the table. The two reserved streams, sim and checks, print like any other, with one exception: a checks record whose pass is true is not printed unless you give --all-checks (or all_checks = true in the run table), because a monitor that checks every clock edge would bury everything else. A failing check, or one whose pass is unknown, always prints. The instance shown is the one whose reaction made the call. A design with no emit! prints no record line.

piperine run step --all-checks
run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon checks kind="require" name="crossed_upward" pass=true
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

At one instant the records come in the order the solver delivers them, not necessarily the order of the statements: here the check, written second, prints first. To print the stream you are debugging and nothing else, filter it:

You wantWrite
one stream, wherever it is written--stream trip
one stream of one instance--stream Step.mon.trip
several--stream trip --stream checks, or the streams key
everything in a file, nothing on the terminal--records step.jsonl
the passing checks too--all-checks
piperine run step --stream trip
run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1

A records file is JSON Lines: one object per record, with the keys time, instance, stream and fields. A real or an integer is a JSON number, a bool a JSON boolean, a text a string, a four-state Logic a string of its characters, and an absent Option the text None. A real is written with every digit it has, so a script reads back exactly the number the solver computed. The file holds every record, the passing checks included:

piperine run step --records step.jsonl
{"time":6.931070840000001e-7,"instance":"Step.mon","stream":"checks","fields":{"kind":"require","name":"crossed_upward","pass":true}}
{"time":6.931070840000001e-7,"instance":"Step.mon","stream":"trip","fields":{"v":0.5000000118411609}}

Tables and plots as files

  • --csv file writes the table as CSV: a header row with the column names, cells separated by commas, numbers written as on the terminal. In place of the table the command prints wrote <file> (<n> rows).
  • --plot file.svg writes one SVG picture and prints wrote <file> after the table. The horizontal axis is the time, the swept param or the frequency. Every printed real signal is a curve on one shared axis, with a legend; every digital signal is a step trace in a lane of its own below. An ac run gets two panels, magnitude in dB over phase in degrees, with a logarithmic frequency axis when the grid is per_decade. A curve breaks where a value is unknown. An operating point has nothing to plot.
  • Paths. A relative csv or records path is taken from the project directory, or from the directory you are in for a lone file. An existing file is replaced.

Here is the step response of the RC project as a picture:

piperine run step --plot step.svg
run step: transient, top Step, backend rust
@6.931071e-7 Step.mon trip v=5.000000e-1

time         Step.vin    Step.vout
0.000000e0   1.000000e0  0.000000e0
1.000000e-6  1.000000e0  6.320928e-1
2.000000e-6  1.000000e0  8.646682e-1
3.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.502193e-1
4.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.816886e-1
5.000000e-6  1.000000e0  9.932653e-1
wrote step.svg

This is the file it wrote, as it is: Step.vin flat at 1 V, Step.vout rising towards it with the time constant of 1 µs. The plot draws the rows of the run's table: its six printed points, one per row.

Params for one run

A param of any instance can be set before the analysis, by its path:

piperine run step --param r1.r=2200 --param c1.c=2.2e-9

The entries of the params key are applied first, then the flags, in the order given. A path that names nothing is E0801. A value the design refuses, such as one that fails the param's where, is E0802: no analysis runs. The same paths serve --sweep in a DC sweep.

The solver's options

Tolerances, the temperature and the bounds the solver gives up at are keys of a run too, each with its flag (table above). They carry the names ngspice gives its .options, in lowercase. A run that sets none behaves as it always has. nodeset seeds the first guess of an operating point; the iteration converges on the same point whatever the guess when the circuit has one, so a seed matters for a circuit that has several. Save this as lowpass.msl, a divider with a capacitor, anywhere:

pub module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

pub module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c >= 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }

pub module Vsource(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { dc: f64 = 1.0; }
analog for Vsource { v(p, n) +> dc; }

module Lowpass() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal vout: Electrical;
    let v1 = Vsource(vin, gnd) { dc: 10.0 };
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let r2 = Resistor(vout, gnd) { r: 3k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c: 1u };
}
piperine run lowpass.msl --analysis op --reltol 1e-9 --vntol 1e-12 --nodeset vout=5 --print vout
run lowpass: op, top Lowpass, backend rust

signal        value
Lowpass.vout  7.500000e0

A nodeset seeds the operating point of every analysis, ac and transient included, which take their own inside the solver: where the circuit has more than one equilibrium, the run reaches the one the seed picks. A transient that needs more attempts than max_steps fails naming the budget; raise it and the same run succeeds.

Four more analyses

Beyond op, dc, ac and transient, a run can ask for the small-signal transfer function, the poles and zeros, the sensitivity of a node to params, and the periodic steady state. Each reads its own keys, and the table has its own shape (the table of shapes above).

tf excites a voltage source and measures a node: the gain, the input resistance seen by the source and the output resistance seen at the node. The source is named by its instance, v1 or in full, Lowpass.v1; a source that is not a voltage source is a P0010 error.

piperine run lowpass.msl --analysis tf --input v1 --output vout
run lowpass: tf, top Lowpass, backend rust

quantity          value
gain              7.500000e-1
input_impedance   4.000000e3
output_impedance  7.500000e2

The divider has gain 3/4, and the source sees 1 kΩ in series with 3 kΩ. pz takes the same two keys and lists the poles and zeros of that transfer function, in rad/s; the capacitor makes one pole, at −(1/R1 + 1/R2)/C:

piperine run lowpass.msl --analysis pz --input v1 --output vout
run lowpass: pz, top Lowpass, backend rust

kind  real         imag
pole  -1.333333e3  0.000000e0

sens gives the derivative of each printed signal with respect to each param named by wrt, at the operating point:

piperine run lowpass.msl --analysis sens --wrt r1.r --wrt r2.r --print vout
run lowpass: sens, top Lowpass, backend rust

param         d(Lowpass.vout)
Lowpass.r1.r  -1.875000e-3
Lowpass.r2.r  6.250000e-4

Raising r1 by one ohm lowers the output by 1.875 mV, and raising r2 by one ohm raises it by 0.625 mV: the output is 10·R2/(R1+R2), and the numbers are its two partial derivatives.

pss finds the periodic steady state of a circuit driven with period period. A transient of length tstab runs first, then the orbit is iterated until it repeats; the table is one period of it. Save a square wave over an RC as pulse.msl:

pub module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

pub module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c >= 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }

module Pulse(p: Electrical) {
    amp: f64 = 1.0;
    width: f64 = 2u;
    period: f64 = 4u;
    edge: f64 = 1p;
}
analog for Pulse {
    let ph = now() - period * (now() / period).floor();
    let level = if ph < edge { ph / edge }
        else if ph < width { 1.0 }
        else if ph < width + edge { 1.0 - (ph - width) / edge }
        else { 0.0 };
    v(p) +> amp * level;
}

module Steady() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal vout: Electrical;
    let src = Pulse(vin);
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c: 1n };
}
piperine run pulse.msl --analysis pss --period 4e-6 --tstab 8e-6 --max-step 1e-9 --print-step 1e-6 --print vout
run pulse: pss, top Steady, backend rust

time         Steady.vout
8.000000e-6  1.192517e-1
9.000000e-6  6.759907e-1
1.000000e-5  8.808037e-1
1.100000e-5  3.241597e-1
1.200000e-5  1.192517e-1

The first and last rows are equal: the circuit is back where the period began. The source's edges are a picosecond wide, so max_step is what keeps the solver from stepping over them.

Describing a design: piperine info

piperine info [<run>] elaborates the top of a run and prints what a host can read of it, simulating and compiling nothing: the params with their type, value, where condition and whether they are structural; the ports; and the record streams. With no run named it describes the only run, or the first when there are several; a lone .msl file works too. A design that does not elaborate gets the diagnostics piperine check prints, and exit 1.

piperine info lowpass.msl
info lowpass: top Lowpass, backend rust

params
path           type  value        where       structural
Lowpass.v1.dc  f64   1.000000e1   -           false
Lowpass.r1.r   f64   1.000000e3   (r > 0.0)   false
Lowpass.r2.r   f64   3.000000e3   (r > 0.0)   false
Lowpass.c1.c   f64   1.000000e-6  (c >= 0.0)  false

ports
name  direction  type

streams
stream  fields

The top of lowpass.msl has no ports and writes no records, so those two tables are empty.

Two backends

A run executes on one of two backends, and the header says which. rust, the default, turns the design into Rust and compiles it; it runs everything. interp interprets the design directly and starts at once, for analog designs; given a design it does not run, it says so and names --backend rust. For a design both run, the two are expected to print the same table.

The choice is made by the first of these that is present: the flag --backend, the environment variable PIPERINE_BACKEND, the run's backend key; otherwise rust.

The Rust backend needs a Rust compiler installed. The first run of a design compiles it and says so on the error output; the result is cached, and a run never compiles during an analysis.

The other commands

piperine new <name> creates the directory <name> with Piperine.toml, src/lib.msl, benches/step.msl and .gitignore, and prints created <name>. The name must be a valid package name: an identifier, no hyphens. It never writes into a directory that exists. In the new project, check, run step and test all succeed.

piperine check resolves the dependencies, parses and resolves every crate, loads the bench of every run, and type-checks and elaborates the top of every run, without running a backend. It ends with ok: <package> (<c> crates, <r> runs), or with every diagnostic of every run. A project with no run has no top to check types from, and the line says so: 0 runs; type checking needs a run. piperine check file.msl checks a lone file.

piperine build compiles the top of every run on its backend, so that the first run starts at once. It prints one line per run:

built : compiled
built : cached
built : interp, nothing to compile

In a new RC project, with nothing compiled yet:

piperine build
built op: compiled
built step: cached

The second run is cached although nothing was compiled for it before: a compiled design depends on the design, not on the analysis, and both runs elaborate the same top, Step, into the same design. One compile serves both.

With --verbose each compiled or cached line is followed by the path of the generated Rust source, which is yours to read: it is what actually runs.

piperine test executes every run of the project in the order of the manifest, or the runs you name, and judges them by their checks records. It prints no table and no other record.

piperine test
PASS op (0 checks)
PASS step (1 checks)
2 runs, 2 passed, 0 failed

The operating point has no check to judge, and passes with zero. The step's check is counted although piperine run step did not print it: the terminal hides a passing check, the verdict never does. piperine test step judges that one run only.

LineWhen
PASS <name> (<n> checks)The run completed and every check passed.
FAIL <name> (<k> of <n> checks failed)A check's pass was false. The line of each such record follows.
FAIL <name> (fatal at <t>: <msg>)The model called fatal: the run ended at that instant and fails with the message. A finish ends a run too, and does not fail it.
FAIL <name> (did not run)The run did not complete; the error is on the error output.
<r> runs, <p> passed, <f> failedAlways the last line.

A target is an objective that reports and never fails. After the line of a run, piperine test prints one line per target, target <name> margin=<value>, holding the last margin the run wrote, in the order the targets first wrote one; a negative margin is shown and does not turn the run red. A require is the constraint that fails.

piperine fmt rewrites in place every .msl file under src/ and benches/ whose layout differs from the standard one, and prints formatted <path> for each; given file names, it formats those only. Every comment you wrote is kept, in its place. piperine fmt --check writes nothing, prints would format <path> for each file that differs and fails if there is one, which is the line for a CI job. A file that does not parse is reported and left untouched.

piperine clean removes the project's target directory and prints removed target, or nothing to remove. With --cache it also removes the cache of compiled designs, which is shared by all your projects. It removes nothing else.

A bigger project: the buck converter

The RC project is a toy. The same commands run a full-size design: a digitally controlled buck converter that steps 12 V down to 0.8 V, with an analog power stage, a 200 MHz digital PWM, an ADC, a fixed-point PID, a soft start and a supervisor, all in MSL. It is the bigger sibling of the converter of chapter 10, and a package like any other: the library buck under src/, one bench file, and two runs.

The bench, benches/startup.msl, plays the host that brings the converter up: it writes the setpoint, then raises en. A second bench in the same file holds en low.

//! The closed-loop start of the buck converter, as two benches over the package: `Startup`
//! writes the setpoint code 0 (0.8 V) and then raises `en`, with Vin 12 V into the 6.6 ohm load
//! of `Buck`'s defaults; `Idle` never raises `en`, so the supervisor stays `Off`.

use buck::dpwm::Osc;

use buck::top::Buck;

use buck::{Fault, Mode};

/// The host's two writes, on a 1 MHz clock (rising at 0.5 us, 1.5 us, ...): `sp_wr` rises at the
/// first edge with `sp_data` 0 already presented (the setpoint register takes it on the rising
/// `wr`), and `en` rises at the second.
module Host(in clk: Bit, out sp_wr: Bit = false, out en: Bit = false) {
    signal n: Bit = 0;
}

digital for Host {
    on clk.rise() {
        let s = n;
        if s < 2 {
            n <- s + 1;
        }
        if s == 0 {
            sp_wr <- true;
        }
        if s == 1 {
            en <- true;
        }
    }
}

/// The start: 128 samples of soft-start ramp, 64 of power-good delay, then regulation.
module Startup() {
    signal clk: Bit;
    signal en: Bit;
    signal sp_wr: Bit;
    signal sp_data: Bit = 0;
    signal load_step: Bit = false;
    signal inject: Bit = false;
    signal pgood: Bit;
    signal fault: Bit;
    signal mode: Bit;
    let osc = Osc(clk) { f: 1M };
    let host = Host(clk, sp_wr, en);
    pub let buck = Buck(en, sp_wr, sp_data, load_step, inject, pgood, fault, mode);
}

/// The converter with `en` held low: no start, the supervisor's mode is `Off` throughout.
module Idle() {
    signal en: Bit = false;
    signal sp_wr: Bit = false;
    signal sp_data: Bit = 0;
    signal load_step: Bit = false;
    signal inject: Bit = false;
    signal pgood: Bit;
    signal fault: Bit;
    signal mode: Bit;
    pub let buck = Buck(en, sp_wr, sp_data, load_step, inject, pgood, fault, mode);
}

The run startup, the last table of the package's Piperine.toml:

# The closed-loop start, 600 us: the reference ramps to code 128 (0.8 V). The run prints the
# whole start on a 0.5 us grid (1 201 rows, time 0 to 600 us); it records those rows as it
# goes and keeps no step, so its memory does not grow with the length of the run.
[run.startup]
bench = "benches/startup.msl"
top = "Startup"
analysis = "transient"
stop = 600e-6
max_step = 1e-8
print = ["Startup.buck.ctl.reference", "Startup.mode", "Startup.buck.vout"]
print_step = 0.5e-6

Two keys deserve a word, and one is missing on purpose. top is needed because the bench file has two modules without ports. print names three signals deep in the hierarchy by their full paths. And there is no from: the run records the whole start, 0 to 600 µs, on its 0.5 µs grid. It can, because the run writes each row of the table as the solver reaches it and keeps none of the converter's steps (each one would hold every unknown of the circuit), so the memory stays constant however long the start.

5.995000e-4  128                         Regulate      8.031299e-1
6.000000e-4  128                         Regulate      8.025999e-1

The first time, the command compiles the converter, which takes a while, and says so on the error output. Then it simulates 600 µs of a circuit switching at 500 kHz with a 200 MHz clock, a couple of minutes on a laptop. The table has 1 201 rows, one per 0.5 µs from time 0 to 600 µs; the listing shows the first five, where the supervisor is still off, the reference is 0 and the output is at its initial rest. The last row, at 600 µs, reads reference 128, the code of 0.8 V; mode Regulate, after Off and SoftStart; and an output within 3 mV of 0.8 V (0.8026). Add --csv start.csv to keep all the rows in a file, or --plot start.svg to draw them.

There are no record lines, yet the converter checks itself at every clock: its PWM carries a monitor that writes a checks record at every edge of the 200 MHz clock, saying the two power switches are never on together. Over the whole start that is more than a hundred thousand records, all passing, so the terminal hides them. piperine test runs both runs of the package and counts every one of them; --records startup.jsonl keeps them in a file.

Output, errors and exit codes

Results and records go to the standard output. Diagnostics, errors and progress go to the standard error. So a pipe or a redirection gets data only:

piperine run step > step.txt
Exit codeMeaning
0Done.
1The design, the run or a test failed: a diagnostic, a solver error, a failed check, a file that could not be written.
2The command line is wrong: an unknown command or flag, a flag given twice, a run name that does not exist, no --analysis for a lone file.

Errors in your sources are the codes of chapter 14, rendered the same way: code, message, file, line and the source excerpt, followed by the stage that failed. The tool adds two codes of its own for the run request, and uses two host codes when a path or a value is refused:

Common mistakes

CodeYou wroteWhy, and the fix
P0010stopp = 1e-3 in a run tableAn unknown key. The report points at it in Piperine.toml: stop.
P0010stop = "5u"A value of the wrong type. Numbers are TOML numbers: stop = 5e-6.
P0010analysis = "tran", or no analysisOne of op, dc, ac, transient.
P0010a transient run with no stopGive the end time, greater than 0.
P0010analysis = "op" with stop = 1e-3, or with plotA key its analysis does not read. Remove it, or change the analysis.
P0010per_decade and points together, or neitherAn AC grid is exactly one of the two.
P0010bench = "benches/stepp.msl"The file does not exist.
P0010piperine run src/lib.msl --analysis opA file under src/ is part of the library. Run a bench, or a run of the project.
P0010a dc run of a design that writes recordsA point of a sweep has no time to stamp a record with. Run op at each value, or a transient.
P0011a bench file with two modules without ports, and no topNo single top can be chosen. Name it: top = "Step" or --top Step.
E0801--print vuot, --stream tripp, --param r9.r=1kThe path names nothing in the design. It is reported before any analysis runs.
E0802--param r1.r=-5The design refuses the value: the param's where r > 0.0 fails.
E0170piperine run step outside any projectNo Piperine.toml was found upward. Change directory, or pass --project.

Good to know

The command line does four analyses and one top at a time, with the stimulus written in the bench. Sweeps over many runs, Monte Carlo, optimization and stimulus decided while a run is going are a host program's work, in Rust, on the same library the command is built on (chapter 21).

Try it

In the project of chapter 1, add a run [run.bode] that sweeps the filter from 1 kHz to 10 MHz with 10 points per decade and plots it. (The source needs a small-signal stimulus: v(p, n) +> dc + ac_stim(1.0, 0.0);, chapter 8. The table needs analysis = "ac", from = 1e3, to = 1e7, per_decade = 10, print = ["vout"] and plot = "bode.svg".) At which frequency should mag(Step.vout) read 0.707 and phase(Step.vout) read −45? (At 1 / (2π · r · c) = 159 kHz.)

Chapter 16

Cheat sheet

One screen of syntax. The rules of the value language are in chapters 2 to 4, and each hardware form has its own chapter.

Nature

nature Bit { value: T }
nature Electrical {
    potential: Voltage { access: v, units: "V", abstol: 1u },
    flow:      Current { access: i, units: "A", abstol: 1p },
}

Module, params, signals

module M(a: Electrical, in clk: Bit,
        out q: Bit = 0, x: f64) where N >= 1 {
    r: f64 = 1k where r > 0.0;     // param
    rs: Option;               // may be left out
    signal mid: Electrical;
    signal s: [Bit; N];
}

Body items

let r1 = Resistor(a, mid) { r: 1k };   // instance
let half = r / 2.0;                    // named expression
y = a & b;                             // definition
q = !(r | qn) after tpd;               // delayed definition
for k in 0..N { pub let rk = Resistor(a, mid) { r: 1k }; }   // generation

Analog

analog for M {
    i(a, b) +> v(a, b) / r;            // flow
    v(vout) +> gain * v(inp);           // potential
    i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n));
    bound_step(1n);
}

Digital

digital for M {
    on clk.rise() { q <- d; }
    on rst_n.fall() || clk.rise() { q <- false; }
    on at(t0) && en { q <- true; q <- false after w; }
    on settle { require("hi", v(p, n) <= hi); }
}

Crossing

module M(a: Electrical, o: Electrical, in ctl: Bit) {
    let y = transition(level, 0.0, tr, tf);
}
analog for M {
    v(o) +> held;                      // analog reads digital
    if ctl { bound_step(1n); }
}
digital for M {
    on above(v(a) - vth) { d <- true; }    // digital reads analog
}

Debug prints

digital for M {
    on clk.rise() { emit!("state", t = now(), q = q); }           // one row per edge
    on cross(v(a) - vth) { emit!("cross", t = now()); }           // a located instant
    on q.changed() { emit!("changed", d = d, q = q); }          // a change, with its inputs
    on settle { emit!("wave", t = now(), va = v(a)); }            // one row per time point
}
analog for M { emit!("newton", va = v(a)); }                      // every iteration: a trace

Templates and extern

cell: C;                               // C: Fn(Cell)
let c0 = cell(Cell { wl: w, bl: b });
let m1 = nch(0.13u, 2u)(d, g, s, b);
#[link("bsim4.osdi", model = "bsim4")]
extern "osdi" module Bsim4(d: Electrical, g: Electrical, s: Electrical, b: Electrical) { l: f64 = 1u; }

Where names come from

Core, no import: gnd Nature m dtemp str, the probes, ddt delay limit noise cross at, bound_step, emit! include_str!.

Library, implied by std::prelude: std::constants std::carriers std::values std::analog std::nets std::convert std::monitor std::io.

Yours hides both, except a core name (E0457). Chapter 13.

Files and data

mod logic;                  // logic.msl or logic/mod.msl
use util::filters::Rc;      // dependency name
use crate::logic::Sar;
const IV: [(f64, f64); 64] = parse_table(include_str!("iv.tbl"));
emit!("log", msg = "overrange", vin = v(inp));

Command line
