Part 2
In depth
How Piperine works underneath: the object model, the device ABI, the solver and its options, the compiler, and the crates. Most models are written without opening this part.

Chapter 17

The object model

Goal

By the end of this chapter you know what a tool sees when it reads your source, and you can ask a design questions from Rust: which ports a module has, where a signal is declared, and who writes it.

Everything so far was about writing models. This chapter is about reading them by program. You need it when you build a tool, and it helps even when you do not, because it is the precise version of a question you ask all the time while debugging: "who drives this signal?"

The Piperine Object Model, the POM, is the in-memory model of a set of MSL sources. One call turns source text into an immutable Design, and every later stage starts from it: a type checker, an elaborator, the simulator front end, an editor. It is the Rust library piperine_lang::pom, and it reads the way Java reflection does. You ask a module for its ports, a signal for the module it is declared in, an instance for the module it places.

The promise: it mirrors your source

The POM's navigability mirrors the source. What you read back from a Design is the structure the author wrote.

Modules, instances and hierarchy come back as you wrote them. A generation for stays one for, never unrolled; a let stays a let. Name resolution only adds links between nodes. Anything that needs another shape, such as flattening, is a separate artifact built from the design. You never have to reverse-engineer an elaborator to find your own module.

sources ──► parse ──► lower ──► resolve ──► index ──► Design
(text)      syntax    arenas    names,       reverse   (immutable,
            tree      of nodes  classes,     tables    Send + Sync,
                                members                serializable)

A short tour

Take Front, a comparator with hysteresis that drives a flip-flop:

/// A comparator with hysteresis that drives a flip-flop.
module Front(vin: Electrical, in clk: Bit, out q: Bit) {
    vth: f64 = 0.6;
    hyst: f64 = 50m;
    signal above_th: Bit = false;        // state: a signal
    signal d: Bit;
    d = above_th;                        // structure: a combinational definition
}

digital for Front {
    on above(v(vin) - vth - hyst / 2.0) { above_th <- true; }    // an analog event
    on above(vth - hyst / 2.0 - v(vin)) { above_th <- false; }
    on clk.rise() { q <- d; }                                    // a digital event
}

With the design built, you ask it questions in Rust. These lines are from the POM's own tour:

// The structure is what was written, by name.
let front = design.module("Front").unwrap();
let ports: Vec<_> = front.ports().iter().map(|p| p.name().unwrap()).collect();
assert_eq!(ports, ["vin", "clk", "q"]);
assert_eq!(front.params().len(), 2);
assert_eq!(front.signals().len(), 2);

// A signal knows where it is declared.
let d = front.signal("d").unwrap();
assert_eq!(d.declared_in().unwrap().name(), Some("Front"));
assert_eq!(d.qualified_name(), "Front.d");

// The `above(..)` calls are instances of a module, and the behavior block belongs to `Front`.
assert_eq!(front.instances().len(), 2);
let block = &front.digital_blocks()[0];
let events: Vec<_> = block.reactions().iter().map(|r| r.event().text()).collect();
assert_eq!(events[2], "clk.rise()");

// Reverse queries: who writes `d`, and who writes `q`.
assert_eq!(d.writers().len(), 1);                        // the definition `d = above_th;`
assert_eq!(front.port("q").unwrap().writers().len(), 1); // the commit `q <- d;`

What it is and is not

  • Always builds. build() returns a Design however broken the input. Parse errors keep the recovered tree and go to diagnostics(), sorted by file, offset and code.
  • Lossless. Every file keeps its text and syntax tree, and the tree can be rebuilt from the POM alone, spans included.
  • A plain value. A Design is Clone, comparable with ==, Send + Sync and serializable to JSON.
  • No type checking, evaluation, elaboration or simulation. Constants and where clauses stay expressions; generics are not instantiated. The POM says what was written and what each name refers to.
  • No mutation. Edit the source and build again.

With packages, a design is built over a graph of crates, each kept as authored, and names resolve across crates by the rules of chapter 12.

Common mistakes

You expectedWhat the object model does
A generation for over 8 cells gives 8 instances to walk.It gives one for with one placement inside, as written. The unrolled list belongs to elaboration.
build() fails on a syntax error.It always returns a Design. Check diagnostics() before you trust the answers.
A param shows its value.It shows its default expression. Nothing is evaluated here.
A type error is reported.The object model resolves names; it does not check types. An E0432 or E0417 comes from a later stage.

Try it

For the Front module above, predict front.signal("above_th").unwrap().writers().len(). (Two: one commit in each of the first two reactions.)

Chapter 18

Device ABI

Everything the solver simulates is a device, and every device meets the solver through one binary interface, the device ABI. A resistor compiled from MSL, a BSIM model compiled to OSDI, an AVR microcontroller emulator, the IR interpreter running a model nobody compiled: the solver calls all of them the same way. You never write against it when you write MSL; the compiler does. This chapter is for when you want to know what your module becomes, or to plug in a device that was not written in MSL.

Status

Built. The ABI lives in the crate piperine-abi, which has no dependencies and is the only crate a device links against. The solver calls every device through it; the old per-kind device interfaces are gone. Its design was settled by an adversarial review: a builder and a critic went four rounds, and its elegance score rose from 5.5 to 8.1 with no blocker left.

Why one ABI

A simulator usually grows one interface per kind of model: one for compiled analog models, one for digital primitives, one for co-simulation, each with its own hooks for setup, temperature, checkpoints and rollback. Piperine has one. The solver calls every device through the same pointers, whether the library came from dlopen (generated Rust, C, an OSDI adapter, an emulator) or lives in the same process (the IR interpreter, a test element, a host stimulus). A bug in coupling is fixed once, for all of them.

The idea, in five points

  1. A device is a shape plus two kinds of code. The shape is plain data describing the instance. The code is functions, which are pure (bytes in, bytes out, with partial derivatives), and reactions, which run at an instant and whose only effect is a commit.
  2. The device has no memory. Everything that exists over time lives in the solver: unknowns, discrete values, pending commits, integrator and delay histories, limiter iterates. State a device needs is a private pin, just as state in MSL is a signal. So checkpoint, rollback and replay need no entry point.
  3. A pin is the only shared thing. A pin is a net seen from an instance, and it obeys the net rules without exception. Ports, internal nodes, branch flows, signals, state, params, temperature, timers, crossings, settle, records: all pins.
  4. Four analog cells. What is not a net is an input or an output of a call. A term is a value added to a row, through one of three operators: 1, d/dt or delay. A held input is a value the solver supplies and perturbs (a limiter iterate, a noise source, an AC stimulus). An ambient input is a coordinate of the call (the time, the frequency, the horizon). A watch is an analog output that enters no row (a crossing, a step cap, an operating-point variable).
  5. One rule runs all code. A function runs when the solver needs one of its outputs and one of its inputs changed. A reaction runs when an event pin it listens to fires.

The device says only what a value is. A capacitor says "this is a charge"; equilibrium, TR-BDF2, the truncation test, jω·c in an AC sweep and the history across an undone step are the solver's business.

Who owns what

One event owner dispatches to an analog and a digital sub-solver, and the boundary between them is events only. Devices sit below both.

flowchart TB
  H["Host
(a reaction)"]
  O["Event owner
discrete store, queue,
records, checkpoints"]
  A["Analog sub-solver
unknowns, rows,
histories, limit iterates"]
  D["Digital sub-solver
discrete nets, microsteps"]
  H -- "commits" --> O
  O -- "boundary events" --> A
  A -- "a crossing fires an event pin" --> O
  O -- "instants (t, n)" --> D
  subgraph DEV["Devices: shape + code, no memory"]
    AF["analog functions"]
    DF["discrete functions"]
    R["reactions"]
  end
  A -- "eval" --> AF
  D -- "eval" --> DF
  D -- "react" --> R
  R -- "commit" --> O

The analog side keeps its own copy of the discrete pins its functions read, and that copy changes only through a boundary event. A located crossing is just a commit to the watch's event pin.

Six entry points

EntrySideContract
piperine_abi_1() -> *const LibrarydeviceThe one exported symbol; 1 is the ABI major.
create(module, values, note) -> CreateddeviceAn instance and its shape, for these param values.
destroy(instance)deviceFrees the instance and releases its shape.
eval(instance, k, frame) -> StatusdeviceRuns function k: reads its inputs, writes its outputs and the partials that were asked for.
react(instance, k, frame) -> StatusdeviceRuns reaction k: reads its inputs, calls commit.
commit(statement, offset, len, bytes, after) -> StatussolverQueues one write of the calling reaction. Nothing else calls it, and it does nothing else.

What a pin holds

A pin holds the bytes of its type and nothing else: no byte beside a value says whether the value is established. This contract is still major 1 and is unreleased: until the first release it changes in place, symbol piperine_abi_1 and minor 0 included, and a compiled model is always rebuilt against the contract it is loaded by, never loaded against another. The layouts follow from that:

A pin of typeLayout
bool (BIT)One field, v: BOOL at offset 0: size 1, alignment 1. BYTE is v: U8 and F64 is v: F64, size 8.
Option<T>A BOOL presence field, then the layout of T. A param that may be left out is such a pin.
Logic<N>Two fields of the leaf of UInt<N>: binary at offset 0 and value after it. Two bits per level, and no byte for a flag.
a drive of a resolved netThe layout of the type driven, then a driving: BOOL field: 0 is a drive that is absent from what resolve receives, 1 a present one. The value pin has the layout of the nature's value type.

There is one commit, and a field is { name, leaf, offset, count }.

A call returns OK, FAULT (with a message), RESHAPE (these param values need a different shape) or FINISH (a reaction ends the analysis at its instant). Nothing may unwind across the boundary.

The Rust side

In Rust a device is a type that implements one trait. No macro is involved: a generic function produces the C thunks.

pub trait Device: Sized + Send + 'static {
    fn prototype() -> &'static Shape;
    fn create(values: &Values<'_>) -> Result<(Self, Arc), Fault>;
    fn eval (&self, k: u32, cx: &mut Eval<'_>)  -> Result<(), Fault>;
    fn react(&self, k: u32, cx: &mut React<'_>) -> Result;      // Flow::Continue | Flow::Finish
}
pub const fn module(name: &'static str) -> Module;       // generic extern "C" thunks; no macro

Eval and React are borrow-checked views over the call's buffers: cx.f64(i) and cx.bytes(i) read input i, cx.set(j, v) writes output j, cx.jac() is the partials, cx.commit(statement, bytes, after) queues a write. A panic becomes a FAULT with its message.

Three devices

These are verbatim from the ABI document, with the partials written by hand so the contract shows. The comment above each one is its shape: reads and writes in order, partials as (write ← read). Generated code computes the partials for you.

A resistor. In MSL it is one line of behavior:

module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 = 1k; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

As a device it is two port pins, one param pin, one term and one function:

// pins:   p, n (PORT continuous);  r (PARAM F64, default 1e3)
// terms:  T0 FLOW {p, n} "i"
// fn 0 "i":  reads [p, n, r]   writes [T0]   partials (0←0) (0←1) (0←2)
fn eval(&self, _k: u32, cx: &mut Eval) -> Result<(), Fault> {
    let (v, r) = (cx.f64(0) - cx.f64(1), cx.f64(2));
    cx.set(0, v / r);
    cx.jac().copy_from_slice(&[1.0 / r, -1.0 / r, -v / (r * r)]);    // the last matters only when seeded
    Ok(())
}

Two resistors of 1 kΩ and 2 kΩ share this shape; the solver seeds each one's param net with its value.

A D flip-flop. The MSL:

module Dff(in clk: Bit, in d: Bit, out q: Bit = false) {}
digital for Dff { on clk.rise() { q <- d; } }

The edge clk.rise() becomes a discrete function that fires a private event pin; the on block becomes a reaction listening to it; q <- d becomes one commit:

// pins: clk, d (PORT BIT);  q (PORT BIT, init LOW);  rise (PRIVATE event)
// fn 0 "rise":  reads [clk.VALUE, clk.PREVIOUS, clk.CHANGED]   writes [rise]
// reaction 0:   on [rise]   reads [d]   writes [S0 {q}]
fn eval(&self, _k: u32, cx: &mut Eval) -> Result<(), Fault> {
    cx.set(0, (cx.bytes(2) == [1] && cx.bytes(0) == HIGH && cx.bytes(1) == LOW) as u8);
    Ok(())
}
fn react(&self, _k: u32, cx: &mut React) -> Result {
    cx.commit(0, cx.bytes(0), 0.0)?;                  // q <- d : applies at (t, n+1)
    Ok(Flow::Continue)
}

An AVR emulator. Not written in MSL at all: a foreign device with its own clock, coupled through the same six entry points. Its whole CPU state is one private pin, so the solver can checkpoint and roll it back like any signal.

// pins:  pad_in[8], pad_out[8] (PORT BIT)     adc0 (PORT continuous)     reset (PORT BIT)
//        firmware (PARAM U8[256], STRUCTURAL)  f_cpu (PARAM F64)
//        cpu (PRIVATE discrete, QUIET; layout: registers, SRAM, timers, its own tick — plain fields)
//        adc_val (PRIVATE F64)                log (PRIVATE discrete, RECORD)
//        irq, wake, sample (PRIVATE event)
//        resolution (GLOBAL RESOLUTION), start (GLOBAL START)
// fn 0 "pad change": reads [pad_in[0..8].CHANGED, reset.CHANGED, adc_val.CHANGED]   writes [irq]
// reaction 0 "run":    on [start, irq, wake]
//                      reads  [cpu, pad_in[0..8], reset, adc_val, resolution, start.CHANGED, TICK, HORIZON]
//                      writes [S0 {cpu}, S1..S8 {pad_out[i], LATER}, S9 {wake, LATER|REPLACE},
//                              S10 {sample, LATER}, S11 {log}]
// reaction 1 "sample": on [sample]   reads [adc0]   writes [S0 {adc_val}]
fn react(&self, k: u32, cx: &mut React) -> Result {
    if k == 1 { cx.commit(0, &real(cx.f64(0)), 0.0)?; return Ok(Flow::Continue); }

    let mut cpu: Cpu = *cx.get::(CPU).unwrap();           // a copy of the state pin; Cpu: Pod
    let (now, horizon, res) = (cx.i64(TICK), cx.i64(HORIZON), cx.f64(RES));
    if cx.bytes(START) == [1] { cpu = Cpu::reset(now); }
    cpu.set_pads(cx.bits(PAD_IN), cx.bytes(RESET));            // pin-change interrupts are raised here
    cpu.adc_result(cx.get::(ADC_VAL));

    // run my own clock through the horizon, or to my first externally visible action
    let stop = cpu.run(&self.flash, self.ticks_per_cycle, horizon);
    let dt = (cpu.tick - now) as f64 * res;
    match stop {
        Stop::PadWrite { pad, level } => cx.commit(1 + pad, &level, dt)?,   // the edge keeps its exact time
        Stop::AdcStart                => cx.commit(10, &[], dt)?,           // fire `sample` then
        Stop::Break { pc }            => cx.commit(11, bytes_of(&Log { pc }), 0.0)?,   // a record
        Stop::Horizon | Stop::Sleep   => {}
    }
    cx.commit(9, &[], cpu.next_wake(now, res))?;               // "advance me to": REPLACE re-arms
    cx.commit(0, bytes_of(&cpu), 0.0)?;                        // the state, committed like any signal
    Ok(Flow::Continue)
}
  • How far it may run. HORIZON is the last tick through which nothing it listens to or reads can change. It runs its own clock up to there, or stops earlier at its first output, because that output may come back to it.
  • "Wake me at." A commit with a delay to its private wake event pin; REPLACE re-arms it.
  • The ADC. A second reaction that reads a continuous pin, so only the samples cost an analog landing.
  • Rollback. None of its own: its state is a pin in the owner's checkpoint. An emulator that cannot expose its state marks run IRREVERSIBLE instead, and pays for it with an analog landing at every instant it fires (in a design with a crossing) and a session the host cannot rewind.

What your MSL becomes

Each MSL construct lands on one ABI piece:

You writeThe device gets
a contribution i(p, n) +> xa term on {p, n}, operator 1 (FLOW)
v(p, n) +> xtwo FLOW terms and a private continuous pin for the branch current
ddt(x), delay(x, td)the term's operator: DDT, DELAY (idt is stdlib over DDT)
noise(psd, name), an AC stimulus, limita held input: NOISE, STIM, LIMIT
now(), freq()an ambient input: TIME, FREQ
cross(e), above(e)a watch (CROSS) that fires a private event pin
bound_step(dt)a watch (CAP)
on clk.rise() { … }a reaction on an event pin, fired by a discrete function for the edge
q <- d, after tda commit, with its delay
a param r: f64; its wherea PARAM pin; a boolean pin that must hold for a write to be accepted
a signal, an internal nodea private pin (discrete, continuous)
temperature(), settlea global pin the session binds
emit!a commit to a pin flagged RECORD

Good to know

Because a function is pure and a device holds no hidden state, a recorded call is a unit test: the same bytes through the compiled device and through the interpreter must give the same answer. In a debug session the solver also checks every Jacobian against finite differences.

What it changes in the spec

The ABI brought a few small changes to the language rules. They are now part of the specification.

  • Delayed commits do not make time points (LATER). A commit whose delay always moves time forward by at least one tick changes nothing at the instant that made it. Only its due time is tested, on its own. The one change you can observe: on settle sees fewer time points.
  • What a time point is. A time at which the analog solver produces an accepted solution and runs the time-point procedure. The simulator may accept solutions at other times; those are not time points, and settle does not fire there.
  • The C interface of an extern module is this ABI. "osdi" names the OSDI adapter; "cosim" is a module whose reaction is irreversible. A "cosim" module stays digital-only for now.
  • Two new core functions, gmin() and source_scale(), in the same family as now() and freq(). They let a model see the solver's continuation variables, as junction models in ngspice do.
  • Smaller ones. Two sentences on bytes (all-zero bytes are the bytes of Logic::X, of None and of every derived default), a set that would change an extern module's structure is refused like a structural param; records travel as quiet commits.

Chapter 19

The solver

The solver is the part of Piperine that turns devices into numbers. It knows physics and time, not the language: it never sees an MSL expression, only devices that speak the ABI of chapter 18. This chapter explains what it does with them, so that you can predict what a simulation will do and read what it reports.

It is also the reference for running it: each analysis with the keys it reads and a command that runs it, and every option of a run with its default, its range and what it changes. Chapter 11 walks through the same ground slowly, with a picture for every idea and one circuit that is run from start to end. Chapter 15 is the reference of the command itself: projects, run tables, output files.

Status

Built. The solver runs every analysis below on devices that use the ABI, with numbers bit-for-bit equal to the solver it replaced, whose results were validated against ngspice. Analog MSL designs run on it through the compiler of chapter 20, and crossings are located inside a step to the tolerances below.

Three parts, one boundary

The solver is three parts. The analog solver owns the continuous unknowns and solves the equations. The digital solver owns the discrete nets and runs instants. The event owner sits above both, owns the clock and the queue of what is due, and decides who runs when. The analog and digital solvers never call each other: everything that crosses between them is an event the owner carries.

flowchart LR
  H["Your host program"] --> O["Event owner
clock, checkpoints, observations"]
  O -- "discrete values an analog
function reads" --> A["Analog solver
Newton, TR-BDF2"]
  A -- "crossings, samples" --> O
  O -- "instants" --> D["Digital solver
microsteps"]
  D -- "values that changed" --> O

Analog code runs at every time step as long as the design has any: continuous behavior is continuous. Digital code runs only at instants where something is due.

The analyses

A run is one top module and one analysis. The analysis is the key analysis of a run table, or the flag --analysis, and each analysis reads keys of its own. A key that the chosen analysis does not read is refused with P0010 before anything runs, so a setting is never silently ignored.

AnalysisanalysisWhat it computesKeys it needsKeys it may take
Operating pointopThe equilibrium: every ddt is zero, digital settled.none
DC sweepdcAn operating point at each value of one param.sweep, from, to, stepplot
ACacThe small-signal response over frequency, linearized at the operating point.from, to, and one of per_decade, pointsplot
TransienttransientThe response over time, with an adaptive step.stopmax_step, print_step, from, plot
Transfer functiontfDC gain, input resistance and output resistance.input, output
Pole-zeropzThe poles and zeros of a transfer function.input, output
SensitivitysensHow the printed signals move with each named param, at the operating point.wrt
Periodic steady statepssThe state that repeats itself after one period, by shooting.periodtstab, max_step, print_step, plot

The solver has three more analyses that a run table does not name yet: noise (the output noise density over frequency, and each source's share), S-parameters (the scattering matrix of an N-port), and sensitivity to many params at once by one adjoint solve. They are built and tested inside the solver, and a run cannot ask for them yet.

Running each analysis

One small circuit serves every command of this chapter: 5 V into 1 kΩ and 1 µF. Save it as filter.msl, anywhere. A lone file needs no project: the flags describe the run.

module Resistor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { r: f64 where r > 0.0; }
analog for Resistor { i(p, n) +> v(p, n) / r; }

module Capacitor(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { c: f64 where c > 0.0; }
analog for Capacitor { i(p, n) +> ddt(c * v(p, n)); }

/// A DC source that is also the small-signal stimulus of an AC analysis.
module Source(p: Electrical, n: Electrical) { dc: f64 = 1.0; }
analog for Source { v(p, n) +> dc + ac_stim(1.0, 0.0); }

/// 5 V into 1 kΩ and 1 µF: a time constant of 1 ms, a corner at 159 Hz.
module Filter() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal vout: Electrical = 0.0;
    let v1 = Source(vin, gnd) { dc: 5.0 };
    let r1 = Resistor(vin, vout) { r: 1k };
    let c1 = Capacitor(vout, gnd) { c: 1u };
}

Operating point. No key but the analysis. Every signal of the top is printed unless --print names some.

piperine run filter.msl --analysis op
run filter: op, top Filter, backend rust

signal       value
Filter.vin   5.000000e0
Filter.vout  5.000000e0

At equilibrium no current flows into the capacitor, so the output sits at the source. The = 0.0 on vout plays no part here: an initial value is where a transient starts, not a constraint on the operating point.

DC sweep. sweep is the path of a param, and the points are from, from + step, and so on while not past to. Each point is an operating point of its own.

piperine run filter.msl --analysis dc --sweep v1.dc --from 0 --to 5 --step 2.5 --print vout
run filter: dc, top Filter, backend rust

Filter.v1.dc  Filter.vout
0.000000e0    0.000000e0
2.500000e0    2.500000e0
5.000000e0    5.000000e0

AC. from and to are frequencies in hertz. The grid is logarithmic with per_decade points per decade, or linear with points points in all; a run gives exactly one of the two. The stimulus is whatever the design marks with ac_stim (chapter 8).

piperine run filter.msl --analysis ac --from 10 --to 1e4 --per-decade 1 --print vout
run filter: ac, top Filter, backend rust

freq        mag(Filter.vout)  phase(Filter.vout)
1.000000e1  9.980319e-1       -3.595274e0
1.000000e2  8.467330e-1       -3.214191e1
1.000000e3  1.571767e-1       -8.095694e1
1.000000e4  1.591348e-2       -8.908819e1

Transient. stop is the end time, in seconds, and the only key it needs. The run starts at time zero from the initial values the design gives, here vout at 0 V. Without print_step the table has one row per accepted step.

piperine run filter.msl --analysis transient --stop 5e-3 --print-step 1e-3 --print vout
run filter: transient, top Filter, backend rust

time         Filter.vout
0.000000e0   0.000000e0
1.000000e-3  3.160611e0
2.000000e-3  4.323395e0
3.000000e-3  4.751116e0
4.000000e-3  4.908450e0
5.000000e-3  4.966326e0

Transfer function and pole-zero. input names a voltage source by its instance and output a node. tf gives three numbers at DC; pz lists the poles and zeros of the same transfer, in rad/s.

piperine run filter.msl --analysis tf --input v1 --output vout
run filter: tf, top Filter, backend rust

quantity          value
gain              1.000000e0
input_impedance   1.000000e20
output_impedance  1.000000e3
piperine run filter.msl --analysis pz --input v1 --output vout
run filter: pz, top Filter, backend rust

kind  real         imag
pole  -1.000000e3  0.000000e0

The one pole is at −1/RC. The input impedance is the number the solver has for an open circuit: at DC the capacitor passes nothing.

Sensitivity. wrt names params, each by its path; the table has the derivative of every printed signal by each.

piperine run filter.msl --analysis sens --wrt v1.dc --wrt r1.r --print vout
run filter: sens, top Filter, backend rust

param         d(Filter.vout)
Filter.v1.dc  1.000000e0
Filter.r1.r   0.000000e0

Periodic steady state. period is the period of the drive. The solver first runs a transient of length tstab, then repeats one period until the state at its end equals the state at its start; the table is one period of that orbit. It needs a circuit with a periodic source, and chapter 15 runs one.

Finding the operating point

The analog solver uses Newton's method: guess, linearize every device at the guess, solve the linear system, repeat. A solution is accepted only when two tests pass at once: no unknown moved more than its tolerance, and the currents into every node balance within tolerance. A large step is damped, and a limiter (limit in MSL) keeps an exponential junction from jumping too far.

When plain Newton fails, the solver makes the problem easier and walks back. First it adds a conductance from every node to ground and lowers it decade by decade (gmin stepping). If that fails, it turns all sources off and ramps them back up (source stepping). These are the same two steps ngspice takes, in the same order.

A mixed design alternates: solve the analog side, run the digital instant on that solution, give the analog side what changed, solve again. After 20 rounds that still change something the solver stops with E0506 and names the nets that kept moving, rather than return a wrong answer. Then settle fires once.

One transient step

Time inside the solver is an integer count of ticks (one femtosecond unless the session says otherwise), so two events at "the same time" really are at the same time. Each step goes like this:

  1. Propose. The step controller proposes how far to go, bounded by the stop time and by any bound_step.
  2. Look ahead. The digital solver runs ahead to that time. It stops at the first instant that touches the analog side, and the step is shortened to land exactly there.
  3. Solve. The analog solver integrates to that time on the values the digital side had just before the instant.
  4. Judge. If Newton failed or the error estimate is too big, everything returns to the checkpoint taken at the start, and the step is retried smaller.
  5. Locate. If a cross expression changed sign inside the step, the step is taken again, shorter, until it ends on the crossing: within etol of zero or within ttol (plus one tick) of the estimated time. The estimate is a secant through the attempts so far.
  6. Accept and react. The solution is accepted. Crossings become events, and the digital instant runs, reading the accepted solution.
  7. Deliver. What the instant changed reaches the analog side. If an analog function reads it, the next step restarts from first order, because a jump has no history to extrapolate from.
flowchart TD
  P["propose t"] --> L["digital looks ahead,
may shorten t"]
  L --> S["analog solves to t
on the values before the instant"]
  S --> J{"converged and
error small?"}
  J -- no --> R["back to checkpoint,
smaller step"] --> P
  J -- yes --> X{"a crossing
inside the step?"}
  X -- "yes, not located" --> C["retake, ending
at the estimate"] --> S
  X -- "no, or located" --> A["accept; crossings fire"]
  A --> I["digital instant at t"]
  I --> D["deliver changes;
restart if needed"] --> P

A rejected step costs only that step: no device keeps memory of its own, so going back is the solver restoring its own copies.

Integration and step size

Every ddt is integrated with TR-BDF2: each step is a trapezoidal stage followed by a second-order backward-difference stage. It is accurate like the trapezoidal rule and does not ring on stiff circuits. You never choose a method; there is only this one.

The error of each step is estimated by comparing the solution with a straight-line prediction through the two stages. A PI controller then grows or shrinks the step from that estimate, smoothly, so it does not oscillate on a stiff edge. A step that lands on a scheduled event or right after a jump is exempt from the error test, because what it measures there is the jump, not an integration error.

The tolerances this section names, and every other number that steers a step, are options of a run. The section "Every option of a run" below lists them with their defaults.

Every option of a run

An option is given in one of three places, and means the same in each: as a key of the run's table in Piperine.toml, as a flag of piperine run, or as a field of the request a host program builds. A flag is the key with - for _ (max_step, --max-step), and it wins over the key. An option that is not given keeps the default of the tables below; a run that gives none is a run with every default. A value outside the range of its option is refused with P0010 before the design is compiled.

[run.step]
analysis = "transient"
stop     = 5e-3
max_step = 10e-6        # the same as --max-step 10e-6
reltol   = 1e-4

Accuracy

These four are accepted by every analysis. A Newton iteration has converged when two tests hold for every unknown at once: the unknown moved by no more than reltol times its magnitude plus an absolute floor, and the equation it belongs to balances within the same bound. The floor is vntol for a node potential and abstol for a branch flow. The same three numbers scale the error a time step may make.

OptionDefaultRangeWhat it sets
reltol1e-3> 0The relative tolerance of every unknown, in Newton and in the step error. One part in a thousand unless you ask for more.
vntol1e-6 (1 µV)> 0The absolute tolerance of a node potential, in volts. A nature's own abstol can tighten it for the nodes of that nature; it never loosens it, and a looser vntol loosens it down to the nature's value.
abstol1e-12 (1 pA)> 0The absolute tolerance of a branch flow, in amperes. A nature's own flow abstol can tighten it for the branches of that nature; it never loosens it, and a looser abstol loosens it down to the nature's value.
trtol7> 0How many times its target the estimated error of a time step may be before the step is rejected. It acts in transient and pss.

Time and steps

OptionRead byDefaultRangeWhat it sets
stoptransientrequired> 0The end time, in seconds. A transient always starts at time zero.
fromtransient00 ≤ from < stopThe first time the result keeps. The solver still integrates from zero; the steps before from are computed and not kept.
max_steptransient, pssnone> 0The longest step, and the first one. Without it a transient starts with a step of stop/1000 and takes none longer than stop/100.
print_steptransient, pssnone> 0The spacing of the table's rows, each value interpolated between the two steps around it. Without it the table has one row per accepted step. It never changes the steps the solver takes.
max_stepstransient, pss2000000≥ 1The budget of step attempts, accepted or rejected. A run that uses it up stops with an error that says where it was.
periodpssrequired> 0The period of the drive, in seconds.
tstabpss0≥ 0The length of the transient that runs before the first period is tried.

The shortest step is fixed at one femtosecond. A step that fails at that length ends the run with E0508, or E0504 when the step follows a digital change (chapter 14). This is what a run that exhausts a small budget prints:

piperine run filter.msl --analysis transient --stop 5e-3 --max-steps 20 --print vout
compiling run `filter` (top Filter) with rustc
error: the transient analysis failed: Transient: at t = 6.02857418308e-4 s
Caused by: Transient: exceeded the 20 step budget at t = 5.528574e-4 s of 5.000000e-3 s (11.06% of the run) with dt = 5.000e-5 s (accepted 20, rejected 0, dt_min floor hits 0, smallest dt seen 5.000e-6 s). The timestep has collapsed: the circuit is either stiff at a switching edge or has a discontinuity the stepper cannot land on. Raise `max_steps` to let it run longer, raise `dt_min` to make the floor fail instead of crawl, or soften the discontinuity.

The numbers in the message are the ones to read. Here every attempt was accepted and the step had grown from 5 µs to 50 µs, which is stop/1000 and stop/100: the run was healthy and the budget was simply too small for it. Many rejections at a step near the floor mean the opposite, a jump the solver cannot land on.

The operating point and the environment

OptionDefaultRangeWhat it sets
nodesetemptya node path and a value eachThe first guess of the named nodes, in volts, for every operating point the run takes: the one an ac, a tf or a transient starts from included. A circuit with one equilibrium ends at it whatever the guess; a circuit with several ends at the one the guess is nearest to. A path that names no node is E0801.
maxrounds20≥ 1How many times an operating point of a mixed design may alternate between the analog solve and the digital reactions before it fails with E0506.
temp27> −273.15The temperature of the run, in degrees Celsius. Models read it in kelvin through temperature(), and vt() follows it.

An initial value is not an option of a run: it belongs to the design. signal vout: Electrical = 0.0; starts a transient with that node at 0 V, as the transient above shows in its first row, and the operating point pays it no attention, as the first listing shows. Use an initial value to say where a transient begins, and nodeset to help an operating point that has more than one answer.

The digital engine

OptionDefaultRangeWhat it sets
resolution1e-15 (1 fs)> 0The length of one tick, in seconds. Every time in a run is a whole number of ticks, so two events closer than a tick are at the same time.
maxmicro1000≥ 1How many microsteps one instant may take before the run fails with E0501.

Sweeps and small-signal keys

KeyRead byRangeWhat it sets
sweepdcthe path of a paramThe param the sweep steps.
from, todcany two valuesThe first value and the bound of the sweep.
stepdcnot 0, and pointing from from toward toThe increment. A negative step sweeps downward.
from, toacfrom > 0, to ≥ fromThe first and the last frequency, in hertz.
per_decadeac> 0A logarithmic grid with this many points per decade.
pointsac> 0A linear grid with this many points in all. A run gives per_decade or points, never both.
inputtf, pzan instance that is a voltage sourceThe source that excites.
outputtf, pzthe path of a nodeThe node that is measured.
wrtsensone or more param pathsThe params to differentiate by.

The remaining keys of a run choose the design and the output, and have nothing to do with the solver: bench, top, params, print, streams, csv, records, plot, all_checks and backend. Chapter 15 documents each with an example.

What is fixed

Some numbers that other simulators expose are not options here. They are listed so that you know their values.

QuantityValueWhere a design can change it
gmin1e-12 SNowhere. It is the smallest conductance a junction model adds, and a model reads it with gmin().
shortest time step1e-15 s (1 fs)Nowhere.
ttol1e-12 s (1 ps)Per crossing: cross(e, 1n). How close in time a located crossing must be.
etolthe run's vntolPer crossing: cross(e, etol: 1m). How close to zero the crossing expression must be.
integration methodTR-BDF2Nowhere. There is one method.
rescue of an operating pointgmin stepping, then source steppingNowhere. Both are tried, in that order, whenever the direct solve fails.

reltol, vntol, abstol, gmin and trtol have ngspice's defaults with ngspice's meanings. Matching ngspice is a design goal: on the same circuit the two simulators are expected to agree to within these tolerances, and the test suite runs MSL circuits side by side with ngspice to hold that (chapter 20). A crossing that falls in the step right after a jump is the one exception to locating: it fires at that step's end.

Choosing among these options is the subject of "What the knobs actually do" in chapter 11, which measures what each one costs and buys on one circuit. The short version: for more accuracy lower reltol; use max_step only when something narrow is being stepped over; and treat print_step as a display setting.

The digital side

One digital instant is a series of microsteps at the same time. In each, the commits due now are applied, the discrete functions that read a changed value run in dependency order, and then the reactions whose event fired run, all reading the same snapshot. Their commits are due at the next microstep, or later if they have an after. The instant ends when nothing more is due now.

  • Only what changed runs. A function whose inputs did not change is not called. A free-running clock costs only what it actually touches.
  • Combinational loops must say so. A loop of functions is accepted only if it passes through a net marked to relax, which is iterated until it stops changing; a net that still moves after its bounded passes stops the run with E0511, naming the loop's drives. Any other loop is refused when the design is built, naming its functions.
  • Only the settled value is seen. Several commits to one net in the same microstep are applied in order and the net is written once. changed() and the previous value compare with the value before the microstep, so x <- 1; x <- 0; on a net that was 0 changes nothing.
  • Reactions see the environment. A reaction or a definition may read temperature(), gmin() and source_scale(); they get the values in force at that instant, the same ones the analog side uses.
  • An instant must go quiet. After 1000 microsteps the solver stops with E0501 and names the nets that keep changing.
  • Running ahead is safe. The digital side runs ahead of the analog side on checkpoints. If an instant turns out to touch the analog side, it is undone and becomes the next landing.

Watching and steering a run

Everything that happens can be observed as it is accepted: each value that changed, each commit, each crossing, each emit! record, and on request each Newton iteration of a function. Observations from a rejected step never reach you.

The host can change a parameter between analyses, schedule a change at a time inside a transient (the solver lands exactly there), or wait for an event and react to it. A change is first checked against the device's own rules; a refused change leaves the circuit as it was.

Chapter 20

From source to solver

The compiler is what connects the two halves of this book: the language of Part 1 and the solver of chapters 18 and 19. It reads your MSL, checks it, flattens the design into an intermediate form and hands the solver one device per instance. This chapter follows a source file down that path, so you know what each stage checks and where an error comes from.

Status

Built for analog circuits, with two backends. Resistors, capacitors, voltage and current sources and a diode with junction limiting run end to end from source, under operating point, DC sweep, AC and transient, through the interpreter and as compiled Rust, and every result is compared with ngspice on both. The Rust backend also runs digital and mixed-signal models from source: type checking and elaboration take definitions, carriers, events, reactions, commits, enums, match and unknown values into the intermediate form, and a full buck converter runs with its loop closed. The interpreter stays analog only. The piperine command line drives all of it: piperine run takes a project or a lone file from source to results in one command (chapter 15).

The stages

flowchart LR
  S[".msl source"] --> P["Parse and resolve
object model"]
  P --> T["Type check"]
  T --> E["Elaborate
one body per variant"]
  E --> I["Intermediate form"]
  I --> R["Interpreter
reference backend"]
  I --> X["Rust backend
rustc, cached library"]
  R --> V["Solver"]
  X --> V
  1. Parse and resolve. The source becomes the object model of chapter 17. Names are resolved; nothing is computed yet.
  2. Type check. Every expression gets a type, every call gets the right number and kind of arguments, and every construct is checked against where it may appear: ddt and limit only inside analog, a where clause only on what it constrains.
  3. Elaborate. Starting from the top module, parameters are folded to numbers, instances are walked, and each distinct combination of a module and its compile-time values becomes one body. Functions stay functions: nothing is inlined in full. The where checks on parameters run here, with the real values.
  4. Intermediate form. The result is a flat table of instances, each pointing at a body, plus the bodies themselves. It is an object model, not a text format, but it prints in a stable form for reading and for tests. Each body knows which reads its contributions depend on, which fixes the shape of the Jacobian before any number is computed.
  5. Run. Today a generic device walks a body at every Newton iteration. It computes values and their derivatives together, with dual numbers, so the Jacobian is exact to the last bit and no derivative is written by hand or derived symbolically.

A source that runs

This divider is a complete design. The top module has no ports; its signals are internal nodes and its instances are the devices.

mod devices;
use devices::*;

module Divider() {
    signal vin: Electrical;
    signal mid: Electrical;
    let v1 = Vsource { dc: 10.0 }(vin, gnd);
    let r1 = Resistor { r: 1k }(vin, mid);
    let r2 = Resistor { r: 3k }(mid, gnd);
}

Its operating point puts mid at 7.5 V, the same number ngspice prints for the same circuit. The diode it can be swapped for shows the analog features the path already carries: a limited junction voltage, the solver's gmin, and a charge term.

analog for Diode {
    let nvt   = n * vt();
    let vcrit = nvt * (nvt / (2.0_f64.sqrt() * is)).ln();
    let vd    = limit(v(a, k), |new, old| pnjlim(new, old, nvt, vcrit), vcrit);
    let id    = is * ((vd / nvt).exp() - 1.0);
    i(a, k) +> id + gmin() * vd + ddt(cj0 * vd);
}

Compared with ngspice

Every circuit the path runs has a twin written for ngspice, and a test runs both and compares every printed number. The tolerance is the same for all of them: 10−3 relative, plus a floor of 1 µV for voltages and 1 pA for currents. The tolerance is never loosened to make a test pass.

CircuitAnalysisWorst difference
Resistive divider, current source into a resistoroperating point0
Diode in series with a resistoroperating point7.5 × 10−7 relative
Diode I–V curveDC sweepinside the floor
Diode under reverse bias to −20 VDC sweep5.0 × 10−20 A
RC low-passAC3 × 10−9 in magnitude, 1.2 × 10−9 rad in phase
RC step responsetransientinside the tolerance

The comparison catches mistakes. Twenty-two deliberate faults were planted in the models and the compiler, such as removing gmin from the diode, and every fault large enough to show above the tolerance turned a test red.

Compiler errors

Errors the language defines keep their codes from chapter 14. Three more belong to the compiler itself:

CodeMeaning
P0001The construct is valid MSL but the compiler cannot run it yet. It names the construct; nothing is silently skipped.
P0003The top module you asked for does not exist.
P0004The compiler built an ill-formed intermediate form. This is a bug in Piperine, not in your source.

Two backends

The intermediate form runs two ways, and they must agree bit for bit.

  • The interpreter walks a body at every call, computing values and derivatives together with dual numbers. It is simple, needs no compiler, and is the reference: every other backend is checked against it. It covers the analog forms only.
  • The Rust backend prints the whole design as one Rust source file, compiles it with the rustc it finds on the machine, and loads the library through the device ABI. It covers analog, digital and mixed forms. A design is compiled once and cached by a hash of everything that went into it, so a second run starts at once and nothing is compiled while a simulation runs. The cache entry is checked against its recorded hash before it is loaded.

Both backends compute the same function, function by function, to the last bit; the ngspice comparisons run on both. A host picks one: on the command line, --backend (rust by default, chapter 15); the test suite picks with PIPERINE_BACKEND (interp or rust) and runs both when it is unset.

Reading what runs

The generated Rust stays on disk next to the library, formatted, and is meant to be read. Every item names the MSL it came from, your names stay as you wrote them, and names the compiler makes up end in _, so they never collide with yours. Here is the resistor's analog block:

// Resistor.analog @ devices.msl:4:1
fn terms_block<R: Real>(p: R, n: R, r: R) -> [R; 1] {
    // contribution to i(p, n), branch b0 @ devices.msl:8:34
    let b0_flow_ = (p - n) / r;
    [b0_flow_]
}

The block is generic over a number type: run with plain floats it gives the current, run with dual numbers it gives the current and its derivatives, which is the Jacobian. Digital code reads the same way: a reaction is a function whose commits go out together when it ends, and an if on a value that may be unknown runs both arms and merges what they commit.

What comes next

One integrated test for every example of this book, run through the same entry point you use, and a single home for end-to-end scenarios compared with ngspice. Chapter 15 shows how you run a design today: the piperine command.

Chapter 21

How Piperine is organized

Piperine is a set of Rust crates, each with one job. Your source goes down through them in a straight line, from text to numbers, and no crate knows about the ones above it: the solver never sees MSL, and the language never sees the solver. This chapter names the crates so that you know where a piece of behavior lives.

flowchart TD
  CLI["piperine-cli
the piperine command"] --> API["piperine-api
projects, runs, results"]
  API --> LANG["piperine-lang
parse, object model, types, elaboration"]
  API --> PROJ["piperine-project
manifest, dependencies, lockfile"]
  LANG --> IR["piperine-ir
the elaborated design"]
  IR --> PLAN["piperine-plan
one plan per body"]
  PLAN --> INTERP["piperine-interp
reference interpreter"]
  PLAN --> RUST["piperine-transpile
generated Rust"]
  INTERP --> ABI["piperine-abi
the device interface"]
  RUST --> ABI
  ABI --> SOLVER["piperine-solver
time, events, analyses"]
  SOLVER --> ANALOG["piperine-analog-solver
Newton, TR-BDF2"]
  SOLVER --> DIGITAL["piperine-digital-solver
nets, microsteps"]

The crates

CrateWhat it does
piperine-cliThe piperine command of chapter 15 and its seven commands. It reads the command line, calls piperine-api and lays out the text; every rule of what a run prints and judges lives in the library.
piperine-apiThe library a Rust program uses to drive Piperine, small on purpose: a Project found from its Piperine.toml, the runs it describes, a Session that compiles one run's top and runs its analysis, and the result as a table of values and the records your models emit. The command line is built on it.
piperine-projectReads Piperine.toml, finds the crates your project depends on and pins them in the lockfile.
piperine-langThe language. It parses your files, builds the object model of chapter 17 with every name resolved, checks types and places, and elaborates the design: parameters folded, instances walked, one body per variant. It also holds the formatter behind piperine fmt, which keeps every comment.
piperine-irThe elaborated design as an object model of its own: the bodies, the flat table of instances, the nets. It knows neither the language nor the solver, and prints in a stable form for reading.
piperine-planTurns each body into the shape the solver expects: its pins, terms, functions and the order of their reads. Both backends use the same plan, so they cannot disagree on it.
piperine-interpThe reference backend. It walks a body at every call and computes values with their derivatives. It needs no compiler, and every other backend is checked against it.
piperine-transpileThe compiled backend. It prints the design as Rust, compiles it once with rustc, keeps the result in a cache and loads it. The generated source stays on disk for you to read.
piperine-abiThe device interface of chapter 18: what a device is, how the solver calls it and what it may read and write. It depends on nothing else, so any device, compiled from MSL or not, plugs in the same way.
piperine-solverOwns time and events, runs the analyses of chapter 19 and alternates between the two sides below.
piperine-analog-solverThe continuous side: Newton iterations, linear algebra, TR-BDF2 integration and the location of crossings. It knows nothing digital.
piperine-digital-solverThe discrete side: nets, the commit queue, instants and microsteps. It knows nothing analog.

One more crate is in the tree and not built yet: a language server that will put the diagnostics of chapter 14 into your editor as you type. Until it is, piperine check gives the same diagnostics on the command line.

Three rules that hold it together

  • Each layer hands the next a finished model. The object model, the elaborated design, the plan and the device are each built once and not changed by the layer that reads them.
  • The device interface is the only door into the solver. Generated Rust, the interpreter and any device you bring yourself meet the solver through the same calls.
  • The two sides of the solver never call each other. Everything that crosses between analog and digital is an event the owner of time carries.
